# Clinical Protocols for Common Emergency Presentations

## How to use this manual

- This manual orients Emergency Medical Officers (EMOs), duty doctors, residents and emergency nursing staff to the common emergencies encountered on shift at this hospital. The goal is **evidence-based standard of care delivered fast**, in a hospital with a large oncology and superspeciality case-mix.
- **Every protocol is laid out in four columns** — *Salient history*, *Salient examination*, *Point-of-care investigations*, and *Basic initial management* — followed by a **RED FLAG** row. Read the red flags first when the patient looks sick.
- Steps marked with an asterisk (*) or the phrase* "Consultant decision" *are not to be initiated independently by the EMO*\*. Inform the Consultant on call, document the time of intimation, and proceed on their instruction.
- The differential-diagnosis boxes that follow each protocol are for **clinical reasoning and recognition**. They are not standing orders.
- Doses are for a **normal-sized adult with normal renal and hepatic function** unless stated. Verify weight-based doses; check renal dose modification for antimicrobials.
- Where this revision differs from Issue 01, the change is deliberate and based on current guidelines (AHA/ACLS 2020–25, ATLS 10th ed., BTF, AHA/ASA stroke, Surviving Sepsis 2021, ESGE/AASLD GI bleed, WHO/NPIC toxicology). A summary of substantive changes is at the end of the manual.
- **Nothing in this manual replaces clinical judgement or the Consultant on call.** When the patient does not fit the protocol, escalate early.

### Departmental general instructions

- The Emergency Department is zoned as **Resuscitation Bay, Red beds, Yellow beds, Green beds and White (day-care) beds**. Zone allocation is decided by the triage nurse and confirmed by the EMO.
- The **first assessment after triage** is done by the EMO, who after history, examination and point-of-care tests allots the patient to the appropriate zone and informs the Consultant on call.
- **Resuscitation-bay and Red-bed patients take priority.** If you are evaluating a Yellow/Green patient and a Red patient arrives, attend the Red patient first and hand over the other.
- Whenever **CODE BLUE** is announced, all doctors not attending a critical patient report to the announced area.
- Every patient entering the ED must be **registered, examined, and documented** — name, age, sex, father’s/husband’s name, contact number, time of arrival, time of first medical contact.
- **Never refer a patient out of the hospital without the Consultant having seen the patient.** If referral is on the Consultant’s advice, obtain their signature on the file with the documented reason and follow the hospital referral SOP.
- **Antibiotics are started only after discussion with the Consultant**, except where this manual mandates immediate empirical therapy (febrile neutropenia, septic shock, meningitis, open fracture) — in which case send cultures first and inform the Consultant simultaneously.
- Any patient with a history of **toxin ingestion, assault, road traffic accident, burns, drowning, hanging, electrocution, sexual assault, or brought dead** is a **Medico-Legal Case (MLC)**. Complete MLC formalities and police intimation without delaying resuscitation.
- Handover between shifts is **written and structured** (identification – problem – vitals trend – investigations pending – treatment given – plan – consultant informed).
- Document your **name, designation, date and time** on every entry. Illegible or unsigned notes are treated as not done.

### Guide to succinct clinical documentation in the ED

### Standard format for the ED case sheet

#### Front sheet \& identification

- Name, age, sex, UHID, contact number, time of arrival, mode of arrival (walk-in / ambulance / referred / in-hospital transfer)
- **Known drug allergies written in CAPITALS on the front sheet**
- Known co-morbid illness — DM, HTN, CAD, CKD, CLD, COPD, malignancy (**site, stage, last chemotherapy date and regimen**), TB, seizure disorder, psychiatric illness
- Current medications, particularly **antiplatelets, anticoagulants, insulin, steroids, immunosuppressants, chemotherapy**
- MLC number where applicable

#### Primary survey (with intervention at each step)

- **A – Airway:** patent / threatened / obstructed → intervention done
- **B – Breathing:** RR, SpO₂ (and FiO₂ / device), bilateral air entry, added sounds
- **C – Circulation:** pulse rate and rhythm, BP, capillary refill, peripheral perfusion, shock index
- **D – Disability:** GCS E\_V\_M\_, pupil size and reaction, focal deficit, plantars, RBS
- **E – Exposure:** temperature, head-to-toe findings, rash, injuries, log roll findings
- Chief complaint **with duration**, focused history, relevant negatives, focused systemic examination

#### Investigations, consults \& treatment

- Write each investigation **with the time it was sent**
- Record salient point-of-care findings — **ECG interpretation, ABG values, RBS, POCUS findings** — not just “sent”
- **Consultation:** name of consultant, time informed, time seen, advice received
- **Treatment:** number each drug; write name, formulation, dose, route, time given
- Record procedures — NG tube, Foley’s, catheter size, cannula gauge, intubation (tube size, grade of view, confirmation)
- Blood product arrangement, consent taken, counselling done and by whom
- Disposition — zone shifted to, admitted under whom, discharged with advice, referred (with reason), LAMA (with signature)

## Triage

Triage determines the **sequence** in which patients are treated by severity, not by order of arrival. When the department is crowded or waiting times lengthen, **periodic re-triage of waiting patients is mandatory** — a patient triaged Green thirty minutes ago may now be Red.

#### RESUSCITATION BAY — the dying patient

**Physiology**

- Airway obstructed or unprotected; pooling secretions, stridor, angioedema of face/tongue
- Apnoea, agonal or gasping respiration, RR \>40 or \<10, SpO₂ \<90% on oxygen
- Pulseless, or shock requiring immediate haemodynamic intervention
- SBP \<90 with hypoperfusion, or HR \>150 / \<40
- GCS ≤8, unresponsive, ongoing seizure
- Intubated pre-hospital

**Examples**

- Cardiac / respiratory arrest
- Critical trauma; trauma needing fluid resuscitation
- Anaphylactic shock
- Drug overdose with RR \<6
- Hypoglycaemia with altered sensorium
- Head injury, intubated, unequal pupils

#### RED BED — at risk of deterioration

**Threat to life, limb or organ within hours**

- Chest pain \<24 h, stable — suspected ACS
- Suspected stroke within window
- Shock index (HR÷SBP) \>0.9
- Any evaluation suggestive of **sepsis**
- Acute alteration in level of consciousness; new confusion in the elderly; post-ictal
- Severe pain (score \>7): flank, oncological, severe generalised abdominal pain
- Acute urinary retention; testicular torsion; acute limb ischaemia \<48 h
- GI bleed — upper or lower
- Hypertensive emergency; acute pulmonary oedema
- **Fever in a patient with chemotherapy in last 14 days, aplastic anaemia or acute leukaemia**
- Suspected ectopic pregnancy; third-trimester pain or bleeding PV; labour
- Toxin ingestion, bites and stings
- Burns — partial/full thickness, inhalational injury, chemical exposure
- Suicidal, homicidal, violent or intoxicated patient
- Outside report of K⁺ \>5.5 mEq/L
- Sudden severe headache; first seizure
- Ocular chemical splash, trauma, sudden vision loss
- Missed dialysis with breathlessness or oliguria
- Danger-zone vitals: HR \>100, RR \>20, SpO₂ \<92%

#### YELLOW BED — stable, multiple resources

**Stable primary survey** plus **risk of early deterioration**

- Patent airway, RR 10–24, SpO₂ \>95%, SBP \>90 without extremes of heart rate, GCS \>13
- **Vulnerable groups:** chronic liver disease, chronic kidney disease, uncontrolled diabetes, immunosuppression, post-transplant, elderly, paediatric, pregnant
- Persistent vomiting or decreased urine output
- Requires **more than one** resource — e.g. labs plus imaging plus specialist consult
- Likely to require admission

#### GREEN BED — stable, single resource

- Stable primary survey with **no** risk factors for deterioration
- Requires **one** resource — a single investigation or a single consult
- Minor injury, simple wound, uncomplicated fever or pain
- Likely OPD-based care or short observation

#### WHITE BED — day care

- IV / IM / SC injections and IV infusions
- Catheter change
- Minor dressing
- Suture removal
- Enema
- ECG, RBS and other single point-of-care tests

**Priority Red regardless of the above:** any patient the triage nurse or EMO is worried about, any patient whose family reports rapid deterioration, and any patient who “looks unwell” without an obvious abnormal number. Clinical gestalt overrides the criteria table — never downgrade it.

### Special alerts — activate on triage, before the workup is complete

#### STEMI alert

- Chest pain / anginal equivalent → **ECG within 10 minutes of arrival**
- ECG shown to Consultant Cardiology / Physician immediately
- Target: door-to-needle ≤30 min (fibrinolysis) or door-to-balloon ≤90 min (primary PCI)

#### Stroke alert

- FAST positive or sudden focal deficit
- **Check RBS first**, then move directly to NCCT head
- Target: NCCT and neurology review within 25 min; needle within 60 min of arrival

#### Sepsis alert

- Suspected infection **plus** any of: SBP \<100, RR ≥22, altered sensorium, lactate \>2
- Cultures → antibiotics within 1 hour, fluid resuscitation started
- Applies with particular urgency to the **neutropenic oncology patient**

#### Trauma alert

- Dangerous mechanism, or abnormal physiology, or anatomical injury
- Simultaneous intimation to Emergency, Surgery, Orthopaedics, Neurosurgery, Anaesthesia and Blood Bank
- Prepare massive transfusion protocol

## Clinical Protocols for Common Presentations in the Emergency Department

All patients are identified and basic information recorded — name, age, date of birth, sex, father’s/husband’s name, contact number — by the emergency doctor or nursing staff **at the same time as, not before, resuscitation**.

# 1.0 Trauma

Trauma care at this hospital is a **team activity**. The EMO runs the primary survey while nursing staff obtain access and monitor; Orthopaedics, General Surgery, Neurosurgery and Anaesthesia are informed **simultaneously**, not sequentially. Do not wait for one consultant to arrive before calling the next.

## 1.1 Road Traffic Accident / Blunt Polytrauma

Replaces the sequential “call orthopaedics, wait, call again” approach of Issue 01. **Priority is physiology, not speciality**: control catastrophic haemorrhage and the airway first; the fracture can wait, the bleed cannot.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Time of injury** and time elapsed to arrival

**Mechanism (predicts injury pattern)**

- Two-wheeler / four-wheeler / pedestrian / heavy vehicle; **helmet or seat-belt worn?**
- Speed, ejection from vehicle, entrapment, death of another occupant, rollover
- Fall from height — height, surface landed on, part striking first
- Assault — weapon, number of blows, site

**Symptoms since injury**

- Loss of consciousness, amnesia for the event, vomiting, seizure, ENT or oral bleeding
- Did the patient walk after the injury? (suggests lower likelihood of major spinal / lower limb injury)
- Chest pain, breathlessness, abdominal pain, inability to pass urine, blood at the meatus
- Weakness, numbness or tingling in any limb
- Estimated blood loss at scene; pre-hospital care given (collar, splint, fluids, tourniquet time)

**AMPLE**

- **A** – Allergies
- **M** – Medications, **especially antiplatelets, warfarin, NOACs, insulin, steroids**
- **P** – Past illness and **P**regnancy
- **L** – Last meal (timing of anaesthesia)
- **E** – Events and Environment of the injury
- Tetanus immunisation status; alcohol or substance intake

### Salient clinical examination

**Primary survey — done, not just written**

- **A** with manual in-line cervical stabilisation: voice, secretions, blood, loose teeth, facial fractures, expanding neck haematoma, tracheal position
- **B**: RR, SpO₂, chest wall symmetry, flail segment, surgical emphysema, air entry, percussion note, open sucking wound
- **C**: pulse volume, BP, capillary refill, **shock index (HR÷SBP)**, cold peripheries; external haemorrhage; scalp laceration bleeding; **pelvic and long-bone instability**
- **D**: GCS (E, V, M separately), pupils size and reaction, gross limb movement, RBS
- **E**: fully expose, then **cover and warm**; temperature

**Secondary survey (only after A–E are stable)**

- Head to toe including scalp, behind the ears (Battle’s sign), periorbital ecchymosis, ENT bleed, CSF rhinorrhoea/otorrhoea
- Chest, abdomen (seat-belt sign, distension, guarding), perineum, **blood at urethral meatus**
- **Log roll with four people** — spinal tenderness, step, gap, open wound; per-rectal examination when indicated
- All four limbs: deformity, open wounds, distal pulses, capillary refill, sensation, **compartment tension**

**Do not spring the pelvis.** A single gentle assessment at most; repeated examination dislodges clot.

### Point-of-care investigations

- **eFAST at the bedside** — pericardial, right and left upper quadrant, pelvis, plus bilateral lung sliding for pneumothorax
- **ABG with lactate and base deficit** — the best early marker of occult hypoperfusion
- CBC, blood group and **cross-match (keep 4 units ready in unstable patients)**
- RFT, electrolytes, LFT, PT/INR, aPTT, RBS, viral markers
- ECG in blunt chest trauma, elderly, or if the mechanism is unexplained (syncope causing the accident)
- Urine pregnancy test in all women of reproductive age

**Imaging**

- Trauma series in the resuscitation bay: **portable chest X-ray AP and pelvis AP**
- **NCCT head and cervical spine** — see Canadian CT Head Rule box below
- *CECT chest, abdomen and pelvis (whole-body CT) only in the haemodynamically stable or stabilised patient*

**Never send an unstable patient to the CT room.** The CT scanner is not a resuscitation area.

### Basic initial management

*Consultant Emergency / Surgery / Orthopaedics / Neurosurgery informed simultaneously. Steps marked*  are Consultant decisions.\*\*\*

**Catastrophic haemorrhage and airway**

- Direct pressure, pressure dressing, elevation; **arterial tourniquet** for uncontrollable limb bleed — note the time applied
- Cervical collar and spinal precautions from first contact
- High-flow oxygen 15 L/min via non-rebreather; suction
- *Definitive airway if GCS ≤8, airway soiling, severe facial trauma, or failure to oxygenate — rapid sequence intubation with in-line stabilisation*

**Circulation**

- Two **wide-bore 16G or 14G** cannulae; if failed, intraosseous access
- Warmed crystalloid — **limit to 1 litre**, then move to blood products; avoid aggressive crystalloid
- *Activate Massive Transfusion Protocol for ongoing bleeding: PRBC : FFP : platelets in 1 : 1 : 1 ratio*
- **Inj. Tranexamic acid 1 g IV over 10 minutes** if within 3 hours of injury, followed by 1 g infusion over 8 hours (**do not give after 3 hours**)
- **Pelvic binder** at the level of the greater trochanters if pelvic fracture suspected
- Splint all long-bone fractures; realign and re-check distal pulses

**Adjuncts**

- Foley’s catheter — **only after urethral injury has been excluded** (see 1.3)
- Nasogastric tube for gastric decompression (**orogastric if base-of-skull fracture suspected**)
- Analgesia: **Inj. Fentanyl 1 mcg/kg IV** titrated, or Inj. Paracetamol 1 g IV. **Avoid NSAIDs** in bleeding, head injury and suspected renal injury
- Tetanus: **Inj. Td 0.5 mL IM**; add **Human Tetanus Immunoglobulin 250 IU IM** at a different site for contaminated wounds, wounds \>6 hours old, or incomplete immunisation
- Open fracture: **Inj. Cefazolin 2 g IV** (or Amoxicillin–clavulanate 1.2 g IV) within 1 hour; splint, cover with saline-soaked sterile gauze, **photograph once for records and do not repeatedly uncover**
- **Prevent hypothermia** — warmed fluids, blankets, raise room temperature. Hypothermia, acidosis and coagulopathy form the lethal triad

**Administrative**

- Complete **MLC formalities and police intimation** without delaying resuscitation
- Counsel family early and document; obtain consent for procedures and transfusion

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- SBP \<90 mmHg, or shock index \>1, or transient response to fluid bolus
- GCS ≤13, deteriorating GCS by ≥2 points, unequal or non-reactive pupils
- RR \>30 or \<10, SpO₂ \<92%, unilateral absent air entry, tracheal deviation, distended neck veins
- Penetrating injury to head, neck, torso or proximal limb
- Flail chest, surgical emphysema, seat-belt sign across the abdomen
- Pelvic instability, open long-bone fracture, amputation, mangled extremity
- **Patient on anticoagulants or antiplatelets** — low threshold for imaging and reversal
- Age \>65 or pregnancy — normal vitals may mask major blood loss

### Priorities in polytrauma — the order that actually saves life

#### Immediately life-threatening (find in the primary survey)

- **Airway obstruction** → open, suction, definitive airway
- **Tension pneumothorax** → clinical diagnosis; **immediate needle decompression** (16G cannula, 5th ICS anterior axillary line) followed by intercostal drain
- **Open pneumothorax** → three-sided occlusive dressing, then ICD
- **Massive haemothorax** (\>1500 mL initial drainage or \>200 mL/h) → ICD and thoracotomy decision
- **Flail chest with pulmonary contusion** → oxygen, analgesia, consider ventilation
- **Cardiac tamponade** → POCUS; pericardiocentesis / surgery
- **Massive external or intra-abdominal haemorrhage** → pressure, binder, blood, surgery

*This is the correct sequence. The Issue 01 statement that priority is “head → chest → orthopaedic” is superseded: an untreated tension pneumothorax or exsanguinating pelvic bleed kills before an extradural haematoma does.*

#### Potentially life-threatening (find in the secondary survey)

- Tracheobronchial injury, pulmonary contusion
- Blunt cardiac injury, traumatic aortic injury
- Traumatic diaphragmatic rupture
- Oesophageal perforation
- Solid organ injury — liver, spleen, kidney
- Hollow viscus perforation (delayed presentation, rising lactate, peritonism)
- Retroperitoneal and pelvic haemorrhage
- Spinal cord injury — **neurogenic shock: hypotension with bradycardia and warm peripheries**
- Compartment syndrome — **pain out of proportion, pain on passive stretch**; pulses are present until late

#### Which consultant, and when

- **Anaesthesia / Critical Care** — the moment the airway is threatened or the patient is shocked
- **General Surgery** — positive FAST, abdominal tenderness, penetrating torso injury, evisceration
- **Neurosurgery** — GCS ≤13, focal deficit, abnormal NCCT, open or depressed skull fracture
- **Orthopaedics** — all fractures and dislocations; open fracture is a **surgical emergency**, not a dressing
- **Urology** — blood at meatus, gross haematuria, inability to void
- **Blood Bank** — at the same time as the first consultant call, not after
- **CTVS / Cardiology** — widened mediastinum, suspected aortic or cardiac injury

### Decision rules for imaging in trauma

#### Canadian CT Head Rule — CT head indicated if any

**High risk (predicts neurosurgical intervention)**

- GCS \<15 at 2 hours after injury
- Suspected open or depressed skull fracture
- Any sign of **base-of-skull fracture** — haemotympanum, raccoon eyes, Battle’s sign, CSF otorrhoea/rhinorrhoea
- ≥ 2 episodes of vomiting
- Age ≥65 years

**Medium risk (predicts injury on CT)**

- Retrograde amnesia \>30 minutes
- Dangerous mechanism — pedestrian struck, ejection, fall \>3 feet or 5 stairs

*Rule does not apply to: anticoagulated patients, seizure after injury, focal deficit, or GCS \<13 — all of these get a CT regardless.*

#### Cervical spine — NEXUS criteria (all five must be absent to clear clinically)

- No posterior midline cervical tenderness
- No focal neurological deficit
- Normal level of alertness
- No evidence of intoxication
- No clinically apparent distracting painful injury

*If any criterion is present, or the patient cannot be assessed, keep the collar on and image.*

*A normal X-ray does not clear the cervical spine in a patient with pain or an altered sensorium — CT is the imaging of choice.*

#### Chest and abdomen

- Chest X-ray in **all** significant blunt trauma
- Pelvis X-ray in all patients with abnormal physiology or who cannot be examined
- **FAST is a rule-in test, not a rule-out test** — a negative FAST in a shocked patient does not exclude abdominal bleeding
- Stable with concerning mechanism or examination → **CECT abdomen and pelvis**
- Unstable with positive FAST → **theatre, not CT**
- Repeat examination and FAST if the patient changes

## 1.2 Head Injury

The brain is injured twice — once by the impact, and again by **hypoxia, hypotension, hypercapnia, hyperthermia, hypoglycaemia and seizures**. The whole of ED management is the prevention of that second injury.

### Salient history to be taken

- Time and mechanism of injury; **helmet worn?**
- Loss of consciousness — present, duration, witnessed?
- **Lucid interval** (initially conscious, then deteriorating) — classic for extradural haematoma
- Amnesia — retrograde and anterograde, duration
- Vomiting — number of episodes, projectile?
- Seizure after the injury
- Headache — severity and progression; visual disturbance; ENT or nasal bleeding, clear fluid discharge
- Weakness, sensory loss, unsteadiness
- **Anticoagulant or antiplatelet use** — the single most important history item in the elderly minor head injury
- Alcohol or drug intake (confounds GCS assessment — never attribute a low GCS to alcohol without imaging)
- Co-morbidity: coagulopathy, chronic liver disease, previous stroke or craniotomy, ventriculoperitoneal shunt
- For children: mechanism inconsistent with injury, delayed presentation → **consider non-accidental injury**

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey with **cervical spine immobilisation** — assume a C-spine injury in every significant head injury
- **GCS documented as E\_V\_M\_, and repeated** — the trend matters more than the number
- **Pupils**: size, symmetry, reaction. Unilateral fixed dilated pupil = uncal herniation until proved otherwise
- Focal deficit: limb power, tone, plantars, facial asymmetry
- Signs of base-of-skull fracture: haemotympanum, periorbital or mastoid ecchymosis, CSF rhinorrhoea/otorrhoea
- Scalp: lacerations (can bleed enough to shock a child), boggy swelling, depressed segment, open wound with brain matter
- Fundus if feasible; **Cushing response — hypertension, bradycardia, irregular respiration = raised ICP**
- RBS in every patient with a reduced GCS

**Severity classification**

- **Mild: GCS 14–15  •  Moderate: GCS 9–13  •  Severe: GCS ≤8**

### Point-of-care investigations

- RBS, ABG (target PaCO₂ 35–40 mmHg, PaO₂ \>100 mmHg)
- CBC, RFT, electrolytes (sodium), LFT, **PT/INR and aPTT**, blood group and cross-match
- **NCCT head** — as per Canadian CT Head Rule; immediately if GCS ≤13, deteriorating GCS, focal deficit, seizure, anticoagulated, or suspected open/depressed fracture
- **NCCT cervical spine** with the head CT in all moderate and severe injuries
- Serum alcohol / toxicology only if it will change management — **never as a substitute for imaging**
- ECG in the elderly or where a medical event may have preceded the fall

*Repeat NCCT at 6–24 hours, or immediately, if GCS falls by ≥2 points, pupil changes, new deficit, or intractable vomiting/headache.*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Neurosurgery informed for every GCS ≤13, every abnormal CT, and every anticoagulated head injury.***

**Prevent secondary brain injury — the targets**

- **SpO₂ ≥94% (PaO₂ \>60–100 mmHg)** — give oxygen
- **SBP ≥110 mmHg** (≥100 mmHg if aged 50–69). *This supersedes the Issue 01 target of \>100 mmHg for all ages.* A single episode of hypotension doubles mortality
- Use **isotonic crystalloid (0.9% NaCl)**. **Never use hypotonic fluids or plain dextrose** — they worsen cerebral oedema
- **Normocapnia, PaCO₂ 35–40 mmHg.** Do not routinely hyperventilate
- RBS 140–180 mg/dL; treat hypoglycaemia immediately
- Temperature \<37.5°C; treat fever actively

**Positioning and general care**

- Head end elevated **30°** with the neck neutral; avoid tight collar ties and tape across the neck (obstruct venous drainage)
- **Keep NPO** — aspiration risk
- Analgesia and antiemetic; treat pain and agitation (both raise ICP)

**Specific measures (Consultant decision)**

- *Signs of herniation — blown pupil, extensor posturing, Cushing response:*
  - **Inj. Mannitol 20% — 0.25–1 g/kg IV over 15–20 min** (about 100–250 mL); ensure euvolaemia and SBP ≥110; monitor serum sodium and osmolality **OR**
  - **3% hypertonic saline 3–5 mL/kg IV** over 10–20 min (preferred if hypotensive)
  - **Brief hyperventilation to PaCO₂ 30–35 mmHg only as a bridge to theatre** — never prolonged
- *Seizure prophylaxis in severe TBI, depressed fracture, penetrating injury, contusion or acute haematoma: Inj. Levetiracetam 20 mg/kg IV loading, then 500–1000 mg BD for 7 days (or phenytoin)*
- *Reverse anticoagulation urgently: warfarin → Inj. Vitamin K 10 mg IV + PCC (or FFP 15 mL/kg); NOAC → specific reversal agent if available, consider PCC; antiplatelets → platelet transfusion only on neurosurgical advice*
- *Intubate for GCS ≤8, loss of airway reflexes, or agitation preventing imaging — RSI with induction agent and neuromuscular blocker, avoid ketamine only if ICP is a concern; do not intubate awake*
- **Steroids are contraindicated in traumatic brain injury** (CRASH trial — increased mortality)

**Disposition**

- GCS 15, normal CT, no risk factors, reliable attendant → discharge with **written head injury advice** and instructions to return
- Any abnormal CT, GCS \<15, anticoagulated, alcohol-intoxicated, or unreliable social support → **admit for observation with hourly neurological charting**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- GCS ≤13, or fall of ≥2 points at any time
- Unequal, fixed or dilated pupil; extensor posturing
- Cushing response — rising BP with falling heart rate
- Post-traumatic seizure; repeated vomiting; severe or worsening headache
- Open or depressed skull fracture; any sign of base-of-skull fracture; penetrating injury
- Focal neurological deficit
- Patient on warfarin, NOAC or dual antiplatelets — **CT even if GCS 15 and asymptomatic**
- SBP \<110 mmHg or SpO₂ \<94% in a head-injured patient — treat as an emergency in itself

## 1.3 Trauma Patient with Urethral Bleeding / Suspected Urethral Injury

The single most important rule: **blood at the urethral meatus means no catheter until the urethra has been imaged.** A blind or forced catheterisation converts a partial urethral tear into a complete disruption and a lifetime of stricture.

### Salient history to be taken

- Mechanism — pelvic crush, straddle injury (bicycle bar, fall astride), penetrating injury, high-velocity RTA
- Ability to pass urine since the injury; **quality of the stream**; last time urine was passed
- Urethral bleeding — spontaneous or only on attempted micturition
- Suprapubic pain, fullness or urge to void without output
- Previous urethral surgery, stricture, TURP, prior catheterisation difficulty
- Attempted catheterisation elsewhere — **how many attempts, what size, by whom** (this changes prognosis)
- Bleeding tendency, anticoagulant use
- Associated: perineal, scrotal or vaginal bleeding; in women, vaginal bleeding suggests associated vaginal laceration with pelvic fracture

### Salient clinical examination

- Primary survey first — urethral injury is almost always accompanied by **pelvic fracture, which is a bleeding emergency**
- Vitals with assessment for haemorrhagic shock

**Classic triad of urethral injury**

- **Blood at the external urethral meatus**
- **Inability to pass urine with a palpable, distended bladder**
- **High-riding or impalpable prostate on per-rectal examination**
- Perineal, scrotal or penile **butterfly** ecchymosis or swelling
- Pelvic instability, pubic symphysis tenderness
- Per-rectal examination — also assess for rectal tear and sphincter tone (open pelvic fracture)
- In females: careful perineal and vaginal examination for laceration

*Document every finding contemporaneously — this is an MLC and a medicolegally contested area.*

### Point-of-care investigations

- CBC, blood group and cross-match, coagulation profile, RFT, electrolytes
- ABG with lactate if shocked
- Urinalysis on any urine obtained (do not obtain by forced catheterisation)

**Imaging**

- **Retrograde urethrogram (RUG)** — the diagnostic investigation of choice; identifies site and whether the tear is partial or complete
- **Pelvis X-ray AP**; CT pelvis if fracture confirmed or suspected
- **CT cystogram** if bladder injury suspected (gross haematuria with pelvic fracture)
- FAST / CECT abdomen as part of the polytrauma workup

*Imaging is arranged after the patient is haemodynamically stable, and never at the expense of haemorrhage control.*

### Basic initial management

***Notify Consultant Urology and Consultant Surgery immediately. No further urethral instrumentation until they have reviewed.***

**What not to do**

- **Do not force a catheter. Do not make repeated attempts. Do not use a smaller catheter or a stylet to “get past it”.**
- *The Issue 01 sequence of escalating to an infant feeding tube when the catheter will not pass is withdrawn — it is unsafe in suspected urethral injury.*

**What to do**

- Resuscitate: two wide-bore cannulae, warmed crystalloid, blood products as indicated
- **Pelvic binder** at the greater trochanters if pelvic fracture is suspected
- *A single gentle attempt at per-urethral catheterisation with a well-lubricated 16 Fr Foley’s may be permitted only on the Consultant’s explicit instruction and only when the classic triad is absent. If there is any resistance, stop.*
- *If the bladder is distended and catheterisation is contraindicated or has failed: suprapubic catheterisation by the Urologist, ideally ultrasound-guided*
- Analgesia: Inj. Paracetamol 1 g IV or Inj. Fentanyl 1 mcg/kg IV
- Tetanus prophylaxis as per wound status
- Antibiotic prophylaxis on Consultant advice if an open injury or instrumentation has occurred
- Monitor **urine output hourly** once drainage is established; watch for post-obstructive diuresis
- Strict input–output charting; serial haemoglobin if bleeding

**Documentation and disposition**

- MLC formalities, police intimation, photographic documentation of external injuries
- Counsel the patient and family about the possibility of stricture, incontinence and erectile dysfunction — and document that counselling
- Admit under Urology / Orthopaedics as per the dominant injury

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Haemodynamic instability — pelvic fracture bleeding, not the urethra, is what kills
- Complete inability to void with a palpable bladder
- High-riding prostate or impalpable prostate on PR
- Open pelvic fracture, or blood on the examining finger on PR (rectal injury)
- Perineal butterfly haematoma — indicates extravasation below the urogenital diaphragm
- Vaginal bleeding in a female with pelvic fracture — assume an open fracture until excluded

# 2.0 Chest Pain, Breathlessness and Syncope

Two rules govern this section. **An ECG is obtained within 10 minutes of arrival** for every adult with chest pain, epigastric pain, unexplained breathlessness, syncope, or sweating — including diabetics and the elderly with vague symptoms. And **breathlessness is a syndrome, not a diagnosis**: the reflex combination of a bronchodilator, a steroid and a diuretic given to every dyspnoeic patient will harm as many as it helps.

## 2.1 Chest Pain

Six diagnoses kill within hours: **acute coronary syndrome, aortic dissection, pulmonary embolism, tension pneumothorax, cardiac tamponade and oesophageal rupture**. The initial assessment is designed to find or exclude these before considering anything else.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Site** — central, retrosternal, left/right, localised (pointing with one finger makes ACS unlikely)
- **Onset** — sudden and maximal at onset (dissection, PE, pneumothorax) versus crescendo (ACS)
- **Character** — heaviness, pressure, band-like, burning, tearing, sharp, pleuritic
- **Radiation** — jaw, neck, either arm, epigastrium, **interscapular region or back (dissection)**
- **Duration** and number of episodes; pain at rest versus on exertion
- **Aggravating** — exertion, deep breathing, lying flat, swallowing, movement, post-prandial
- **Relieving** — rest, sublingual nitrate, sitting forward (pericarditis), antacid

**Associated symptoms**

- Sweating, nausea, vomiting, palpitation, presyncope or syncope, breathlessness, *ghabrahat*
- Fever, cough, expectoration, haemoptysis
- Limb weakness or new neurological deficit (**chest pain plus stroke = dissection**)
- Unilateral leg swelling or pain, recent surgery, immobilisation, long travel, malignancy, oral contraceptives (**PE risk**)
- Severe retching or vomiting before the pain (**Boerhaave**)

**Background**

- **Co-morbidity:** diabetes, hypertension, dyslipidaemia, CAD with previous MI/PCI/CABG, CKD, malignancy, connective tissue disease (Marfan)
- **Family history** of premature coronary disease or sudden death
- Smoking, tobacco chewing, alcohol, cocaine or amphetamine use
- **Medications** — antiplatelets, anticoagulants, sildenafil/tadalafil in the last 24–48 hours, chemotherapy (anthracyclines, 5-FU, trastuzumab, VEGF inhibitors)
- Treatment already given at a referring facility

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; patient on a monitored bed
- **Blood pressure in both arms** — a difference \>20 mmHg raises suspicion of dissection
- Pulse: rate, rhythm, volume, **radio-radial and radio-femoral delay**, all peripheral pulses
- JVP, pedal oedema, hepatojugular reflux
- Chest: air entry, unilateral reduction, added sounds (wheeze, crepitations), percussion note, **subcutaneous emphysema**
- Cardiac: apex, heart sounds, **new murmur (papillary muscle rupture, aortic regurgitation of dissection)**, **pericardial rub**, muffled sounds
- Focused neurological screen — limb power, speech, pupils
- Legs — unilateral swelling, calf tenderness, Homan’s sign
- Chest wall — localised tenderness, herpetic rash, costochondral tenderness (a diagnosis of exclusion only)
- Abdomen — epigastric tenderness, guarding

### Point-of-care investigations

- **ECG within 10 minutes of arrival** — repeat at 15–30 minutes if the first is non-diagnostic and pain continues
  - Add **right-sided leads V3R–V4R** for every inferior wall STEMI and **posterior leads V7–V9** if ST depression in V1–V3
- **RBS** and **ABG with lactate** if unstable
- CBC, RFT, electrolytes, LFT, PT/INR
- **Troponin I** at presentation (and repeated at 3 hours where the initial value is normal and suspicion persists)
- **Point-of-care ultrasound:** regional wall motion abnormality, pericardial effusion, RV dilatation, lung sliding, B-lines, IVC, abdominal aorta
- Chest X-ray — **widened mediastinum, pneumothorax, pneumomediastinum, consolidation, pulmonary oedema**
- *D-dimer — only when pre-test probability is low/intermediate (Wells, PERC). A positive D-dimer in a high-probability patient wastes time; go straight to CTPA*
- *2D echocardiography — Cardiology*
- *CT aortogram if dissection suspected; CT pulmonary angiogram if PE suspected*

*Never send a haemodynamically unstable patient out of the ED for imaging.*

### Basic initial management

*Consultant Cardiology / Physician informed with the ECG in hand. Steps marked*  are Consultant decisions.\*\*\*

**Universal first steps**

- Monitored bed, continuous ECG, SpO₂ and non-invasive BP
- **Oxygen only if SpO₂ \<94%** — routine oxygen in a normoxaemic ACS patient is harmful
- IV access; blood samples drawn at cannulation
- Analgesia and anxiolysis as needed

**If ACS is suspected — loading doses**

- **Tab. Aspirin 300 mg** (non-enteric coated, chewed) PO stat
- **Tab. Clopidogrel 300 mg** PO stat (**600 mg if going for primary PCI**; 75 mg only if aged \>75 years) *or Ticagrelor 180 mg on Consultant advice*
- **Tab. Atorvastatin 80 mg** PO stat
- **Tab. Sorbitrate (isosorbide dinitrate) 5 mg or GTN 0.4 mg sublingual**, may repeat every 5 minutes up to 3 doses

**Contraindications to nitrates:** inferior wall or RV infarction, SBP \<90 mmHg or a fall of \>30 mmHg from baseline, marked bradycardia or tachycardia, severe aortic stenosis or HOCM, sildenafil in last 24 h / tadalafil in last 48 h

**Withhold antiplatelets** if there is massive GI bleed, suspected haemorrhagic stroke, suspected aortic dissection, or known aspirin allergy

**Reperfusion — the clock starts at arrival**

- *STEMI: primary PCI if achievable within 120 minutes of first medical contact. Otherwise fibrinolysis within 30 minutes of arrival — complete the checklist in Section 10*
- *Fibrinolytic: Inj. Tenecteplase weight-adjusted IV bolus over 5–10 seconds (30–50 mg by weight band), with Inj. Enoxaparin 30 mg IV bolus followed by 1 mg/kg SC BD*
- *NSTEMI / unstable angina: anticoagulation with Inj. Enoxaparin 1 mg/kg SC BD (0.75 mg/kg BD if \>75 y; 1 mg/kg OD if CrCl \<30) and risk stratification (GRACE/TIMI) for the timing of angiography*

**If dissection, PE, tamponade or pneumothorax is suspected**

- *Aortic dissection: do not give antiplatelets or anticoagulants. Target SBP 100–120 and HR \<60 with Inj. Labetalol 10–20 mg IV boluses or esmolol infusion, adding a vasodilator only after rate control. Urgent CT aortogram and CTVS referral. Adequate analgesia*
- *Massive PE with shock: thrombolysis on Consultant decision; otherwise anticoagulate with Inj. Enoxaparin or UFH*
- *Tamponade: fluid bolus, urgent echocardiography, pericardiocentesis*
- *Tension pneumothorax: immediate needle decompression, then intercostal drain — do not wait for a chest X-ray*

**Disposition**

- All patients with suspected ACS are admitted to a monitored bed; **no suspected-ACS patient is discharged from the ED on a single normal ECG and troponin**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Haemodynamic instability, SBP \<90 mmHg or shock index \>1
- ECG showing ST elevation, new ST depression, new LBBB, or dynamic T-wave changes
- Ongoing pain with profuse sweating, vomiting or pallor
- Syncope with chest pain
- Pulse or BP differential between limbs; chest pain with a new neurological deficit
- Hypoxia with a clear chest on auscultation (**think pulmonary embolism**)
- Tracheal deviation, unilateral absent breath sounds, distended neck veins
- Chest pain in a patient with recent chemotherapy, malignancy, or a central line

### Quick guide to the killer differentials of chest pain

#### Acute Coronary Syndrome

- Central heaviness or pressure, exertional, with sweating, nausea, giddiness or syncope; radiation jaw to epigastrium or either arm
- Atypical or silent in **diabetics, the elderly, women and CKD patients** — have a low threshold

**ECG**

- **STEMI:** ST elevation ≥1 mm in two contiguous leads; in V2–V3, ≥2 mm in men and ≥1.5 mm in women
  - **II, III, aVF** → inferior wall
  - **V1–V4** → anterior wall
  - **I, aVL, V5, V6** → lateral wall
  - **Tall R and ST depression in V1–V3** → posterior wall — do V7–V9
- **NSTEMI:** ST depression or T inversion with troponin elevation
- **Unstable angina:** typical symptoms \>20 minutes, crescendo pattern, no ECG change and normal troponin
- **Troponin I** above the 99th percentile of the assay
- **Echo:** regional wall motion abnormality

#### Aortic Dissection

- Sudden, tearing, maximal at onset, radiating to the interscapular region or migrating
- **“Chest pain plus” syndrome** — chest pain with stroke, limb weakness, limb ischaemia, paraplegia, anuria or abdominal pain
- BP or pulse differential between limbs; new early diastolic murmur of aortic regurgitation
- Background of hypertension, Marfan syndrome, bicuspid aortic valve, pregnancy, cocaine use
- Chest X-ray: widened mediastinum (**normal in up to 20%**)
- **Diagnosis: CT aortogram**

*Giving aspirin, clopidogrel and heparin to a dissection mistaken for an MI is the classic lethal error — examine both arms before you load.*

#### Pulmonary Embolism

- Sudden breathlessness, pleuritic pain, haemoptysis, syncope
- Risk: recent surgery, immobilisation, malignancy (**very high in the oncology population**), previous VTE, pregnancy or puerperium, oral contraceptives, long travel
- **Hypoxia with a clear chest** is the classic clue
- Tachycardia, tachypnoea, raised JVP, unilateral leg swelling
- **ECG:** sinus tachycardia (commonest), T inversion V1–V3, RBBB, right axis deviation, S1Q3T3 (uncommon)
- **POCUS:** RV dilatation, septal flattening, McConnell’s sign; compression ultrasound of the leg veins
- **Diagnosis: CTPA.** Use Wells score and PERC to decide whether D-dimer is useful at all

### Quick guide to the killer differentials of chest pain (continued)

#### Cardiac tamponade

- Background of trauma, malignancy (**especially lung, breast, lymphoma — common at this hospital**), CKD, tuberculosis, recent MI, post-cardiac surgery
- **Beck’s triad:** hypotension, raised JVP with distended neck veins, muffled heart sounds
- Pulsus paradoxus \>10 mmHg; tachycardia; breathlessness relieved by sitting forward
- **ECG:** low-voltage QRS, electrical alternans
- **POCUS:** circumferential pericardial fluid with right atrial systolic and right ventricular diastolic collapse, plethoric IVC

**Immediate action**

- Wide-bore IV access and **crystalloid bolus** to maintain preload
- **Avoid intubation and positive-pressure ventilation if at all possible** — it can precipitate arrest
- Prepare for **pericardiocentesis**; urgent Cardiology/CTVS

#### Tension pneumothorax

- Background of trauma, COPD, bullous lung disease, tuberculosis, recent central line or lung biopsy, mechanical ventilation
- Sudden severe breathlessness with chest pain
- Tracheal deviation away from the side, unilateral absent breath sounds, hyper-resonance, hypotension, distended neck veins
- **POCUS:** absent lung sliding, barcode/stratosphere sign on M-mode, lung point

**Immediate action**

- **This is a clinical diagnosis — do not wait for a chest X-ray**
- **Needle decompression** with a 16G cannula in the 5th intercostal space, anterior axillary line (or 2nd space mid-clavicular line)
- Followed by **intercostal drain insertion** in the safe triangle

#### Oesophageal rupture (Boerhaave) and other causes

**Boerhaave syndrome**

- Severe retching or vomiting followed by sudden severe substernal pain
- Tachycardia, dyspnoea, sweating, fever, shock
- **Subcutaneous emphysema in the neck; Hamman’s crunch** on auscultation
- Chest X-ray: pneumomediastinum, pleural effusion, hydropneumothorax
- **Diagnosis: CECT thorax with oral water-soluble contrast**; requires broad-spectrum antibiotics and urgent surgical opinion

**Others worth naming**

- **Pericarditis** — sharp pain, worse lying flat, relieved sitting forward, friction rub, widespread concave ST elevation with PR depression
- **Lobar pneumonia** — fever, productive cough, pleuritic pain, focal crepitations, consolidation
- **GORD / peptic ulcer, oesophageal spasm, biliary colic, pancreatitis** — diagnoses of exclusion in the ED
- **Herpes zoster** — dermatomal pain preceding the rash
- **Musculoskeletal** — reproducible local tenderness; never the first diagnosis in a patient at cardiac risk

## 2.2 Shortness of Breath

This protocol replaces the fixed drug sequence of Issue 01 (deriphylline, hydrocortisone, nebulisation and frusemide for every dyspnoeic patient). **Frusemide given to a dehydrated pneumonia patient, or a bronchodilator given for pulmonary embolism, delays the correct treatment.** Decide the mechanism first, then treat.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Onset** — sudden (pulmonary embolism, pneumothorax, acute MI, anaphylaxis, foreign body) versus progressive over days (pneumonia, heart failure, effusion, anaemia)
- **Duration** and whether acute or acute-on-chronic
- **Severity** — breathless on exertion / on minimal activity / at rest; can the patient complete a sentence?
- Positional — **orthopnoea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea, platypnoea**

**Associated symptoms**

- Fever, cough (dry or productive), sputum character, haemoptysis
- Chest pain — central or pleuritic
- Wheeze or audible musical sound; nocturnal or seasonal variation
- Pedal oedema, decreased urine output, weight gain
- Unilateral leg swelling; recent surgery, immobilisation, long travel, malignancy
- Rash, lip or tongue swelling, new drug or food exposure (**anaphylaxis**)
- Recurrent vomiting or choking (**aspiration**)

**Background**

- **Co-morbidity:** asthma, COPD, tuberculosis, ILD, ischaemic or valvular heart disease, **rheumatic heart disease**, CKD with dialysis schedule, CLD, diabetes, malignancy
- **Medication:** inhaler use and technique, adherence to cardiac medication and diuretics, recent steroid course, beta-blockers, chemotherapy
- Smoking, biomass or *chulha* smoke exposure, occupational dust, mining, farming
- Missed dialysis — date of last session

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **can the patient speak in full sentences?**
- Position — tripod, unable to lie flat
- Work of breathing: RR, accessory muscle use, intercostal and subcostal recession, nasal flaring, pursed lips, paradoxical abdominal movement
- **Stridor** (upper airway) versus **wheeze** (lower airway) versus **silent chest** (critical)
- SpO₂ with the delivery device and flow rate recorded
- JVP, pedal oedema, calf tenderness, cyanosis
- Chest: symmetry, tracheal position, percussion note, air entry (**note any unilateral reduction**), crepitations — basal, diffuse or localised; pleural rub
- Cardiac: heart sounds, gallop, murmur, pericardial rub
- Subcutaneous emphysema in neck and chest wall
- Sensorium — **drowsiness or confusion in a breathless patient suggests hypercapnia or exhaustion, and is pre-terminal**
- Look at the tongue, lips and skin for angioedema and urticaria

### Point-of-care investigations

- **ECG** — ischaemia, arrhythmia, right heart strain, low voltage
- **ABG** — always record the FiO₂ or oxygen flow rate so the P/F ratio can be calculated
- **Point-of-care ultrasound (BLUE protocol):**
  - **A-profile with lung sliding** → COPD/asthma; if with DVT → pulmonary embolism
  - **B-profile (bilateral B-lines)** → pulmonary oedema
  - **Absent lung sliding** → pneumothorax
  - **Consolidation, hepatisation, dynamic air bronchogram** → pneumonia
  - Cardiac views — LV function, RV size, valve motion, pericardial fluid; IVC for fluid status
- CBC, RFT, electrolytes, LFT, RBS
- Chest X-ray — **portable if unstable; never send an unstable patient to the radiology department**
- *NT-proBNP where the distinction between cardiac and respiratory dyspnoea is unclear*
- *Troponin I, D-dimer, CTPA — as indicated by pre-test probability*
- Blood culture before antibiotics if infection suspected; sputum for AFB and CBNAAT where relevant
- Urine ketones and ABG in the diabetic with Kussmaul breathing

### Basic initial management

*Consultant Physician / Pulmonology / Critical Care informed. Steps marked*  are Consultant decisions.\*\*\*

**Universal first steps**

- Shift to a **monitored bed**; continuous ECG, SpO₂, NIBP; head-end elevation
- **Oxygen to a target SpO₂ \>94%, or 88–92% in known COPD or chronic CO₂ retention**
- IV access; send samples at cannulation
- **Then treat by mechanism, using the table below** — not by reflex

**Escalation of respiratory support**

- *Nasal cannula → face mask → non-rebreather → high-flow nasal oxygen → NIV → intubation*
- *NIV is first-line in acute exacerbation of COPD with respiratory acidosis and in cardiogenic pulmonary oedema. It is contraindicated with a reduced sensorium, vomiting, haemodynamic instability, facial trauma or an unprotected airway*
- *Prepare for intubation early where there is exhaustion, a silent chest, rising PaCO₂ with falling pH, SpO₂ \<90% despite maximal oxygen, or a falling GCS*

**Common initial drugs, used for the right indication**

- **Nebulised Salbutamol** 2.5–5 mg **+ Ipratropium** 500 mcg, every 20 minutes for three doses — *for bronchospasm only*
- **Inj. Hydrocortisone 100–200 mg IV** or **Inj. Methylprednisolone 40–60 mg IV** — *for asthma, COPD exacerbation and anaphylaxis*
- **Inj. Furosemide 40 mg IV** — *for fluid overload and pulmonary oedema with adequate blood pressure only; harmful in sepsis, pneumonia and hypovolaemia*
- **Inj. Adrenaline 0.5 mg (0.5 mL of 1:1000) IM into the anterolateral thigh** — *for anaphylaxis; repeat every 5 minutes as needed. This is the first and most important drug; steroids and antihistamines are adjuncts*

***Inj. Deriphylline (aminophylline) is no longer recommended as routine therapy for undifferentiated breathlessness.*** It has a narrow therapeutic index, causes arrhythmias and vomiting, and adds little to nebulised bronchodilators. Use only on Consultant instruction in refractory bronchospasm

**Do not forget**

- Treat the precipitant — infection, arrhythmia, ischaemia, anaemia, missed dialysis, non-adherence to medication, pulmonary embolism
- Correct hypoglycaemia and electrolytes; give thiamine where indicated
- If the patient does not improve within 30 minutes, **reassess the diagnosis** rather than repeating the same drug

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Stridor, pooling of secretions, gross lip or tongue swelling, expanding neck mass or injury
- Inability to speak in sentences; RR \>30 or \<10; SpO₂ \<92% despite oxygen
- **Silent chest** on auscultation in an asthmatic — pre-arrest
- Altered sensorium, drowsiness, agitation or exhaustion in a breathless patient
- Unilateral absent air entry with tracheal deviation and hypotension
- Associated chest pain, profuse sweating, hypotension, or shock index \>1
- Hypoxia with a clear chest — suspect pulmonary embolism
- Rising PaCO₂ with falling pH on serial blood gases

### Differential diagnosis of breathlessness — what to look for and what to start

#### Airway and pleural

**Upper airway obstruction / foreign body**

- Sudden choking, drooling, stridor, inability to phonate; low GCS with pooling secretions
- Head-tilt chin-lift or jaw thrust, suction, oxygen; **prepare for a definitive airway and call Anaesthesia and ENT**; back blows and abdominal thrusts for a choking conscious patient

**Anaphylaxis**

- Sudden onset after exposure; urticaria, angioedema, wheeze, vomiting, abdominal pain, hypotension
- **Inj. Adrenaline 0.5 mg IM thigh, repeat 5-minutely**; high-flow oxygen; IV crystalloid 1–2 L; nebulised salbutamol; Inj. Chlorpheniramine 10 mg IV; Inj. Hydrocortisone 200 mg IV; observe 6–12 hours for biphasic reaction

**Pneumothorax**

- Trauma, COPD, TB, tall thin young male; sudden pain and dyspnoea; hyper-resonance, absent breath sounds
- **Tension** → needle decompression then ICD. **Simple** → oxygen, ICD if large or symptomatic

**Pleural effusion**

- TB, malignancy, heart failure, CKD, CLD, pneumonia; stony dull, absent breath sounds
- POCUS-guided **therapeutic thoracocentesis** if in distress; send fluid for cell count, protein, LDH, ADA, cytology, culture

#### Parenchymal and airway disease

**Acute exacerbation of asthma or COPD**

- Wheeze, prolonged expiration, accessory muscle use, tripod position; previous similar episodes, inhaler use
- ABG: acute or acute-on-chronic respiratory acidosis
- **Target SpO₂ 88–92%**; salbutamol + ipratropium nebulisation 20-minutely × 3; systemic steroid; magnesium sulphate 2 g IV over 20 min in severe asthma; **NIV** for respiratory acidosis; antibiotics only if infective exacerbation

**Pneumonia**

- Fever, chills, productive cough, pleuritic pain; focal crepitations, bronchial breathing
- **CURB-65**: Confusion, Urea \>7 mmol/L, RR ≥30, BP \<90/60, age ≥65
- **Blood cultures before antibiotics.** Score 0–1 → oral (Amoxicillin–clavulanate 625 mg TDS + Azithromycin 500 mg OD). Score ≥2 → **Inj. Ceftriaxone 1 g IV BD + Azithromycin 500 mg**; escalate on Consultant advice. Adequate hydration

**Aspiration**

- Reduced sensorium, stroke, alcohol, vomiting; right lower zone changes
- Suction, oxygen, head elevation, NPO; antibiotics on Consultant advice — not automatic for chemical pneumonitis

#### Cardiac, vascular and non-pulmonary

**Acute pulmonary oedema / heart failure**

- Orthopnoea, PND, raised JVP, bilateral basal crepitations, gallop, pedal oedema; check adherence and dialysis schedule
- **POCUS B-profile**, plethoric IVC; chest X-ray bat-wing pattern; NT-proBNP raised
- Sit up, oxygen; **BP \>140/90** → Inj. Furosemide 40 mg IV, NIV and **nitroglycerine infusion**; **normal BP** → furosemide and NIV; **BP \<90** → this is cardiogenic shock, do not give diuretics or nitrates — Consultant and inotrope
- **SCAPE** (sympathetic crashing acute pulmonary oedema): sudden onset, severe hypertension, distress → **NIV plus high-dose nitrate: GTN 400–1000 mcg IV bolus then infusion**, titrated 10–15 minutely. Diuretic is secondary here

**Pulmonary embolism**

- See the chest pain section. Hypoxia with clear chest; RV strain on ECG and POCUS
- *Anticoagulate: Inj. Enoxaparin 1 mg/kg SC BD, or UFH 80 U/kg bolus then 18 U/kg/h. Thrombolysis if shocked — Consultant decision*

**Non-pulmonary causes not to miss**

- **Metabolic acidosis** — DKA, sepsis, renal failure, salicylate, methanol: deep sighing Kussmaul breathing with a clear chest
- **Severe anaemia**, **thyrotoxicosis**, **sepsis**, **massive ascites**, **pain and anxiety** (a diagnosis of exclusion)

## 2.3 Oncology Patient with Cough or Shortness of Breath

In a cancer hospital, breathlessness in a patient on treatment has a differential of its own. **Pulmonary embolism, neutropenic pneumonia, drug-induced pneumonitis and superior vena cava obstruction are all far more likely here than in the general population**, and none of them respond to a bronchodilator.

### Salient history to be taken

**Oncological background — ask every time**

- **Type and site of primary; stage; current treatment intent (curative or palliative)**
- **Last chemotherapy: date, regimen, cycle number**
- Radiotherapy — site, dose, dates (**thoracic radiotherapy in the last 1–6 months** raises radiation pneumonitis)
- **Immunotherapy / checkpoint inhibitor** (nivolumab, pembrolizumab, atezolizumab) — pneumonitis can occur weeks to months later
- Targeted agents — bleomycin, gemcitabine, methotrexate, taxanes, EGFR inhibitors (pneumonitis); anthracyclines, trastuzumab, 5-FU (cardiotoxicity)
- Growth factor (G-CSF) received? Last blood counts and date
- Presence of a **chemoport, PICC or central line** — duration and site

**Current symptoms**

- Onset and progression of breathlessness; cough dry or productive; **haemoptysis**
- Fever, chills, rigors (**fever plus recent chemotherapy = febrile neutropenia until proved otherwise**)
- Chest pain, pleuritic or central
- Facial or arm swelling, headache worse on bending forward, distended neck and chest veins (**SVC obstruction**)
- Hoarseness, stridor, difficulty swallowing (**airway or mediastinal compression**)
- Unilateral leg swelling; previous VTE
- Orthopnoea, weight gain, oedema (cardiotoxicity, pericardial effusion)
- Antiemetics, analgesics and **opioids** taken at home — dose and last dose

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; temperature in every patient
- **Inspect the chemoport / PICC / central line site** — erythema, tenderness, discharge, thrombosis of the arm
- Work of breathing, SpO₂ on room air and on oxygen
- **Face, neck and upper limbs** — plethora, oedema, distended non-pulsatile neck veins, dilated chest wall collaterals, Pemberton’s sign
- Chest: unilateral dullness and absent breath sounds (**malignant effusion**), crepitations — focal or diffuse, wheeze, pleural rub
- Cardiac: muffled sounds, raised JVP, pulsus paradoxus (**malignant pericardial effusion with tamponade**)
- Mouth — mucositis, candidiasis; skin — rash, cellulitis, perianal tenderness (**do not perform a per-rectal examination in neutropenia**)
- Legs — unilateral swelling and calf tenderness
- Sensorium and hydration status

### Point-of-care investigations

- **CBC with absolute neutrophil count** — the single most important test
- **Blood cultures from a peripheral vein AND from each lumen of the central line, before antibiotics**
- ABG with lactate; RBS
- RFT, electrolytes, LFT, **serum calcium (corrected)**, PT/INR
- Procalcitonin and CRP where the picture is unclear
- **Chest X-ray** — remember it may be **normal in neutropenic pneumonia** because there are no neutrophils to form an infiltrate
- **POCUS** — pleural effusion, B-lines, consolidation, pericardial effusion, RV strain, IVC
- Sputum for Gram stain, culture, AFB and CBNAAT; respiratory viral panel in season
- *CT chest (HRCT) — far more sensitive than a chest X-ray in the neutropenic patient; CT pulmonary angiogram if PE suspected; CT thorax with contrast if SVC obstruction suspected*
- *2D echocardiography for suspected cardiotoxicity or pericardial effusion*
- *Pleural fluid aspiration for cell count, protein, LDH, ADA, cytology and culture*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Medical Oncology informed in every case, alongside Pulmonology or Critical Care as required.***

**Immediate**

- Monitored bed; **oxygen to target SpO₂ \>94%**; head-end elevation
- IV access — use the peripheral vein for sampling as well as the port; **never use the port without aseptic technique and a trained nurse**

**If febrile and neutropenia is possible**

- **Do not wait for the counts.** Draw cultures and give the first dose of antibiotic **within 60 minutes of arrival** — see protocol 7.4
- **Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV** *or* **Inj. Cefoperazone–Sulbactam 3 g IV** *or* **Inj. Meropenem 1 g IV** as per unit policy and prior colonisation
- *Add Inj. Vancomycin or Teicoplanin if line infection, skin or soft tissue infection, mucositis, or haemodynamic instability*

**If bronchospasm is genuinely present**

- Nebulised **Salbutamol 2.5 mg + Ipratropium 500 mcg**, repeated as needed
- **Inj. Hydrocortisone 100 mg IV** — *not as a reflex, and note that steroids may mask fever and worsen an untreated fungal infection*

**Condition-specific**

- *Suspected SVC obstruction: head-end elevation, oxygen, Inj. Dexamethasone 8 mg IV BD, urgent contrast CT, oncology and radiation oncology referral for stenting or radiotherapy. Avoid IV lines in the upper limbs*
- *Suspected drug-induced or radiation pneumonitis: exclude infection first, then Inj. Methylprednisolone 1–2 mg/kg/day on Oncology instruction; checkpoint-inhibitor pneumonitis needs high-dose steroids and stopping the drug*
- *Symptomatic malignant pleural effusion: POCUS-guided therapeutic thoracocentesis, drain no more than 1–1.5 L at one sitting to avoid re-expansion pulmonary oedema*
- *Suspected pulmonary embolism: anticoagulate with low-molecular-weight heparin, which is preferred over warfarin in malignancy*
- *Suspected malignant pericardial effusion with tamponade: fluid bolus, urgent echo, pericardiocentesis*
- *If opioid toxicity is suspected (pinpoint pupils, reduced RR, drowsiness): Inj. Naloxone 0.04–0.1 mg IV, titrated — use small doses so as not to precipitate pain crisis and withdrawal*

**Disposition**

- Admit all febrile neutropenic patients and all with hypoxia. **Discuss goals of care and any documented advance directive with the treating oncologist before escalating to intubation** in advanced or refractory disease

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Fever with chemotherapy in the last 14 days** — treat as febrile neutropenia immediately
- SpO₂ \<92%, RR \>30, inability to complete a sentence
- Facial or upper limb swelling with distended neck veins — SVC obstruction
- Stridor, hoarseness, or difficulty in swallowing — airway compression
- Hypotension, shock index \>1, or lactate \>2 mmol/L
- Massive haemoptysis
- New confusion or drowsiness
- Chemoport or PICC site erythema, tenderness or discharge

## 2.4 Syncope and Transient Loss of Consciousness

The task in the ED is **not to explain every faint, but to separate the benign from the arrhythmic and the haemorrhagic**. Most syncope is vasovagal; the small proportion that is cardiac carries a real short-term risk of death, and it is identified by the history, the ECG and the examination — not by admitting everybody.

### Salient history to be taken

**The three questions that decide most cases**

- **1. What was the patient doing at the moment of collapse?**
  - *Standing for a long time, heat, pain, venepuncture, emotion, after a meal* → **vasovagal**
  - *Standing up from lying or sitting* → **orthostatic**
  - **During exertion** → **cardiac until proved otherwise** (aortic stenosis, HOCM, anomalous coronary, arrhythmia)
  - *On coughing, micturating, swallowing, or turning the head* → situational or carotid sinus
- **2. Was there a prodrome?**
  - Nausea, sweating, greying of vision, warmth, tinnitus → reflex syncope
  - **None at all — "I was talking and then I was on the floor"** → arrhythmia
- **3. What happened on recovery?**
  - **Immediate orientation** → syncope
  - **Prolonged confusion, tongue-biting (lateral), incontinence, post-ictal drowsiness** → seizure

**Also ask**

- **Palpitations before the event; chest pain; breathlessness**
- **Family history of sudden death under 40, drowning, or unexplained road accident**
- **Drugs:** antihypertensives, diuretics, alpha blockers, antiarrhythmics, QT-prolonging drugs, insulin, alcohol
- Known structural heart disease, previous myocardial infarction, pacemaker or ICD
- Recurrent episodes, and whether they are stereotyped
- **Injury sustained** — a facial injury with no protective reaction suggests a sudden loss of tone

*Obtain a witness account wherever possible. It is often more useful than the whole workup.*

### Salient clinical examination

- **Vital signs, including a manual respiratory rate and temperature**
- **Lying and standing blood pressure after 3 minutes** — a fall of ≥20 systolic or ≥10 diastolic is significant
- **Blood pressure in both arms**
- **Cardiac:** murmur (especially an ejection systolic murmur radiating to the carotids), added sounds, JVP, signs of failure
- **Neurological:** GCS, focal deficit, tongue and mouth for a lateral bite, neck stiffness
- **Abdomen and rectum** — pulsatile mass, tenderness, melaena, signs of concealed bleeding
- Pallor; per-vaginal loss in a woman of childbearing age
- Examine for injuries sustained in the fall — scalp, cervical spine, wrists, hips

### Point-of-care investigations

- **12-lead ECG in every single patient — this is the one mandatory test**
- **RBS**
- **Urine hCG in every woman of childbearing age** — ruptured ectopic presents as syncope
- CBC (anaemia, occult bleed), electrolytes, RFT
- Troponin only if the history or ECG suggests ischaemia — not as a screen
- POCUS: cardiac function, IVC, **aorta**, free fluid
- *Echocardiography if a murmur or structural disease is suspected*
- *CT pulmonary angiogram only if PE is genuinely suspected — syncope alone is not an indication*

***A CT head is NOT routine. It is indicated only for head injury, a focal deficit, anticoagulation, or a persistently abnormal sensorium.***

### Basic initial management

**Immediate**

- Lie flat with the legs raised; IV access; monitor if any red flag is present
- **Treat the obvious and dangerous first:** hypoglycaemia, bleeding, arrhythmia, anaphylaxis, drug effect
- **Stop or reduce the offending drug** and document the change

**Reflex (vasovagal) and situational syncope**

- **Reassurance and explanation — this is the treatment**
- Advise on trigger avoidance, adequate salt and fluid intake, and **counter-pressure manoeuvres** (leg crossing, hand grip, tensing the arms at the onset of a prodrome)
- Advise sitting or lying at the first warning symptom

**Orthostatic syncope**

- Rehydrate; review antihypertensives, diuretics, alpha blockers and antidepressants
- Advise slow rising, compression stockings, and avoiding large hot meals and alcohol
- Look for an underlying cause: bleeding, adrenal insufficiency, autonomic neuropathy in the diabetic

**Suspected cardiac syncope — admit and monitor**

- **Continuous cardiac monitoring; cardiology referral; echocardiography**
- Treat the arrhythmia per Section 9.6; correct potassium and magnesium; **stop QT-prolonging drugs**
- Consider ischaemia, aortic stenosis, HOCM, pulmonary embolism, tamponade and dissection

**Disposition**

- **Admit** — any red flag below, an abnormal ECG, exertional syncope, a significant injury, or an elderly patient with no clear cause
- **Observe and reassess** — intermediate risk, or an unclear history awaiting a witness account
- **Discharge** — typical vasovagal or situational syncope, normal ECG, normal examination, a reliable escort and written advice
- **Advise every patient not to drive until reviewed**, and record that the advice was given

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Syncope during exertion** — cardiac until proved otherwise
- **Syncope while supine** — almost never vasovagal
- **No prodrome, or palpitations immediately before the event**
- **Abnormal ECG** — any conduction abnormality, ischaemia, pre-excitation (delta wave), Brugada pattern, long or short QT, epsilon wave, pathological Q waves, sustained bradycardia
- **Family history of sudden cardiac death under 40**
- **New murmur, or known structural heart disease or heart failure**
- **Syncope with chest pain, breathlessness, back pain or abdominal pain** — think ACS, PE, dissection, ruptured AAA, ruptured ectopic
- **Syncope with a fall in haemoglobin, melaena, or a pulsatile abdominal mass**
- **Injury without any protective reaction**, particularly a facial injury

### Quick guide — syncope or seizure, and the diagnoses that must not be missed

#### Syncope versus seizure

**Favours syncope**

- Prodrome of nausea, sweating, greying vision \| Pallor during the event \| Brief duration (seconds) \| **Immediate recovery of orientation** \| Triggered by posture, pain or emotion

*Brief jerking (convulsive syncope) occurs in up to half of faints and does not make it a seizure.*

**Favours seizure**

- Aura (déjà vu, epigastric rising, olfactory) \| **Lateral tongue bite** \| Head turning, sustained rhythmic jerking \| Cyanosis rather than pallor \| **Post-ictal confusion lasting minutes** \| Occurrence from sleep

**Neither — consider**

- Hypoglycaemia \| Intoxication \| Psychogenic non-epileptic attack \| Drop attack \| Cataplexy \| Vertebrobasilar ischaemia (never isolated syncope)

#### The dangerous causes, and how they announce themselves

- **Arrhythmia** — no prodrome, palpitations, abnormal ECG, structural heart disease
- **Aortic stenosis or HOCM** — exertional syncope with a murmur
- **Acute coronary syndrome** — syncope may be the only symptom in the elderly and the diabetic
- **Pulmonary embolism** — syncope with breathlessness, tachycardia or hypoxia
- **Aortic dissection** — syncope with tearing pain, a pulse deficit or a BP differential
- **Ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm** — syncope with back or abdominal pain in an older patient; POCUS the aorta
- **Ruptured ectopic pregnancy** — syncope in a woman of childbearing age; do the test
- **Occult GI bleed** — syncope with pallor; examine the rectum
- **Subarachnoid haemorrhage** — syncope with a sudden severe headache

# 3.0 Nausea, Vomiting, Diarrhoea, GI Bleed and Acute Abdominal Pain

Two cautions frame this section. **Vomiting does not localise the pathology to the abdomen** — myocardial infarction, raised intracranial pressure, diabetic ketoacidosis, glaucoma and pregnancy all present with vomiting. And **abdominal pain in the elderly, the diabetic, the immunosuppressed and the patient on steroids is dangerously under-expressed** — peritonitis may present with nothing but tachycardia and a rising lactate.

## 3.1 Acute Abdominal Pain

The purpose of the ED assessment is not to reach a final diagnosis. It is to answer three questions: **is the patient in shock, is there peritonitis, and does this need a surgeon now?**

### Salient history to be taken

**LMN-OPQRST mnemonic**

- **L** – Last menstrual period and full menstrual history in every woman of reproductive age
- **M** – Medical history: diabetes, hypertension, CAD, CKD, CLD, malignancy, previous abdominal surgery, tuberculosis, sickle cell
- **N** – Natal and antenatal history where relevant
- **O** – Onset: sudden and maximal (perforation, ruptured aneurysm, torsion, ectopic) versus gradual (inflammatory)
- **P** – Provoking and palliating factors: food, movement, position, defecation, vomiting, drugs
- **Q** – Quality: colicky (obstruction, ureteric, biliary) versus constant and boring (inflammation, pancreatitis) versus tearing (aneurysm)
- **R** – Radiation: to the back (pancreatitis, aneurysm, posterior ulcer), tip of the shoulder (diaphragmatic irritation), groin (ureteric), right scapula (biliary)
- **S** – Site and shift of site (**periumbilical shifting to right iliac fossa — appendicitis**)
- **T** – Timing, and **T**reatment already taken (analgesics may mask; antibiotics may partially treat)

**Associated symptoms**

- Fever with chills; vomiting — **bilious, feculent, or projectile**
- **Constipation and obstipation (no flatus)** — the cardinal symptom of obstruction
- Abdominal distension; jaundice; haematemesis; melaena; bleeding per rectum
- Dysuria, haematuria, decreased urine output, retention
- Bleeding or discharge per vaginam; syncope in a woman of reproductive age
- Chest pain, breathlessness, cough (**basal pneumonia and inferior MI both present as upper abdominal pain**)
- Scrotal or testicular pain (**always ask a male with lower abdominal pain**)

**Oncology-specific**

- Chemotherapy in the last 14 days (**neutropenic enterocolitis**), known peritoneal disease, ascites, stoma, previous bowel surgery or stenting

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **temperature, and shock index in every patient**
- General: pallor, icterus, dehydration, stigmata of chronic liver disease, lymphadenopathy

**Abdomen**

- **Inspection** — distension, visible peristalsis, scars, sinuses, dilated veins, hernial orifices, **flank or periumbilical ecchymosis**, stoma
- **Palpation** — begin away from the pain; tenderness, **guarding, rigidity, rebound**, mass, organomegaly, **expansile pulsatile mass**
- **Percussion** — shifting dullness, fluid thrill, loss of liver dullness (**perforation**), percussion tenderness (a gentler and more reliable sign than rebound)
- **Auscultation** — absent (peritonitis, late obstruction) or exaggerated tinkling (early obstruction) bowel sounds; bruit
- **Hernial orifices in every patient** — an obstructed hernia is repeatedly missed
- **Scrotal and testicular examination in every male** — torsion presents as abdominal pain
- **Per-rectal examination** when indicated — melaena, mass, tenderness, empty rectum in obstruction. *Avoid in neutropenia*
- **Per-vaginal/speculum examination** on Consultant advice — cervical motion tenderness, adnexal mass, discharge, bleeding
- Chest and cardiac auscultation — basal crepitations, murmur, arrhythmia
- Peripheral pulses — all four limbs (**mesenteric ischaemia, dissection**)

### Point-of-care investigations

- **ECG** in all patients over 40, with epigastric pain, or with any cardiac risk factor
- **RBS**; **urine pregnancy test / β-hCG in every woman of reproductive age** — no exceptions
- **ABG with lactate and base deficit** — a normal-looking abdomen with a lactate of 4 is mesenteric ischaemia until proved otherwise
- CBC, RFT, electrolytes, LFT with bilirubin, **serum amylase and lipase**, PT/INR, blood group and cross-match
- Urinalysis and microscopy; urine culture
- Blood culture if febrile or septic

**Point-of-care ultrasound at the bedside**

- Free fluid (Morison’s pouch, splenorenal, pelvis), **abdominal aorta diameter**, gallbladder, hydronephrosis, bladder volume, IVC

**Formal imaging**

- **Erect chest X-ray and supine abdomen** (or left lateral decubitus if unable to sit) — free gas, dilated loops, air–fluid levels
- USG abdomen and pelvis — appendicitis, gallbladder, liver, kidney, gynaecological pathology, pancreas, free fluid
- *CECT abdomen and pelvis — the definitive test for obstruction, perforation, collection, and vascular pathology; CT angiography for suspected mesenteric ischaemia or aneurysm*

*Never send an unstable patient for imaging. Resuscitate first, image second.*

### Basic initial management

*Consultant Surgery / Gastroenterology / Urology / Gynaecology informed as appropriate. Steps marked*  are Consultant decisions.\*\*\*

**If the patient is unstable**

- Resuscitation bay; monitored bed; **call for help early — Consultant Intensivist and a second doctor**
- Two **wide-bore 16G** cannulae; blood samples at cannulation; **warmed crystalloid (Ringer’s lactate) 20 mL/kg bolus**, reassessed after each bolus
- Airway support and high-flow oxygen; intubate if the airway is threatened
- *Arrange and activate blood products; massive transfusion protocol for ongoing haemorrhage*
- *Vasopressor (noradrenaline) via a central or a large peripheral line if hypotension persists after adequate fluid — Consultant decision*
- Urinary catheter for **hourly urine output** (target \>0.5 mL/kg/h)
- **Nasogastric tube and aspiration** for suspected obstruction or peritonitis; **avoid in suspected variceal bleed and in oesophageal pathology**
- **Keep NPO**

**Analgesia — give it, do not withhold it**

*Withholding analgesia “so as not to mask the signs” is outdated and cruel; adequate analgesia improves the reliability of examination.*

- **Inj. Paracetamol 1 g IV** — first line
- **Inj. Tramadol 50 mg** in 100 mL NS IV slowly, or **Inj. Fentanyl 1 mcg/kg IV** for severe pain
- **Inj. Drotaverine 40–80 mg IV** or **Inj. Hyoscine butylbromide (Buscopan) 20 mg IV/IM** for colic

**Avoid NSAIDs** in suspected GI bleed, renal impairment, hypovolaemia and the elderly

**Antiemetic and acid suppression**

- **Inj. Ondansetron 4–8 mg IV** *or* **Inj. Metoclopramide 10 mg IV** (avoid metoclopramide in suspected obstruction and in the young because of dystonia)
- **Inj. Pantoprazole 40 mg IV**

**Antibiotics**

- *Empirical broad-spectrum cover after blood cultures in suspected perforation, peritonitis, cholangitis, or sepsis: Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV (2.25 g if AKI/CKD) or Inj. Ceftriaxone 1 g IV + Inj. Metronidazole 500 mg IV*

**Disposition**

- Peritonitis, obstruction, free gas, positive FAST, or unremitting pain → **admit under Surgery**
- Undifferentiated pain that settles → **serial examination in the ED, do not discharge on a single assessment**; specify return precautions in writing

**No patient is referred out of the hospital without the Consultant having seen them.** If referral is on Consultant advice, record the reason and obtain their signature on the file

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Shock index \>1, SBP \<90 mmHg, or a transient response to fluid
- **Rigid, board-like or silent abdomen**; rebound or percussion tenderness
- Lactate \>2 mmol/L or metabolic acidosis with an unremarkable abdominal examination — **suspect mesenteric ischaemia**
- **Elderly hypertensive with abdominal or back pain and a pulsatile mass** — ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm
- **Any woman of reproductive age with abdominal pain, with or without bleeding PV** — exclude ectopic pregnancy
- Bilious or feculent vomiting with obstipation and distension
- Fever with jaundice and right upper quadrant pain (**cholangitis**)
- Abdominal pain within 14 days of chemotherapy (**neutropenic enterocolitis**)
- Scrotal pain, or absent femoral pulses

### Regional differential diagnosis — salient features and the test that settles it

#### Right upper quadrant and epigastrium

**Acute hepatitis**

- RUQ pain and tenderness, nausea, vomiting, malaise, anorexia, dark urine, jaundice, fever
- CBC, **LFT, PT/INR**, viral markers (HAV IgM, HBsAg, anti-HCV, HEV IgM), serum ammonia if encephalopathic; USG to exclude biliary obstruction

**Acute cholecystitis**

- RUQ pain and localised guarding, fever, nausea, vomiting; **positive Murphy’s sign**
- *Red flags for gangrene or empyema: persistent fever, tachycardia, hypotension, palpable mass*
- USG — stones, wall thickening \>3 mm, pericholecystic fluid, sonographic Murphy’s

**Choledocholithiasis and biliary colic**

- Colicky RUQ pain after fatty food, radiating to back or right scapula; jaundice if the duct is obstructed
- USG for CBD dilatation; **MRCP** to confirm; ERCP for therapy

**Acute cholangitis — a life-threatening emergency**

- **Charcot’s triad:** fever + jaundice + RUQ pain. Add hypotension and altered sensorium → **Reynolds’ pentad**
- **Blood cultures before antibiotics**, then Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam; **urgent biliary decompression by ERCP** is the definitive treatment — antibiotics alone will not save the patient

**Acute pancreatitis**

- Severe constant epigastric pain radiating to the back, relieved by sitting forward; vomiting
- **Lipase (or amylase) more than 3× the upper limit of normal**; CECT only after 72 hours unless a complication is suspected; aggressive fluid resuscitation with Ringer’s lactate

#### Right and left lower quadrant, and pelvis

**Acute appendicitis**

- Periumbilical pain **shifting to McBurney’s point** (two-thirds along umbilicus to ASIS), anorexia, low-grade fever, vomiting after the pain begins
- CBC, CRP, urinalysis, **β-hCG**; USG or CT to confirm; **Alvarado score** aids stratification

**Ectopic pregnancy — exclude in every woman of reproductive age**

- Amenorrhoea, constant crampy lower abdominal pain, spotting or bleeding PV, syncope, shoulder-tip pain
- Tender adnexal mass, cervical motion tenderness; **hypotension out of proportion to visible bleeding**
- **Urine pregnancy test, serum β-hCG, transvaginal USG, free fluid on FAST**; wide-bore access, blood cross-matched, immediate Gynaecology referral

**Pelvic inflammatory disease / salpingitis**

- Lower abdominal pain, purulent vaginal discharge, cervical motion tenderness, fever
- USG for tubo-ovarian abscess; screen for chlamydia and gonorrhoea; treat both partners

**Testicular torsion**

- **Sudden severe testicular pain, often presenting as lower abdominal pain in an adolescent**; high-riding transverse testis, absent cremasteric reflex
- **This is a six-hour emergency — call Urology immediately, do not wait for a Doppler**

**Ovarian torsion, ruptured ovarian cyst, mittelschmerz**

- Sudden unilateral pain, vomiting; USG with Doppler

#### Diffuse, vascular and oncological

**Hollow viscus perforation**

- Sudden severe pain, board-like rigidity, absent bowel sounds, loss of liver dullness; NSAID or steroid use, known ulcer, typhoid
- **Erect chest X-ray — gas under the diaphragm** (absent in up to 30%; CT is more sensitive). Resuscitate, NPO, NG tube, antibiotics, urgent surgery

**Intestinal obstruction**
