- Colicky pain, vomiting, distension, obstipation; previous surgery (adhesions), hernia, malignancy
- X-ray: dilated loops with air–fluid levels. **NG decompression, IV fluids, correct potassium, NPO, catheterise, surgical review**

**Acute mesenteric ischaemia**

- **Pain out of proportion to a soft abdomen**, in an elderly patient with atrial fibrillation, heart failure or vascular disease
- **Lactic acidosis with an unremarkable abdomen.** CT angiography. Mortality is high and rises with every hour of delay

**Ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm**

- Elderly hypertensive; sudden abdominal or back pain, syncope, **pulsatile expansile mass**, unequal femoral pulses
- Bedside aorta view on POCUS; do not delay for CT if unstable — straight to theatre

**Neutropenic enterocolitis (typhlitis)**

- **Fever, right iliac fossa pain and diarrhoea within 14 days of chemotherapy**
- CT: caecal wall thickening. **Bowel rest, IV fluids, broad-spectrum antibiotics with anaerobic cover, no per-rectal examination, no enema**; surgery only for perforation or uncontrolled bleeding

**Malignant bowel obstruction**

- Known intra-abdominal malignancy, progressive vomiting and distension. NG decompression, dexamethasone, octreotide and antiemetics; **discuss goals of care before surgical referral**

**Medical mimics not to miss**

- **Inferior wall MI, basal pneumonia, diabetic ketoacidosis, hypercalcaemia, adrenal crisis, sickle cell crisis, lead poisoning, acute intermittent porphyria, herpes zoster, spontaneous bacterial peritonitis in the cirrhotic**

## 3.2 Haematemesis / Upper Gastrointestinal Bleed

Issue 01 listed the history and the signs of blood loss but stopped short of management. **The two decisions that change outcome are made in the first thirty minutes: protect the airway, and decide whether this is variceal or non-variceal** — because the drugs are different.

### Salient history to be taken

**Characterise the bleed**

- Quantity, number of episodes, amount per episode; **fresh red blood, clots, or coffee-ground**
- **Melaena** — black, tarry, sticky, offensive stool (indicates ≥ 50–100 mL of blood, and may persist for days after bleeding stops)
- Haematochezia with haemodynamic instability suggests a **brisk upper** GI bleed, not a lower one
- Distinguish haematemesis from **haemoptysis** and from swallowed epistaxis — see the comparison box

**Aetiological history**

- **Chronic liver disease**: alcohol, viral hepatitis, previous jaundice, ascites, known varices, previous banding or sclerotherapy → *variceal*
- **NSAIDs, aspirin, steroids, known acid peptic disease, epigastric pain relieved by food** → *peptic ulcer*
- **Repeated retching or vomiting before the blood appeared, binge drinking, DKA** → *Mallory–Weiss tear*
- **Weight loss, anorexia, dysphagia, early satiety** → *gastric or oesophageal malignancy*
- Recent endoscopic procedure, banding, polypectomy, or aorto-enteric graft
- **Anticoagulants and antiplatelets** — warfarin, NOACs, aspirin, clopidogrel, and the time of the last dose

**Warning features**

- Altered sensorium (**a late and ominous sign**), syncope, postural giddiness
- Decreased urine output; chest pain or breathlessness (demand ischaemia)
- Fever, jaundice, abdominal distension in the known cirrhotic (**suspect SBP as a precipitant**)

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey with the airway assessed first — **massive haematemesis with a reduced sensorium is an airway emergency**
- Vitals with **postural change**: a fall of systolic BP \>20 mmHg or rise of pulse \>20/min on standing indicates significant volume loss
- **Shock index (HR ÷ SBP) \>0.9** suggests significant blood loss; **\>1.0** predicts ICU admission

**Signs of significant loss**

- Cold clammy peripheries, thready pulse, tachycardia, peripheral cyanosis, tachypnoea
- *Orthostatic systolic drop \>30 mmHg ≈ \>10% volume loss; supine SBP \<90 mmHg ≈ \>20% loss*
- Reduced urine output

**Signs of chronic liver disease**

- Icterus, spider naevi, palmar erythema, gynaecomastia, loss of axillary hair, testicular atrophy, parotid enlargement, Dupuytren’s contracture, caput medusae, **asterixis**
- Ascites — shifting dullness, fluid thrill; splenomegaly

**Other**

- Per-abdomen — epigastric tenderness, mass, organomegaly
- **Per-rectal examination for melaena** — do it; do not rely on the history
- CNS — GCS, focal deficit, grade of encephalopathy
- Chest — aspiration, effusion, crepitations

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Blood group and cross-match — the very first sample.** Keep at least 2–4 units reserved
- CBC (**note that the initial haemoglobin does not reflect acute loss**), repeat 6–12 hourly
- **ABG with lactate and base deficit**
- RFT and electrolytes — **a raised urea with a normal creatinine supports an upper GI source**
- LFT with bilirubin and albumin; **PT/INR and aPTT**
- RBS; ECG if aged \>50 or with known CAD (**demand ischaemia is common and easily missed**)
- **USG abdomen** if chronic liver disease is undiagnosed — portal vein, spleen size, ascites, portal vein thrombosis
- Diagnostic ascitic tap in the cirrhotic with ascites, to exclude **spontaneous bacterial peritonitis**
- Chest X-ray if aspiration is suspected

*Never send an unstable patient for ultrasound or endoscopy suite transfer without a doctor accompanying.*

**Risk stratification**

- **Glasgow–Blatchford Score** — urea, haemoglobin, systolic BP, pulse ≥100, melaena, syncope, hepatic disease, cardiac failure
  - **0–1** → low risk, may be suitable for outpatient management
  - **≥2** → needs inpatient evaluation
  - **\>7** → high likelihood of needing urgent endoscopy, transfusion or intensive monitoring

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Gastroenterology / Physician and Anaesthesia informed. Blood bank alerted at the same time.***

**Resuscitation**

- **Two wide-bore 18G or 16G cannulae**; samples drawn at cannulation
- **Ringer’s lactate or normal saline 500–1000 mL bolus** if shocked, then reassess; avoid over-transfusion of crystalloid, which worsens portal pressure and dilutes clotting factors
- *Protect the airway: intubate before endoscopy if there is massive bleeding, a reduced sensorium, or an inability to clear blood*
- Head-end elevation and left lateral position; suction at the bedside
- **Restrictive transfusion strategy: transfuse PRBC to keep haemoglobin 7–9 g/dL** (target \>8 g/dL if known coronary disease). **Over-transfusion in variceal bleeding increases rebleeding and mortality**
- *Platelets if \<50,000 with active bleeding; FFP if INR \>1.7 with active bleeding; do not correct the INR of a cirrhotic reflexively — it does not reflect bleeding risk*
- *Activate massive transfusion protocol for ongoing bleeding with shock*

**Drugs — variceal bleed (known or suspected chronic liver disease)**

- **Inj. Terlipressin 2 mg IV stat, then 1–2 mg IV every 4–6 hours** *(caution in ischaemic heart disease and peripheral vascular disease; watch for hyponatraemia)* **OR**
- **Inj. Octreotide 50 mcg IV bolus then 50 mcg/h infusion** *or* **Inj. Somatostatin 250 mcg bolus then 250–500 mcg/h**
- **Inj. Ceftriaxone 1 g IV OD for 7 days** — antibiotic prophylaxis is **mandatory** in cirrhotic GI bleed and reduces mortality
- **Inj. Pantoprazole 40 mg IV** — continued until varices are confirmed as the source
- *Lactulose 30 mL via NG tube to clear blood from the gut and prevent encephalopathy*

**Drugs — non-variceal bleed**

- **Inj. Pantoprazole 80 mg IV bolus, followed by 8 mg/h infusion** (or 40 mg IV BD if an infusion is not feasible)
- **Inj. Ondansetron 4–8 mg IV** for vomiting

*Tranexamic acid is not recommended for routine upper GI bleeding — the HALT-IT trial showed no mortality benefit and an excess of venous thromboembolism and seizures.*

**Anticoagulation and antiplatelets**

- *Stop the offending agent. Reverse warfarin with Inj. Vitamin K 10 mg IV plus PCC or FFP in major bleeding. Discuss NOAC reversal and the thrombotic risk of stopping antiplatelets after recent stenting with the Consultant — do not stop dual antiplatelets unilaterally in a patient with a stent placed in the last 6 months*

**Definitive management**

- **Inform Consultant Gastroenterology for endoscopy: within 12 hours for suspected variceal bleeding, within 24 hours for non-variceal bleeding, and immediately if the patient cannot be stabilised**
- *Balloon tamponade (Sengstaken–Blakemore) only as a bridge in exsanguinating variceal bleeding, with the airway secured*
- **NG lavage** only to assess for ongoing bleeding, only with a stable airway, and **never in suspected variceal bleeding or corrosive injury**

**Post-endoscopy**

- Repeat CBC if unstable or bleeding continues, otherwise at 12 hours
- **Rebleed criteria:** \>100 mL fresh bleed in 2 hours, unstable vitals, haemoglobin fall \>3 g/dL, haematocrit fall \>9%. Monitor vitals two-hourly

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Haematemesis with a reduced sensorium — **airway first**
- SBP \<90 mmHg, shock index \>1, postural drop, cold clammy peripheries
- Ongoing fresh haematemesis, or haematochezia with instability
- Known cirrhosis with encephalopathy, jaundice or ascites
- Haemoglobin \<7 g/dL, or lactate \>2 mmol/L
- Age \>60, or significant comorbidity (CAD, CKD, malignancy)
- Patient on anticoagulants, or INR \>1.7
- Glasgow–Blatchford score \>7

### Distinguishing the source of bleeding — a bedside guide

#### Haematemesis (upper GI)

- Background of chronic liver disease, peptic ulcer, NSAIDs, malignancy
- Dark brown, coffee-ground or fresh red with clots
- **Vomiting precedes the blood**
- Not frothy
- **Melaena present**
- Food particles may be present
- **Acidic pH**
- Raised blood urea with normal creatinine

#### Haemoptysis (respiratory)

- Background of tuberculosis, bronchiectasis, lung malignancy, mitral stenosis, anticoagulation
- Bright red
- **Cough precedes the blood**
- **Frothy** because of admixed air
- **Melaena absent**
- No food particles
- **Alkaline pH**
- *Massive haemoptysis in the ED is best defined functionally: any amount that compromises airway, breathing or haemodynamics. Position the bleeding side down, secure the airway, Inj. Tranexamic acid 1 g IV, and arrange CT bronchial angiography and bronchial artery embolisation*

#### Oro-nasal bleed

- Blood trickling from the nose or posterior pharynx, swallowed and later vomited

**Anterior epistaxis**

- Pinch the soft part of the nose continuously for **15–20 minutes**, patient sitting and leaning forward
- Topical oxymetazoline or phenylephrine; ice pack

**Posterior epistaxis**

- Check and treat hypertension if BP \>160/90
- Anterior or posterior nasal packing; refer ENT for cauterisation

**Oral bleed**

- Identify the source — trauma, tooth socket, tumour, mucositis
- Local pressure, topical tranexamic acid or vasoconstrictor; check the coagulation profile

## 3.3 Bleeding Per Rectum / Lower Gastrointestinal Bleed

The first task is to decide whether this is truly a lower GI bleed. **Up to 15% of patients with apparent lower GI bleeding and haemodynamic instability are actually bleeding briskly from an upper source** — which is why an NG aspirate or an early upper endoscopy matters in the unstable patient.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Blood alone, blood mixed with stool, blood on the paper, or blood coating the stool?**
- Colour — bright red (distal), maroon (right colon or small bowel), **black tarry melaena (upper GI)**
- Amount and number of episodes; duration
- Clots present or absent

**Pointers to the cause**

- Diarrhoea with blood and mucus, tenesmus → **colitis, inflammatory bowel disease, dysentery**
- Abdominal pain → colitis, **ischaemic colitis**, malignancy
- Constipation, straining, pain on defecation → **anal fissure, haemorrhoids**
- Painless massive bleed in the elderly → **diverticular disease, angiodysplasia**
- Weight loss, altered bowel habit, tenesmus → **colorectal malignancy**
- Recent polypectomy, biopsy or radiotherapy to the pelvis (**radiation proctitis**)
- Known haemorrhoids, previous similar episodes and their treatment

**Background**

- Chronic liver disease, coagulopathy, chronic kidney disease
- **Anticoagulants, antiplatelets, NSAIDs**
- Malignancy and chemotherapy (**neutropenic enterocolitis, chemotherapy-related mucosal injury**)
- Fever and recent travel or food history (infective colitis)

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **shock index in every patient**
- Postural blood pressure and pulse if the patient is stable enough to sit

**Signs of significant blood loss**

- Cold clammy peripheries, thready pulse, peripheral cyanosis, tachypnoea, reduced urine output
- *Orthostatic systolic drop \>30 mmHg ≈ \>10% volume loss; supine SBP \<90 mmHg ≈ \>20% loss*
- Pallor; icterus and stigmata of chronic liver disease

**Abdomen and perineum**

- Tenderness, guarding, mass, organomegaly, bowel sounds
- **Per-rectal examination and anoscopy** — fresh blood, melaena, haemorrhoids, fissure (usually posterior midline), mass, ulcer

***Do not perform a per-rectal examination in a neutropenic patient*** — it risks bacteraemia and perineal abscess

- Inspect the perianal skin — fistula, abscess, fissure, prolapsed pile

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Blood group and cross-match first**; keep units reserved
- **CBC — haemoglobin and haematocrit repeated 12-hourly** in active bleeding; platelet count
- ABG with lactate; RBS
- RFT and electrolytes, LFT, **PT/INR, aPTT**
- β-hCG in women of reproductive age
- Stool for routine, microscopy, occult blood and culture where infective colitis is suspected; **stool for C. difficile toxin** after recent antibiotics or chemotherapy

**Imaging and endoscopy**

- **CT angiography** — the investigation of choice in ongoing brisk bleeding; localises the site before intervention
- *Colonoscopy after bowel preparation for stable patients; sigmoidoscopy or anoscopy for suspected distal source*
- *Upper GI endoscopy if the patient is unstable or the NG aspirate is bloody — to exclude an upper source*
- *Nuclear scintigraphy or mesenteric angiography with embolisation where available*

**Risk stratification**

- **Oakland score** (age, sex, previous admission for LGIB, PR findings, heart rate, systolic BP, haemoglobin) — a score ≤8 identifies patients suitable for outpatient management

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Gastroenterology / Surgery informed with the history, examination and shock index.***

**Resuscitation**

- Two **wide-bore** cannulae; samples at cannulation; monitored bed
- **Ringer’s lactate or normal saline bolus** while blood is being arranged; **high-flow oxygen if shocked**
- **Transfuse PRBC for haemoglobin \<7 g/dL, or ongoing bleeding with instability** (threshold \<8 g/dL with coronary disease)
- *Platelets if \<50,000 with active bleeding; FFP if INR \>1.5 with active bleeding; Inj. Vitamin K 10 mg IV for 3 days in warfarin-related or coagulopathic bleeding*
- Urinary catheter and hourly output in the unstable patient
- Keep NPO until the plan for endoscopy is decided

**Drugs**

- **Inj. Pantoprazole 40 mg IV** — until an upper source is excluded
- **Inj. Ondansetron 4–8 mg IV** for vomiting
- *Antibiotics are not routine. Give them for suspected infective colitis with systemic features, neutropenic enterocolitis, or perforation — after cultures*

*Inj. Tranexamic acid is not recommended routinely in gastrointestinal bleeding (HALT-IT). Use only on Consultant instruction for a specific indication*

- *Stop anticoagulants and antiplatelets after weighing the thrombotic risk with the Consultant*

**Definitive management**

- *Emergency colonoscopy or sigmoidoscopy, angiographic embolisation, or surgery — as decided by the Gastroenterologist and Surgeon*
- *Local measures for bleeding haemorrhoids or fissure on Surgical advice*

**Disposition**

- Admit all patients with instability, ongoing bleeding, anaemia, coagulopathy, significant comorbidity or age \>60
- A young patient with a small, painful, bright red bleed on the paper, a visible fissure or pile, normal vitals and normal haemoglobin may be managed as an outpatient with surgical follow-up and clear return precautions

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- SBP \<90 mmHg, shock index \>1, postural drop, or ongoing brisk bleeding
- **Haematochezia with haemodynamic instability — assume an upper GI source until excluded**
- Haemoglobin \<7 g/dL or a fall of \>2 g/dL
- Abdominal pain with peritonism — consider ischaemic colitis or perforation
- Fever with abdominal pain and diarrhoea in a patient on chemotherapy
- Patient on anticoagulants, or INR \>1.5
- Age \>60 with comorbidity

## 3.4 Vomiting (Undifferentiated)

**Vomiting does not localise the pathology to the abdomen.** Before reaching for an antiemetic, ask what else in the body causes vomiting — because the answer includes myocardial infarction, raised intracranial pressure, diabetic ketoacidosis, glaucoma and pregnancy.

### Salient history to be taken

- Onset, duration, number of episodes, relation to food
- Content — **bilious, feculent, blood-stained, undigested food from hours earlier (gastric outlet obstruction)**
- **Projectile and without nausea → raised intracranial pressure**
- Nausea present or absent (absent nausea points away from a GI cause)

**Deliberately ask about the non-GI causes**

- **Headache**, visual disturbance, neck stiffness, photophobia, recent head injury
- **Chest pain, sweating, breathlessness** — inferior wall MI presents with vomiting
- Vertigo, unsteady gait, tinnitus, hearing loss (**posterior circulation stroke, labyrinthitis**)
- Polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss, missed insulin (**diabetic ketoacidosis**)
- Dysuria, flank pain, decreased urine output
- Eye pain with a red eye and haloes (**acute angle-closure glaucoma**)
- **Last menstrual period** — pregnancy in every woman of reproductive age
- Abdominal pain, distension, constipation, obstipation

**Drug and toxin history**

- New drugs, **chemotherapy**, opioids, digoxin, theophylline, antibiotics, iron, alcohol
- Toxin or organophosphate exposure; possible overdose
- Steroid-dependent patient who has stopped steroids (**adrenal crisis**)

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey with **temperature and RBS in every patient**

**Hydration and perfusion**

- Skin turgor, mucous membranes, capillary refill, postural BP, urine output, sunken eyes

**Look for the cause**

- Sensorium and GCS; **pupils, neck stiffness, fundus for papilloedema, focal deficit, cerebellar signs, nystagmus**
- Abdomen — distension, tenderness, guarding, succussion splash, bowel sounds, hernial orifices
- Cardiac and chest auscultation; peripheral pulses
- Eye — red painful eye with a fixed mid-dilated pupil and a hard globe
- Smell of ketones or alcohol; features of a toxidrome
- Skin — hyperpigmentation (Addison’s), rash, jaundice

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS in every patient**; urine ketones if diabetic or if RBS is high
- **ECG** — in any patient over 40, with chest symptoms, with electrolyte disturbance, or on digoxin
- **ABG with electrolytes and lactate** — vomiting classically produces **hypokalaemic hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis**; an unexpected acidosis points to DKA, sepsis, uraemia or a toxin
- CBC, RFT, **serum electrolytes including calcium and magnesium**, LFT
- Serum amylase and lipase where pancreatitis is possible
- **Urine pregnancy test in every woman of reproductive age**
- Urinalysis; urine culture
- *Erect chest and abdominal X-ray if obstruction or perforation is suspected*
- *USG abdomen; NCCT head if raised ICP, headache, focal deficit or persistent projectile vomiting*
- *Serum digoxin, drug levels or toxicology as indicated*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant informed with the history, examination and metabolic panel before treatment is escalated.***

**Rehydration and correction**

- IV access; **Normal saline or Ringer’s lactate**, bolus if hypotensive, then maintenance
- **Correct potassium** — vomiting causes hypokalaemia; add KCl 20–40 mEq per litre at no more than 10–20 mEq/h through a peripheral line, with cardiac monitoring
- Correct magnesium and calcium; **hypokalaemia will not correct until magnesium is replaced**
- Urinary catheter and input–output charting where dehydration is severe

**Symptomatic**

- **Inj. Ondansetron 4–8 mg IV** — first line; watch for QT prolongation in the elderly and with other QT-prolonging drugs
- **Inj. Metoclopramide 10 mg IV** — avoid in suspected bowel obstruction, in Parkinson’s disease and in young patients (dystonia)
- **Inj. Pantoprazole 40 mg IV** or **Inj. Ranitidine 50 mg IV**
- *Inj. Promethazine 25 mg IM, or Inj. Dexamethasone 8 mg IV for refractory vomiting — Consultant advice*
- Keep NPO until obstruction is excluded; then start clear fluids and step up

**Treat the cause**

- **DKA** → protocol 4.3   **•**   **Inferior MI** → protocol 2.1   **•**   **Raised ICP** → protocol 4.1
- **Obstruction** → NG decompression, NPO, surgical referral   **•**   **Pregnancy** → Gynaecology
- **Adrenal crisis** → Inj. Hydrocortisone 100 mg IV stat and fluids   **•**   **Glaucoma** → immediate Ophthalmology

**Disposition**

- Discharge only when the patient tolerates oral fluids, is not dehydrated, has a normal metabolic panel and a benign cause; provide ORS and clear return advice

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Projectile vomiting without nausea, or vomiting with headache, neck stiffness or focal deficit** — raised intracranial pressure
- Severe abdominal pain, distension, obstipation, or bilious/feculent vomit — obstruction or peritonitis
- Haematemesis or coffee-ground vomit
- Chest pain, sweating or breathlessness with vomiting — acute coronary syndrome
- Severe dehydration, decreased urine output, postural hypotension
- Metabolic acidosis or high lactate on blood gas
- Vomiting in a patient with a ventriculoperitoneal shunt, or on chemotherapy, or on digoxin
- Vertigo with vomiting and inability to walk — posterior circulation stroke

## 3.5 Acute Gastroenteritis

Most acute gastroenteritis is self-limiting and needs **fluid, not antibiotics**. The job of the ED is to correct the deficit, identify the minority who are septic or dysenteric, and avoid the reflex ciprofloxacin-and-metronidazole prescription that drives resistance and prolongs *C. difficile* risk.

### Salient history to be taken

- Onset, duration, frequency of stools and vomiting
- Stool character — watery, **blood, mucus**, greasy, offensive; volume
- Tenesmus and abdominal cramps; fever with chills
- Ability to keep down oral fluids; urine output and colour; thirst
- Food history — outside food, street food, common source, family members affected; recent travel
- Recent **antibiotics or chemotherapy** (**C. difficile**, neutropenic enterocolitis)
- Drug history — laxatives, metformin, magnesium, colchicine, immunosuppressants

**Background that changes management**

- Age \<5 or \>65; pregnancy
- **Diabetes, CKD, CLD, IHD, immunosuppression, HIV, malignancy on treatment, post-transplant**
- Known inflammatory bowel disease
- Diuretics, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, NSAIDs, digoxin, lithium, insulin (**all become dangerous when the patient is dry**)

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **temperature, and postural BP if the patient can sit**

**Assess dehydration formally**

- **Mild (3–5%)** — thirst, dry mucosa, normal vitals
- **Moderate (6–9%)** — sunken eyes, reduced skin turgor, tachycardia, oliguria, capillary refill 2–3 s
- **Severe (≥10%)** — lethargy, hypotension, cold peripheries, capillary refill \>3 s, anuria, altered sensorium
- Weight where possible — the most accurate measure of deficit in children

**Abdomen**

- Distension, tenderness, guarding, rigidity (**a rigid abdomen is not gastroenteritis**), bowel sounds
- Per-rectal examination if dysentery or a mass is suspected (*not in neutropenia*)
- Look for a **surgical mimic** — appendicitis, obstruction, ischaemic colitis, ectopic pregnancy, DKA, and in children, intussusception

### Point-of-care investigations

- RBS; **urine ketones in diabetics**
- **RFT and serum electrolytes** — sodium, potassium, bicarbonate, chloride; correct before discharge
- CBC; ABG with lactate if the patient is unwell
- Urinalysis and specific gravity
- **Urine pregnancy test in women of reproductive age**
- Stool routine and microscopy, stool culture **if there is blood, fever or immunosuppression**
- **Stool C. difficile toxin/GDH after recent antibiotics or chemotherapy**
- *X-ray abdomen if distension, obstruction or toxic megacolon is suspected*
- *USG abdomen if the pain is disproportionate or a surgical cause is possible*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant informed; every patient with severe dehydration, blood in the stool, or immunosuppression is discussed.***

**Fluids — the mainstay**

- **Mild to moderate dehydration, tolerating orally: ORS**, 50–75 mL/kg over 4 hours, then replace ongoing losses. Give small frequent sips
- **Moderate to severe, or not tolerating orally: IV Ringer’s lactate or normal saline**, 20 mL/kg bolus (10 mL/kg in the elderly or cardiac patient), reassess, repeat as needed
- Then maintenance plus ongoing losses; **add potassium once urine output is established**
- Urinary catheter and hourly output charting in severe dehydration

**Symptomatic**

- **Inj. Ondansetron 4–8 mg IV** for vomiting — improves oral rehydration success
- **Inj. Drotaverine 40 mg IV** or **Hyoscine butylbromide 20 mg IV** for cramps
- **Racecadotril 100 mg TDS** — antisecretory, safe in adults
- **Zinc 20 mg daily for 14 days in children**
- **Probiotics** may shorten the illness modestly

***Avoid loperamide*** *where there is fever, bloody diarrhoea, or suspected C. difficile* — it risks toxic megacolon

**Antibiotics — the exception, not the rule**

***Do not prescribe ciprofloxacin and metronidazole for every case of diarrhoea.***

- *Indicated for: bloody diarrhoea with fever, suspected enteric fever, cholera, severe traveller’s diarrhoea, immunosuppression, or sepsis*
- *Tolerating orally: Tab. Ciprofloxacin 500 mg BD or Tab. Cefixime 200 mg BD for 3–5 days*
- *Not tolerating orally: Inj. Ceftriaxone 1 g IV OD, ± Inj. Metronidazole 500 mg IV TDS if amoebiasis is suspected*
- *Suspected C. difficile: oral Vancomycin 125 mg QID (not IV) — Consultant decision*

**Disposition**

- Admit if severe dehydration, persistent vomiting, significant electrolyte derangement, acute kidney injury, bloody diarrhoea with fever, immunosuppression, or extremes of age
- Discharge with ORS, written return advice, and **explicit instruction to hold metformin, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics and NSAIDs until eating and drinking normally**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Severe dehydration, hypotension, anuria, altered sensorium
- Metabolic acidosis or high lactate on blood gas
- **Bloody diarrhoea with high fever**
- Severe or localised abdominal pain, guarding, distension — consider a surgical cause or toxic megacolon
- Diarrhoea in a patient on chemotherapy within 14 days — see 3.7
- Acute kidney injury, hyperkalaemia or profound hypokalaemia
- Extremes of age, pregnancy, immunosuppression, post-transplant

## 3.6 Oncology Patient with Nausea and Vomiting

Chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting is common, but it is a **diagnosis of exclusion in the ED**. Bowel obstruction, hypercalcaemia, brain metastases, hyponatraemia and opioid toxicity all present the same way and all need different treatment.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Type, site and stage of cancer; treatment intent**
- **Last chemotherapy: date, regimen, cycle** — and its emetogenic potential
  - *Highly emetogenic: cisplatin, high-dose cyclophosphamide, anthracycline–cyclophosphamide combinations, dacarbazine*
  - *Moderately emetogenic: carboplatin, oxaliplatin, irinotecan, ifosfamide*
- Timing of the vomiting relative to chemotherapy:
  - **Acute** (within 24 h)  •  **Delayed** (24–120 h)  •  **Anticipatory** (before the next cycle)  •  **Breakthrough** (despite prophylaxis)
- **Antiemetics already taken at home** — which, what dose, when, and did they help
- Radiotherapy to the abdomen, pelvis or brain

**Red-flag alternative causes — ask directly**

- **Constipation and obstipation, abdominal distension** → obstruction (opioids and vincristine both cause ileus)
- **Headache, projectile vomiting, visual change, seizure, drowsiness** → brain metastases and raised ICP
- **Polyuria, constipation, confusion, bone pain** → hypercalcaemia of malignancy
- **Confusion with normal calcium** → hyponatraemia / SIADH
- **Drowsiness with pinpoint pupils** → opioid toxicity
- Fever, diarrhoea, mucositis, oral thrush
- Poor oral intake and weight loss; last urine output

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **temperature and RBS**
- **Hydration status** — mucosa, skin turgor, capillary refill, postural BP, jugular filling
- Sensorium and GCS; **pupils, fundus, focal neurological deficit, neck stiffness**
- Abdomen — distension, tenderness, mass, **bowel sounds (absent or tinkling)**, hernial orifices, stoma output
- Mouth — **mucositis, candidiasis**, ulceration
- Chemoport / PICC site — erythema, tenderness
- Chest and cardiac examination
- Peripheral neuropathy, gait, cerebellar signs (chemotherapy toxicity)

### Point-of-care investigations

- **CBC with absolute neutrophil count**
- **RFT, serum electrolytes, and serum calcium corrected for albumin** — do not omit calcium and magnesium
- LFT, RBS, ABG with lactate if unwell
- **Urine ketones** if not eating
- Blood cultures if febrile — before antibiotics
- *X-ray abdomen erect and supine if obstruction is suspected*
- *USG or CECT abdomen for obstruction, ascites, collection*
- *NCCT / MRI brain if there are neurological symptoms or unexplained vomiting*
- *Serum sodium with paired urine osmolality and sodium if SIADH is suspected*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Medical Oncology informed with the history, examination and biochemistry.***

**Rehydration**

- IV access; **Inj. Normal saline 1000 mL over 1 hour** if dehydrated, then reassess and continue maintenance
- Correct **potassium and magnesium**; correct calcium disturbance

**Antiemetics — step by step, and combined**

- **Inj. Ondansetron 8 mg IV** (5-HT₃ antagonist) — first line for acute CINV; monitor QT
- **Inj. Dexamethasone 8–12 mg IV** — adds substantially to ondansetron; *withhold or discuss if febrile neutropenia or uncontrolled diabetes is a concern*
- **Inj. Metoclopramide 10 mg IV** — useful for delayed CINV and gastric stasis; **avoid in complete bowel obstruction**
- *Refractory: Tab. Olanzapine 5–10 mg at night, Inj. Ranitidine/Pantoprazole, Tab. Aprepitant 125 mg for the highly emetogenic regimen, Inj. Promethazine 25 mg IM*
- *Anticipatory vomiting: Tab. Lorazepam 0.5–1 mg — behavioural, not pharmacological, in origin*
- **Inj. Pantoprazole 40 mg IV**

*A common practical combination used here — Ranitidine 50 mg + Metoclopramide 10 mg + Ondansetron 8 mg in 100 mL NS over 30 minutes — remains acceptable, but the metoclopramide must be withheld if obstruction is suspected.*

**Treat the cause, not just the symptom**

- *Bowel obstruction → NPO, NG decompression, IV fluids, Inj. Dexamethasone 8 mg IV, Inj. Octreotide 100 mcg SC TDS, surgical opinion, and a goals-of-care discussion*
- *Hypercalcaemia → IV normal saline 200–300 mL/h, then Inj. Zoledronic acid 4 mg IV once rehydrated; calcitonin for rapid effect. Avoid loop diuretics until euvolaemic*
- *Raised ICP from brain metastases → Inj. Dexamethasone 8–16 mg IV stat, head elevation, neuro-imaging, Radiation Oncology referral*
- *Hyponatraemia / SIADH → fluid restriction; hypertonic saline only for seizures or coma, on Consultant instruction*
- *Opioid toxicity → hold the opioid, Inj. Naloxone 0.04–0.1 mg IV titrated, rotate the opioid*
- *Constipation → rule out obstruction first, then laxatives and an enema*

**Disposition**

- Admit for intractable vomiting, dehydration with AKI, electrolyte derangement, obstruction, neurological signs, or febrile neutropenia
- Discharge only when tolerating orally, with a **written antiemetic schedule for the next 5 days** and oncology follow-up

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Fever with chemotherapy in the last 14 days** — febrile neutropenia protocol
- Absent bowel sounds, distension, obstipation — obstruction
- Headache, drowsiness, focal deficit, seizure, papilloedema
- Corrected calcium \>12 mg/dL, or sodium \<125 mEq/L
- Acute kidney injury or severe dehydration
- Haematemesis or coffee-ground vomit
- Pinpoint pupils with a reduced respiratory rate

## 3.7 Oncology Patient with Diarrhoea

Three causes of diarrhoea in this population are treated in **opposite** ways: chemotherapy-induced diarrhoea responds to loperamide, *Clostridioides difficile* colitis is made worse by it, and immune checkpoint-inhibitor colitis needs corticosteroids and stopping the drug. The history decides which one you are looking at.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Type, site and stage of cancer; last chemotherapy date, regimen and cycle**

**Which drug?**

- **Irinotecan** — *early* cholinergic diarrhoea within hours (with cramps, sweating, lacrimation) and *late* diarrhoea after 24 hours
- **5-Fluorouracil, capecitabine, methotrexate, cytarabine** — mucosal injury
- **Immune checkpoint inhibitors** (nivolumab, pembrolizumab, ipilimumab, atezolizumab) — **immune-mediated colitis, may occur weeks to months after a dose**
- **Tyrosine kinase inhibitors** — sunitinib, sorafenib, lapatinib
- **Recent antibiotics** → *C. difficile*
- **Pelvic or abdominal radiotherapy** → radiation enteritis or proctitis

**Characterise**

- Number of stools per day **above the patient’s baseline** — this is how CTCAE grades severity
- Blood, mucus, tenesmus, nocturnal stools, incontinence
- Abdominal pain and cramps; fever; vomiting
- Oral intake, urine output, weight loss, giddiness on standing
- Antidiarrhoeals already taken at home, dose and response
- Stoma output if present

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **temperature and postural blood pressure**
- **Hydration and perfusion** — mucosa, skin turgor, capillary refill, jugular filling, sensorium
- Abdomen — distension, tenderness, **guarding and rigidity (perforation)**, bowel sounds, **right iliac fossa tenderness (typhlitis)**

***No per-rectal examination and no enema in a neutropenic patient***

- Perianal inspection only — fissure, fistula, excoriation, abscess
- Mouth — mucositis and candidiasis (a marker of gut mucosal injury)
- Chemoport / PICC site
- Weight, if a baseline exists

### Point-of-care investigations

- **CBC with absolute neutrophil count**
- **RFT, serum electrolytes including potassium, magnesium and calcium**; LFT; RBS
- ABG with lactate; serum albumin
- **Blood cultures if febrile, before antibiotics**
- **Stool: routine, microscopy, culture, and C. difficile toxin/GDH**
- Stool for ova, parasites and cryptosporidium in the profoundly immunosuppressed
- *X-ray abdomen for distension or suspected toxic megacolon*
- *CECT abdomen for suspected neutropenic enterocolitis, colitis or perforation — caecal wall thickening is the classic finding*
- *Sigmoidoscopy with biopsy for suspected immune colitis — Gastroenterology*

**CTCAE grading (stools above baseline per day)**

- **Grade 1:** \<4  •  **Grade 2:** 4–6  •  **Grade 3:** ≥7 or incontinence or needing hospitalisation  •  **Grade 4:** life-threatening

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Medical Oncology informed in every case. Never start loperamide before considering C. difficile and immune colitis.***

**Fluid and electrolytes**

- **Inj. Ringer’s lactate 1000 mL IV over 1 hour** if dehydrated, then reassess and continue
- Correct **potassium and magnesium** aggressively; monitor renal function
- ORS for ongoing losses if tolerating orally

**Non-infective, non-immune chemotherapy-induced diarrhoea (grade 1–2)**

- **Cap. Loperamide 4 mg stat, then 2 mg after every loose stool** (maximum 16 mg/day)
- **Racecadotril 100 mg TDS** as an alternative or adjunct
- **Probiotics** (e.g. *Bacillus clausii*, *Saccharomyces boulardii*) — *avoid in the profoundly neutropenic or those with a central line, because of fungaemia risk*
- Bland diet, avoid lactose, caffeine and high-fibre foods

**Specific situations**

- *Early irinotecan cholinergic syndrome → Inj. Atropine 0.25–0.5 mg SC/IV*
- *Refractory grade 3–4 chemotherapy diarrhoea → Inj. Octreotide 100–150 mcg SC TDS*
- *Suspected C. difficile → oral Vancomycin 125 mg QID for 10 days (or fidaxomicin); stop loperamide; isolate the patient; hand hygiene with soap and water, not alcohol rub*
- *Suspected immune checkpoint-inhibitor colitis → hold the checkpoint inhibitor; grade 2 and above needs Inj. Methylprednisolone 1–2 mg/kg/day IV; exclude infection first; Gastroenterology referral. Loperamide alone is inadequate and delays diagnosis*
- *Suspected neutropenic enterocolitis → bowel rest, IV fluids, Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV or Meropenem, no PR examination, no enema, surgical review*
- *Febrile with neutropenia → cultures then broad-spectrum antibiotics within 60 minutes (protocol 7.4)*

**Disposition**

- Admit for grade 3–4 diarrhoea, fever, neutropenia, dehydration with AKI, electrolyte derangement, blood in the stool, or abdominal tenderness
- Discharge grade 1–2 only if afebrile, non-neutropenic, well hydrated and tolerating orally — with written instructions, loperamide dosing and 24-hour oncology contact

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Fever with recent chemotherapy** — assume febrile neutropenia
- Grade 3–4 diarrhoea, or any diarrhoea with hypotension
- Blood in the stool, severe abdominal pain, distension or guarding
- Right iliac fossa pain with fever and neutropenia — **neutropenic enterocolitis**
- Acute kidney injury, hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia
- Diarrhoea on a checkpoint inhibitor — needs steroids, not loperamide
- Recent antibiotics with profuse watery diarrhoea — test for C. difficile

# 4.0 Central Nervous System — Unconsciousness, Seizure, Altered Sensorium and Weakness

One instruction governs this entire section: **check the blood sugar in every patient with an abnormal sensorium, before anything else.** Hypoglycaemia mimics stroke, seizure, psychosis and coma, is instantly reversible, and causes permanent damage if missed for an hour.

## 4.1 The Unconscious Patient

Coma is a symptom with a very wide differential. Work the airway and the glucose first, then use a structured mnemonic so that nothing is forgotten while the CT is being arranged.

### Salient history to be taken

*History comes from attendants, bystanders, the ambulance crew, old records and the patient’s pockets and phone. Send someone to obtain it while you resuscitate.*

- **Time last seen normal**; onset sudden or gradual; witnessed events
- Preceding symptoms — headache, vomiting, fever, seizure, chest pain, breathlessness, giddiness, focal weakness
- Trauma — a fall may be the cause **or** the consequence of the collapse
- **Access to drugs or poison** — empty strips, containers, pesticide smell, syringes; suicidal intent, recent stressors
- Alcohol or substance use; **insulin or oral hypoglycaemic use, and last meal**
- Known **epilepsy** and adherence to anti-seizure medication
- Known diabetes, hypertension, CAD, CKD, **chronic liver disease**, COPD, thyroid disease, HIV, malignancy, psychiatric illness, previous stroke
- Anticoagulants, antiplatelets, sedatives, opioids, antipsychotics, recent dose changes
- Recent fever, headache, neck stiffness, rash, ear discharge, sinusitis (**CNS infection**)
- Exposure — heat, cold, carbon monoxide, snake or scorpion, altitude, diving

### Salient clinical examination

**Primary survey first**

- **Airway**: is it patent and protected? Pooling of secretions, snoring, gag reflex. **GCS ≤8 usually means intubate**
- **Breathing**: rate and pattern — Cheyne–Stokes, Kussmaul, apneustic, ataxic; SpO₂
- **Circulation**: pulse, BP, perfusion. **Hypertension with bradycardia = raised ICP**
- **Disability**: **RBS immediately**, GCS (E, V, M documented separately), pupils, posturing
- **Exposure**: temperature, full head-to-toe survey, log roll, needle marks, rash, skin colour

**Focused neurological examination in coma**

- **Pupils** — pinpoint (opioid, pontine, organophosphate), mid-position fixed (midbrain), unilateral fixed dilated (**uncal herniation**), bilateral dilated (anticholinergic, hypoxia, brain death)
- Brainstem reflexes — corneal, gag, cough, oculocephalic (**not in suspected cervical injury**)
- **Fundus** — papilloedema, subhyaloid haemorrhage (SAH), hypertensive changes
- Motor response — symmetry, **decorticate versus decerebrate posturing**, tone, deep tendon reflexes, **plantars**
- **Meningeal signs** — neck stiffness, Kernig’s, Brudzinski’s

**Whole-body clues**

- Smell — ketones, alcohol, kerosene, garlic (organophosphate), uraemia, fetor hepaticus
- Stigmata of chronic liver disease, **asterixis**; uraemic frost; thyroid signs; skin pigmentation (Addison’s)
- Signs of head injury — Battle’s sign, raccoon eyes, ENT bleed, CSF leak
- Bladder distension; tongue bite and incontinence (recent seizure)

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS — the first test, at the bedside, in every patient**
- **ABG with lactate, sodium, potassium, ionised calcium and carboxyhaemoglobin if available**
- CBC, **RFT and serum electrolytes including sodium, calcium and magnesium**
- LFT with bilirubin and **serum ammonia** if hepatic encephalopathy is possible
- PT/INR, aPTT; serum osmolality and calculated osmolar gap in suspected toxic alcohol
- **Thyroid function** if myxoedema or thyroid storm is suspected; serum cortisol if adrenal crisis suspected
- Urine toxicology screen; blood alcohol; serum paracetamol and salicylate where relevant
- Blood culture and urine culture if sepsis is suspected; **malaria antigen and peripheral smear**
- **ECG** — arrhythmia, ischaemia, electrolyte pattern, QRS widening or QT prolongation from a toxin

**Imaging**

- **NCCT head — urgently, in almost every unexplained coma**; do not delay it for laboratory results
- *CECT or MRI brain for suspected encephalitis, abscess, space-occupying lesion, venous sinus thrombosis*
- *Lumbar puncture for suspected meningitis — after excluding raised ICP on imaging and correcting coagulopathy. Do not delay the first dose of antibiotic for the LP*
- Chest X-ray; POCUS for cardiac function, IVC and free fluid

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Critical Care / Physician / Neurology informed immediately. Shift to the Resuscitation Bay.***

**The universal first five minutes**

- **Airway**: position, suction, oropharyngeal airway, high-flow oxygen. *Intubate for GCS ≤8, absent gag, hypoxia or hypoventilation*
- **Breathing**: support ventilation; target SpO₂ ≥94% and normocapnia
- **Circulation**: two IV lines, **Ringer’s lactate bolus if SBP \<90**, samples drawn at cannulation

**The “DONT” bundle — give what is reversible**

- **D – Dextrose:** if RBS \<70 mg/dL, **Inj. 25% Dextrose 100 mL IV** (or 50% Dextrose 50 mL), then recheck. Follow with a 10% dextrose infusion if a sulfonylurea or long-acting insulin is involved
- **O – Oxygen:** to target saturation
- **N – Naloxone:** if opioid toxicity is suspected (pinpoint pupils, RR \<12) — **Inj. Naloxone 0.4–2 mg IV**, titrated and repeated
- **T – Thiamine:** **Inj. Thiamine 100–500 mg IV** before or with glucose in alcohol use, malnutrition or hyperemesis, to prevent **Wernicke’s encephalopathy**

**Then treat the cause**

- Position with **head end at 30°**, neck neutral; cervical collar if trauma is possible
- Prevent aspiration — **keep NPO**, left lateral position if not intubated
- Urinary catheter, temperature control, pressure-area care, eye care
- *Suspected raised ICP or herniation → Inj. Mannitol 20% 0.5–1 g/kg IV or 3% saline 3–5 mL/kg; brief hyperventilation only as a bridge*
- *Suspected meningitis → Inj. Ceftriaxone 2 g IV BD + Inj. Vancomycin 15 mg/kg IV BD, add Inj. Ampicillin 2 g IV q4h if elderly, pregnant or immunosuppressed, and Inj. Dexamethasone 10 mg IV just before the first antibiotic dose. Add Inj. Acyclovir 10 mg/kg IV TDS if encephalitis is possible*
- *Ongoing seizure → protocol 4.2.  Poisoning → protocol 5.1.  Stroke → protocol 4.4*
- *Correct sodium, calcium and magnesium; correct severe acidosis on Consultant advice*
- *Hepatic encephalopathy → see the box below*
- Full nursing chart: hourly GCS, pupils, vitals and urine output

**Never label an unconscious patient as “alcohol” or “hysteria” without a glucose, a CT and a period of observation.**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- GCS ≤8, or a fall of ≥2 points
- Unequal, fixed or dilated pupil; extensor posturing; abnormal respiratory pattern
- Cushing response — rising blood pressure with falling heart rate
- RBS \<70 or \>400 mg/dL
- Focal neurological deficit; ongoing or recurrent seizure
- Fever with neck stiffness or a purpuric rash
- Severe metabolic acidosis or lactate \>4 mmol/L on blood gas
- History of toxin ingestion, or an unexplained toxidrome
- Temperature \<35°C or \>40°C

### Causes of coma and altered sensorium — the AEIOU-TIPS and TIME framework

#### Structural and traumatic

- **Trauma** — extradural, subdural, contusion, diffuse axonal injury
- **Intracranial vascular** — ischaemic stroke (especially basilar and brainstem), intracerebral haemorrhage, subarachnoid haemorrhage, **cerebral venous sinus thrombosis** (young, postpartum, dehydrated, on OCPs)
- **Infection** — bacterial and tuberculous meningitis, viral encephalitis (HSV), cerebral malaria, brain abscess, neurocysticercosis, septic encephalopathy
- **Space-occupying lesion** — primary or metastatic tumour, abscess
- **Hypertensive encephalopathy** and **PRES**

**Herniation syndromes to recognise**

- **Uncal** — ipsilateral fixed dilated pupil, contralateral hemiparesis, falling GCS
- **Central** — progressive loss of brainstem reflexes rostral to caudal
- **Tonsillar** — neck stiffness, respiratory arrest, bradycardia

#### Metabolic, endocrine and toxic

- **Hypoglycaemia** — the single most important reversible cause
- **Hyperglycaemic emergencies** — DKA, hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state
- **Hyponatraemia and hypernatraemia**; hypercalcaemia; hypomagnesaemia; hypophosphataemia
- **Uraemic encephalopathy**; **hepatic encephalopathy**
- **Hypoxia and hypercapnia** — CO₂ narcosis in COPD
- **Endocrine** — myxoedema coma, thyroid storm, adrenal crisis, hypopituitarism
- **Wernicke’s encephalopathy** — confusion, ophthalmoplegia, ataxia; give thiamine before glucose
- **Toxins** — alcohol, methanol, ethylene glycol, opioids, benzodiazepines, antipsychotics, tricyclics, organophosphate, aluminium phosphide, carbon monoxide, snake envenomation
- **Environmental** — heat stroke, hypothermia, drowning, electrocution, high-altitude cerebral oedema
- **Post-ictal state** and non-convulsive status epilepticus

#### Hepatic encephalopathy — recognition and management

**West Haven grading**

- **1** — short attention span, reversed sleep cycle, euphoria or anxiety
- **2** — lethargy, apathy, disorientation for time, **asterixis**
- **3** — somnolence to semi-stupor, gross disorientation, responsive to voice
- **4** — coma, unresponsive to voice or pain

**Look for a precipitant — there almost always is one**

- **GI bleed, infection (especially SBP), constipation, dehydration, electrolyte disturbance (hypokalaemia, hyponatraemia), sedatives, excess diuretics, high protein load, portal vein thrombosis, hepatocellular carcinoma**

**Management**

- Airway protection — **grade 3–4 usually needs intubation**
- **Syp. Lactulose 30 mL 4–8 hourly orally or by NG tube**, titrated to 2–3 soft stools per day; **lactulose enema** if not passing stool
- **Tab. Rifaximin 550 mg BD**
- **Pass a nasogastric tube and aspirate** — if the aspirate is blood-stained, manage as UGI bleed (protocol 3.2)
- *Treat infection: Inj. Cefoperazone–Sulbactam 3 g IV BD or as per protocol; diagnostic ascitic tap for SBP*
- **Correct hypokalaemia and hyponatraemia; avoid sedatives; avoid aggressive diuresis**
- *Inj. L-Ornithine L-Aspartate infusion, maximum 20 g/24 h, on Consultant advice*
- *Vitamin K 10 mg IV for 3 days; FFP only for active bleeding or before a procedure*
- *Terlipressin and albumin for hepatorenal syndrome; mannitol for cerebral oedema in acute liver failure*

## 4.2 Seizure and Status Epilepticus

**Status epilepticus is defined as a seizure lasting 5 minutes or more, or two or more seizures without full recovery of consciousness in between.** Treatment is time-driven and dose-driven: the commonest error in Indian emergency departments is an inadequate dose of benzodiazepine, followed by an unnecessary delay before the second-line drug.

### Salient history to be taken

**Semiology — describe the seizure**

- Time of onset, number of episodes, interval between them, total duration
- Any **aura** or warning; focal onset with or without progression to bilateral tonic–clonic
- Loss of consciousness; eye deviation; automatisms
- **Tongue bite (lateral is more specific), urinary or faecal incontinence, secondary trauma**
- Duration and character of the **post-ictal state**

**Background**

- Known seizure disorder — type, drugs, doses, **adherence, recent change or missed doses**, last seizure
- Previous status epilepticus or ICU admission

**Precipitants — ask for each**

- Sleep deprivation, alcohol binge or **alcohol withdrawal**, stress
- Fever and infection; recent head injury; **new drug with an interaction** (macrolides, fluoroquinolones, tramadol)
- Missed dialysis; hypoglycaemia; hyponatraemia; **pregnancy or postpartum (eclampsia)**
- Toxin, overdose, stimulant use, organophosphate, isoniazid
- Known malignancy or brain metastases; HIV; neurocysticercosis; tuberculoma

**Was it really a seizure?**

- Ask about **chest pain, palpitation, prodrome and rapid full recovery** — which suggest syncope, not seizure

### Salient clinical examination

- **During the seizure:** protect from injury, do not restrain, **do not force anything into the mouth**, left lateral position, suction, oxygen
- Time the seizure by the clock — not by impression
- Complete primary survey; **RBS in every patient**; temperature
- Pupils, GCS, focal deficit, **Todd’s paresis** (post-ictal focal weakness that resolves)
- Meningeal signs, fundus, papilloedema
- Head-to-toe for injury sustained during the fit — **posterior shoulder dislocation, head injury, tongue laceration, vertebral compression fracture, aspiration**
- Stigmata of chronic liver disease, uraemia, alcohol withdrawal (tremor, sweating, agitation)
- Signs of a toxidrome; needle marks
- **In a woman of reproductive age: check blood pressure, urine protein and pregnancy status — eclampsia is treated with magnesium, not with a benzodiazepine alone**

*A patient who does not regain consciousness after the convulsions stop may be in non-convulsive status epilepticus — subtle eye deviation or twitching; needs EEG and Neurology.*

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS at the bedside — first**
- **ABG with sodium, potassium, ionised calcium and lactate** (a transient lactic acidosis after a generalised seizure is expected and clears in an hour)
- CBC, **RFT and serum electrolytes — sodium, calcium, magnesium**, LFT
- Serum ammonia if sensorium remains altered
- **Anti-seizure drug levels** (phenytoin, valproate, carbamazepine) where available and adherence is in doubt
- Urine toxicology; blood alcohol; β-hCG in women of reproductive age
- ECG — **QT interval, Brugada pattern, arrhythmia** (a cardiac cause masquerading as seizure)
- Blood culture and urine culture if febrile; malaria smear

**Imaging (after stabilisation)**

- **NCCT head in every first unprovoked seizure**, and in any seizure with head trauma, focal deficit, fever, malignancy, immunosuppression, anticoagulation, persistent altered sensorium, or age \>40
- *MRI brain and EEG on Neurology advice*
- *Lumbar puncture if meningitis or encephalitis is suspected, after imaging*

*A patient with known well-controlled epilepsy, a typical brief seizure, a clear precipitant and full recovery does not automatically need a CT — discuss with the Consultant.*

### Basic initial management

***Time the seizure. Call the Consultant at 5 minutes, not at 30. Prepare for intubation from the outset in refractory status.***

**0–5 minutes — stabilisation**

- Left lateral position, head-end slightly raised; **suction and high-flow oxygen**
- IV access; **RBS**; if \<70 mg/dL, **Inj. 25% Dextrose 100 mL IV with Inj. Thiamine 100 mg IV**
- Continuous monitoring — ECG, SpO₂, NIBP

**5–20 minutes — first-line benzodiazepine (give the full dose)**

- **Inj. Lorazepam 0.1 mg/kg IV — usually 4 mg — at 2 mg/min; may repeat once after 5 minutes** (maximum 8 mg)
- *If no IV access:* **Inj. Midazolam 10 mg IM** (0.2 mg/kg), or intranasal/buccal midazolam
- *Alternative:* **Inj. Diazepam 0.15–0.2 mg/kg IV (10 mg), may repeat once** — shorter duration of anticonvulsant action, so a second-line drug is still required

*Under-dosing the benzodiazepine is the commonest cause of “refractory” status. Give the full weight-based dose.*

**20–40 minutes — second-line anti-seizure drug (choose one)**

- **Inj. Levetiracetam 60 mg/kg IV (maximum 4500 mg)** in 100 mL NS over 15 minutes — preferred: minimal interactions, no cardiac monitoring needed
- **Inj. Sodium Valproate 40 mg/kg IV (maximum 3000 mg)** over 10 minutes — *avoid in liver disease, pregnancy, and suspected mitochondrial disease*
- **Inj. Fosphenytoin 20 mg PE/kg IV** at up to 150 mg PE/min, **or Inj. Phenytoin 20 mg/kg IV in normal saline only, at no more than 50 mg/min with continuous ECG and BP monitoring** — *never mix phenytoin with dextrose; watch for hypotension and arrhythmia; extravasation causes tissue necrosis*

*The Issue 01 dose of levetiracetam at 20 mg/kg is a maintenance-level dose. The loading dose in status epilepticus is 60 mg/kg.*

**\>40 minutes — refractory status epilepticus**

- ***Intubate and ventilate.*** Transfer to ICU with continuous EEG where available
- *Anaesthetic infusion: Midazolam 0.2 mg/kg bolus then 0.05–2 mg/kg/h, or Propofol 1–2 mg/kg bolus then 2–10 mg/kg/h, or Thiopentone — titrated to seizure suppression*

**Special situations**

- ***Eclampsia*** *→ Inj. Magnesium sulphate 4–6 g IV over 20 minutes, then 1–2 g/h infusion; control BP; urgent Obstetrics referral for delivery. Magnesium, not lorazepam, is the drug of choice*
- ***Isoniazid overdose*** *→ Inj. Pyridoxine gram-for-gram with the ingested INH* (empirically 5 g IV)
- ***Alcohol withdrawal*** → benzodiazepines and thiamine; anti-seizure drugs are usually unnecessary
- ***Hyponatraemic seizure*** *→ 3% saline 100 mL IV over 10 minutes*, repeated up to three times until the seizure stops
- ***Organophosphate*** *→ atropine and diazepam; do not use phenytoin*

**Continuing care and disposition**

- Maintenance anti-seizure medication started on Neurology advice; restart the patient’s own drug if the cause was non-adherence
- Monitor for aspiration, rhabdomyolysis (**check CK and urine myoglobin after prolonged status**) and injury
- Admit all status epilepticus, first seizures with an abnormal CT, persistent altered sensorium, or an identified precipitant needing treatment
- **Counsel every discharged patient in writing: no driving, no swimming alone, no working at heights or with machinery, no cooking over an open flame alone, until reviewed by Neurology**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Seizure continuing beyond 5 minutes, or recurrent without recovery
- Failure to regain consciousness after the convulsions stop — **non-convulsive status**
- Focal seizure, or new focal deficit persisting beyond an hour
- Fever with seizure and neck stiffness
- First seizure after the age of 40, or in a patient with malignancy or HIV
- **Seizure in pregnancy or within 6 weeks postpartum — eclampsia until proved otherwise**
- Seizure with head injury, anticoagulation, or a ventriculoperitoneal shunt
- Hypoglycaemia, sodium \<125 mEq/L, or hypocalcaemia
- Hypoxia, hypotension or arrhythmia during or after the seizure

## 4.3 Altered Sensorium — Metabolic and Glycaemic Emergencies

Protocol 4.4.1 covers the undifferentiated unconscious patient. This protocol details the three metabolic derangements that most often present as confusion or drowsiness in this hospital: **hypoglycaemia, the hyperglycaemic emergencies, and hyponatraemia**.

### Salient history to be taken

**Hypoglycaemia**

- Insulin or oral hypoglycaemic use — **which agent, dose, timing, and any recent change**
- **Sulfonylureas (glimepiride, glibenclamide) cause prolonged, recurrent hypoglycaemia** — they cannot be treated with a single bolus and discharged
- Missed or reduced meal; unaccustomed exertion; alcohol binge; vomiting
- Renal or hepatic impairment, sepsis, adrenal insufficiency, malnutrition
- Autonomic warning symptoms — sweating, tremor, palpitation, hunger — or **hypoglycaemia unawareness** in long-standing diabetes

**Hyperglycaemic emergencies**

- Insulin omission or pump failure; new-onset diabetes
- **Precipitant — infection, myocardial infarction, stroke, pancreatitis, steroids, surgery, alcohol**
- Polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss, abdominal pain, vomiting, breathlessness

**Hyponatraemia and others**

- Diuretics, SSRIs, carbamazepine, chemotherapy, recent excess water or beer intake
- Malignancy (**SIADH**), TB, pneumonia, CNS disease
- Vomiting, diarrhoea, poor intake; missed dialysis
- Steroid-dependent patient who has stopped steroids (**adrenal crisis**)

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **RBS in every patient**; temperature
- GCS, pupils, focal deficit, plantars — **hypoglycaemia can produce a dense hemiparesis that reverses with glucose**
- Hydration and perfusion status; postural blood pressure; jugular filling
- **Kussmaul breathing and the smell of ketones** — diabetic ketoacidosis
- Look for the **precipitant**: chest, abdomen, skin, feet (diabetic foot), perineum, IV lines, catheter, teeth
- Signs of infection are often **absent or minimal in DKA even when sepsis is the trigger**
- Asterixis, uraemic frost, stigmata of chronic liver disease
- Hyperpigmentation of palmar creases and buccal mucosa (Addison’s)
- Seizure activity; muscle cramps or weakness (electrolyte)

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS immediately, then hourly during treatment**
- **ABG or VBG with sodium, potassium, chloride, bicarbonate, lactate**
- **Urine ketones or capillary blood ketones** in any diabetic with hyperglycaemia or acidosis
- CBC, RFT, **serum electrolytes including calcium, magnesium and phosphate**, LFT
- **Serum osmolality** (measured and calculated); anion gap and corrected sodium
- ECG — **hyperkalaemia (tall tented T waves), hypokalaemia (U waves), ischaemia**
- Blood culture, urine culture, chest X-ray — look for the precipitant
- Serum amylase and lipase (may be non-specifically raised in DKA)
- *Serum cortisol and TSH where adrenal or thyroid causes are suspected*
- *NCCT head if the sensorium does not improve with correction*

**Defining the picture**

- **DKA:** RBS \>250 mg/dL, pH \<7.30, HCO₃ \<18 mEq/L, ketones positive, anion gap \>12
- **HHS:** RBS usually \>600 mg/dL, osmolality \>320 mOsm/kg, minimal ketosis, pH \>7.30, profound dehydration
- **Hypoglycaemia:** RBS \<70 mg/dL, or \<54 mg/dL as clinically significant

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Physician / Critical Care informed. DKA and HHS are managed in a monitored bed or ICU.***

**Hypoglycaemia**

- **Conscious and cooperative:** 15–20 g oral fast-acting carbohydrate (glucose powder, sugar in water, juice); recheck in 15 minutes
- **Unconscious or unable to swallow:** **Inj. 25% Dextrose 100 mL IV** (or 50% Dextrose 50 mL) through a good-sized vein, then recheck in 15 minutes and repeat if needed
- *No IV access:* **Inj. Glucagon 1 mg IM** — ineffective in alcoholics and in hepatic glycogen depletion
- **Follow with a 10% dextrose infusion** and a complex-carbohydrate meal once alert

***A patient who has taken a sulfonylurea or long-acting insulin must be admitted and observed for at least 24 hours***, with hourly then two-hourly RBS. Recurrent hypoglycaemia is the rule, not the exception

- *Octreotide 50–100 mcg SC 6–8 hourly for refractory sulfonylurea hypoglycaemia — Consultant decision*
- If the sensorium does not recover with a normal RBS, **look for a second diagnosis** — stroke, post-ictal state, sepsis, toxin, prolonged hypoglycaemic brain injury
- Review and correct the cause; **adjust the home regimen and counsel before discharge**

**Diabetic ketoacidosis and hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state**

- **Two wide-bore IV lines. Fluid first, insulin second, potassium alongside**
- **Fluid:** Inj. **0.9% Normal Saline 1000–1500 mL in the first hour** (10 mL/kg in the elderly or cardiac patient), then 250–500 mL/h, guided by perfusion and urine output. Switch to **5% dextrose with saline once RBS falls below 250 mg/dL** — the insulin must continue to clear the ketosis
- **Potassium — check before starting insulin:**
  - **K⁺ \<3.3 mEq/L → hold insulin, replace potassium first** (insulin will cause fatal hypokalaemia)
  - **K⁺ 3.3–5.3 → add 20–40 mEq KCl per litre of fluid**
  - **K⁺ \>5.3 → no potassium; recheck in 2 hours**
- **Insulin:** *Inj. Regular insulin infusion at 0.1 U/kg/h* (a bolus is optional and often unnecessary). Target a fall in glucose of 50–75 mg/dL per hour; do not chase the sugar faster
- **Bicarbonate only if pH \<6.9**, on Consultant instruction
- Urinary catheter and hourly output; NG tube if vomiting or with a reduced sensorium; DVT prophylaxis in HHS
- **Find and treat the precipitant** — infection, MI, stroke, non-adherence
- Monitor hourly RBS, and VBG with electrolytes 2–4 hourly. Watch for **cerebral oedema** (especially in the young), hypokalaemia and hypoglycaemia

**Hyponatraemia**

- *Severe symptomatic (seizure, coma): 3% hypertonic saline 100–150 mL IV over 10–20 minutes, repeated up to three times until symptoms improve*
- **Do not raise serum sodium by more than 8–10 mEq/L in 24 hours** — osmotic demyelination is irreversible
- Asymptomatic: identify the cause, restrict fluid for SIADH, replace volume for hypovolaemic hyponatraemia

**Other reversible causes**

- *Adrenal crisis → Inj. Hydrocortisone 100 mg IV stat and fluid resuscitation — do not wait for the cortisol result*
- *Hypercalcaemia → saline rehydration then zoledronic acid;  Hypocalcaemia → calcium gluconate*
- *Uraemic encephalopathy → urgent nephrology referral for dialysis*

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- RBS \<54 mg/dL, or any hypoglycaemia after a sulfonylurea or long-acting insulin
- pH \<7.1, bicarbonate \<10, or a rising lactate
- Serum potassium \<3.3 or \>6.0 mEq/L; ECG changes of hyperkalaemia
- Serum sodium \<120 mEq/L, or a seizure attributable to sodium
- Serum osmolality \>320 mOsm/kg with a reduced sensorium
- Shock, anuria, or an identified septic focus
- Failure of the sensorium to improve after the metabolic abnormality has been corrected

## 4.4 Acute Weakness / Paralysis and Suspected Stroke

Issue 01 directed MRI for onset under 6 hours and CT for onset over 6 hours. **That is reversed and unsafe. A non-contrast CT head comes first in every suspected stroke, in every time window**, because the single question that must be answered before any treatment is whether the stroke is haemorrhagic. Time is brain: every minute of delay costs roughly 1.9 million neurons.

### Salient history to be taken

**Typical symptoms — BE-FAST**

- **B**alance — sudden loss of balance or coordination
- **E**yes — sudden loss or blurring of vision, diplopia
- **F**ace — asymmetry on smiling
- **A**rm — drift or weakness of one arm or leg
- **S**peech — slurring, or inability to speak or comprehend
- **T**ime — **the exact time the patient was last seen normal**

***“Time of onset” means the time last seen well, not the time the deficit was noticed.*** For a patient who woke with the deficit, onset is the time they went to sleep

**Details that decide treatment**

- Onset, progression, or resolution of symptoms; any fluctuation
- Associated headache (**sudden severe headache suggests haemorrhage or SAH**), vomiting, seizure, incontinence, neck pain
- Recent trauma, previous intracranial haemorrhage or surgery, recent major surgery or GI bleed
- **Anticoagulants, antiplatelets, thrombolysis contraindications** — go through the checklist explicitly
- Baseline functional status — **pre-stroke modified Rankin Scale**, because this decides thrombectomy eligibility

**Background**

- Diabetes, hypertension, dyslipidaemia, CAD, **atrial fibrillation, valvular and rheumatic heart disease**, prosthetic valve, CKD, malignancy
- Previous stroke or TIA; smoking; oral contraceptives; pregnancy or puerperium

**When it is not a stroke — ask deliberately**

- **Hypoglycaemia; Todd’s paresis after a seizure; hypokalaemic periodic paralysis** (ascending flaccid weakness, sparing bulbar muscles, often after a heavy carbohydrate meal or exertion — **not uncommon in this region**)
- **Guillain–Barré syndrome** (ascending symmetrical weakness over days, areflexia, preceding diarrhoea or respiratory illness)
- **Spinal cord compression** — back pain with a sensory level and bladder involvement; **in a cancer patient this is malignant cord compression and is an emergency**
- Migraine with aura, functional weakness, Bell’s palsy, myasthenic crisis, transverse myelitis, hypertensive encephalopathy

### Salient clinical examination

- **RBS first — hypoglycaemia is the commonest stroke mimic**
- Complete primary survey; **blood pressure in both arms**; temperature

**Neurological examination**

- **GCS and the three P’s** — **P**upils (size, symmetry, reaction), **P**ower in all four limbs, **P**lantar response
- Cranial nerves — **facial weakness with forehead sparing indicates an upper motor neurone lesion (stroke); forehead involvement indicates a lower motor neurone lesion (Bell’s palsy)**
- Speech — dysarthria versus expressive or receptive aphasia; neglect; gaze preference
- Tone, reflexes, sensory level, coordination, gait if safe
- **NIHSS score** — document it; it decides thrombectomy eligibility
- Meningeal signs; fundus

**Do not miss**

- **Cardiac auscultation for atrial fibrillation and murmurs; carotid bruit; all peripheral pulses; BP differential (dissection)**
- **Palpate the bladder and examine the perineum and anal tone** if a cord lesion is possible
- **HINTS plus examination** in the patient whose predominant symptom is vertigo — see the box in protocol 4.6

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS — before anything else**
- **NCCT head immediately — target within 25 minutes of arrival.** *This applies to every time window.* It answers the only question that matters first: is there blood?
- *CT angiography of the head and neck, and CT perfusion, where thrombectomy is a possibility or the window is extended*
- *MRI with diffusion-weighted imaging — more sensitive for small, posterior and early infarcts, but it does not replace the initial NCCT and must not delay thrombolysis*
- CBC with platelet count, **PT/INR and aPTT**, RFT, electrolytes, LFT
- **ECG — atrial fibrillation, recent MI**; troponin if indicated
- Chest X-ray, ABG — can be done after the CT
- *2D echocardiography where cardio-embolism, endocarditis or dissection is suspected*
- *MRI whole spine urgently if cord compression is suspected — this is a separate emergency pathway*
- *Serum potassium and ECG urgently if periodic paralysis is suspected*

*A doctor accompanies the patient to CT. The CT scanner is not a place to leave a stroke patient unmonitored.*

### Basic initial management

***Activate the STROKE ALERT at triage. Consultant Neurology / Physician informed before the CT, not after it.***

**Immediate**

- **IV cannula, preferably 20G or larger in the non-paretic arm**
- **If RBS \<70 mg/dL → Inj. 25% Dextrose 100 mL IV and reassess** — the deficit may resolve completely
- Oxygen only if SpO₂ \<94%; **head end at 30°**, neck neutral
- **Keep strictly NPO until a formal swallow screen is passed** — aspiration is the leading cause of early post-stroke death
- Normothermia and normoglycaemia (target RBS 140–180 mg/dL); treat fever

**Blood pressure**

- **Ischaemic stroke, not for thrombolysis:** permissive hypertension — **do not lower the BP unless it exceeds 220/120 mmHg**, or there is myocardial infarction, aortic dissection, heart failure or pre-eclampsia. Lower by no more than 15% in the first 24 hours
- **Ischaemic stroke, for thrombolysis:** **BP must be \<185/110 mmHg before the bolus and \<180/105 mmHg for 24 hours after**
- **Intracerebral haemorrhage:** target systolic **140 mmHg** (avoid dropping below 130)
- *Agents: Inj. Labetalol 10–20 mg IV over 1–2 minutes, repeated once, or a nicardipine infusion. Avoid sublingual nifedipine — the fall is precipitous and uncontrolled*

**Reperfusion — acute ischaemic stroke**

- *Intravenous thrombolysis within 4.5 hours of last-seen-well, after CT excludes haemorrhage and the checklist is completed:*
  - **Inj. Tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg IV (maximum 25 mg) as a single bolus over 5–10 seconds**, *or*
  - **Inj. Alteplase 0.9 mg/kg IV (maximum 90 mg)** — 10% as a bolus over 1 minute, the remainder over 60 minutes
  - **Target door-to-needle time ≤60 minutes**
- *Mechanical thrombectomy up to 24 hours in selected patients with large-vessel occlusion, based on NIHSS, ASPECTS, pre-stroke mRS and perfusion imaging — refer early to a thrombectomy-capable centre and do not wait to see if thrombolysis works*

*Antiplatelets and heparin are withheld for 24 hours after thrombolysis.*

**If not thrombolysed**

- *Tab. Aspirin 300 mg PO/NG stat (after CT excludes haemorrhage), then 75–150 mg daily; dual antiplatelet therapy for minor stroke or high-risk TIA on Neurology advice*
- *Statin; DVT prophylaxis with intermittent pneumatic compression; glycaemic and temperature control*

**Intracerebral haemorrhage**

- *BP control as above; reverse anticoagulation urgently — Vitamin K plus PCC for warfarin, specific agents or PCC for NOACs*
- *Neurosurgical referral for cerebellar haemorrhage, hydrocephalus, large lobar bleed or deteriorating GCS*

**Other causes of acute weakness**

- *Malignant cord compression → Inj. Dexamethasone 8–16 mg IV stat, urgent MRI whole spine, Radiation Oncology and Neurosurgery — hours matter for ambulation*\*
- *Guillain–Barré → serial single-breath count and vital capacity; intubate before the patient tires; IVIG or plasma exchange*
- *Hypokalaemic periodic paralysis → cautious oral or IV potassium with cardiac monitoring; identify thyrotoxicosis*

**Disposition**

- Admit every acute stroke to a monitored bed with regular neurological observation, swallow screening and early physiotherapy referral

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- BP \>185/110 mmHg in a candidate for thrombolysis; SpO₂ \<94%
- Unresponsive, difficult to arouse, or ongoing seizure
- Anisocoria, extensor posturing, deteriorating GCS — raised ICP or herniation
- Sudden severe (“worst ever”) headache with neck stiffness — subarachnoid haemorrhage
- Chest pain or BP differential between arms with a neurological deficit — **aortic dissection: do not thrombolyse**
- Back pain with a sensory level, bladder involvement or leg weakness — cord compression
- Ascending weakness with breathlessness or a falling single-breath count — impending respiratory failure
- Vertigo with inability to walk unaided, or with any other neurological sign

## 4.5 Oncology Patient with Altered Sensorium or Seizure

Issue 01 carried this protocol under a mislabelled heading. It is restored here as an oncology-specific neurological emergency, because in a cancer patient a change in sensorium has a distinct and largely treatable differential.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Type, site and stage of malignancy; known brain or leptomeningeal metastases**
- **Last chemotherapy — date, regimen, cycle**; last radiotherapy, especially cranial
- Immunotherapy or checkpoint inhibitor — date of last dose

**Onset and character**

- Onset of the change in sensorium; fluctuating or progressive; **sundowning**
- Seizure — focal or generalised, number, duration
- **Headache, worse in the morning or on bending, with projectile vomiting** — raised ICP
- Visual disturbance, diplopia, focal weakness, unsteadiness, decreased movements

**The reversible causes — ask for each**

- **Fever, cough, dysuria, diarrhoea, line site pain** — infection in the immunosuppressed
- **Constipation, polyuria, bone pain, thirst** — hypercalcaemia
- **Poor intake, vomiting, diuretics, excess free water** — hyponatraemia and SIADH
- **Opioid dose and last dose; recent dose escalation or rotation** — opioid neurotoxicity
- Benzodiazepines, antiemetics, steroids (**steroid psychosis**), antidepressants, anticholinergics
- **Ifosfamide** (encephalopathy), **methotrexate**, cytarabine, 5-FU, vincristine, tacrolimus, ciclosporin (**PRES**)
- Alcohol or benzodiazepine withdrawal; poor nutrition (**Wernicke’s**)

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **RBS and temperature in every patient**
- Level of consciousness, GCS, orientation, attention (**delirium is fluctuating inattention**)
- **Pupils, fundus for papilloedema, focal deficit, meningeal signs, cerebellar signs**
- Hydration status; postural blood pressure
- **Chemoport / PICC line site — erythema, tenderness, discharge**
- Mouth for mucositis and candidiasis; skin for rash, cellulitis, herpes zoster
- Chest, abdomen and perineum for a septic focus (*no per-rectal examination in neutropenia*)
- Bladder distension; faecal loading
- Signs of opioid toxicity — **pinpoint pupils, reduced respiratory rate, myoclonus**

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS; CBC with absolute neutrophil count**
- **Serum calcium corrected for albumin, and serum sodium** — the two most commonly missed reversible causes
- RFT, electrolytes including magnesium and phosphate, LFT, **serum ammonia** if liver disease
- ABG with lactate
- **Blood cultures (peripheral and from each line lumen) and urine culture if febrile — before antibiotics**
- ECG — QT interval, arrhythmia

**Imaging**

- **NCCT head urgently** — metastases, haemorrhage into a metastasis, hydrocephalus, midline shift
- *Contrast MRI brain — far more sensitive for metastases, leptomeningeal disease and PRES*
- *Lumbar puncture for suspected meningitis or leptomeningeal disease, after imaging and after correcting the platelet count and coagulation*
- *EEG for suspected non-convulsive status epilepticus*
- *Serum and urine osmolality if SIADH is suspected*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Medical Oncology and Neurology / Critical Care informed together.***

**Immediate**

- Airway protection, oxygen, IV access, monitored bed; **correct hypoglycaemia**
- **Inj. Thiamine 100 mg IV** where nutrition is poor

**Raised intracranial pressure from brain metastases**

- **Inj. Dexamethasone 16 mg IV stat, then 4–8 mg IV 6-hourly** — the mainstay; symptoms often improve within hours
- *Head end at 30°; Inj. Mannitol 20% 0.5–1 g/kg IV for herniation or rapid deterioration*
- *Urgent Radiation Oncology referral for whole-brain radiotherapy or stereotactic radiosurgery; Neurosurgery for a solitary resectable lesion or hydrocephalus*

**Seizure**

- Manage as protocol 4.2. **Inj. Levetiracetam is preferred** in this population because it does not interact with chemotherapy through the cytochrome P450 system, unlike phenytoin, carbamazepine and valproate

**Hypercalcaemia of malignancy**

- **Inj. Normal saline 200–300 mL/h** to restore urine output, then **Inj. Zoledronic acid 4 mg IV over 15 minutes** (dose-adjusted for renal function)
- *Calcitonin 4 IU/kg SC/IM 12-hourly for a rapid but transient effect; denosumab for bisphosphonate-refractory cases*

**Avoid loop diuretics until the patient is fully rehydrated; avoid thiazides entirely**

**Hyponatraemia / SIADH**

- *Fluid restriction; 3% saline only for seizures or coma, with the correction limited to 8–10 mEq/L in 24 hours*

**Infection**

- *Febrile with possible neutropenia → cultures then broad-spectrum antibiotics within 60 minutes (protocol 7.4)*
- *Suspected CNS infection → Inj. Ceftriaxone 2 g IV BD + Inj. Vancomycin, add Inj. Acyclovir 10 mg/kg IV TDS if encephalitis is possible*

**Drug-related**

- *Opioid neurotoxicity → hold or reduce the opioid, hydrate, rotate the opioid; Inj. Naloxone 0.04–0.1 mg IV titrated only if respiratory depression is present*
- *Ifosfamide encephalopathy → stop the drug; Inj. Methylene blue 50 mg IV 4-hourly on Oncology instruction*
- *PRES → control blood pressure, withdraw the offending agent*
- *Delirium with agitation → treat the cause first; Inj. Haloperidol 0.5–1 mg IV/IM in low dose if needed, avoiding benzodiazepines except in withdrawal*

**Goals of care**

*In advanced or refractory disease, discuss the ceiling of care with the treating oncologist and the family before escalating to intubation, and document the decision clearly.*

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Papilloedema, unequal pupils, projectile vomiting, Cushing response — raised ICP
- New focal deficit or seizure
- Corrected calcium \>12 mg/dL, or sodium \<125 mEq/L
- Fever with recent chemotherapy — febrile neutropenia
- Fever with neck stiffness — CNS infection in an immunosuppressed host
- Pinpoint pupils with a reduced respiratory rate — opioid toxicity
- Platelet count \<20,000 with a new neurological deficit — intracranial bleed
- Failure to regain consciousness after a seizure — non-convulsive status

## 4.6 Vertigo and Dizziness

Issue 01 treated all vertigo with prochlorperazine. **Around one in four patients presenting with isolated acute vertigo has a posterior circulation stroke**, and a vestibular sedative given to that patient masks the diagnosis and delays reperfusion. The examination — not the description of the symptom — separates peripheral from central.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Ask about timing and triggers, not about the quality of the sensation** — patients describe vertigo, presyncope and unsteadiness interchangeably

**Timing and trigger — the useful classification**

- **Episodic, seconds, triggered by head position** → BPPV
- **Episodic, minutes to hours, spontaneous, with hearing loss and tinnitus** → Ménière’s disease
- **Episodic, spontaneous, with headache and photophobia** → vestibular migraine
- **Continuous, days, spontaneous, of acute onset** → **vestibular neuritis or posterior circulation stroke** — this is the group in which the HINTS examination matters

**Associated symptoms — ask each one deliberately**

- Headache, especially occipital or neck pain (**vertebral artery dissection**)
