- **Diplopia, dysarthria, dysphagia, facial numbness, limb weakness or numbness, ataxia** — the “dangerous D’s”
- **New unilateral hearing loss** — may be peripheral, but also occurs with AICA territory infarction
- Chest pain, palpitation, sweating, breathlessness (**cardiac cause presenting as dizziness**)
- Ear discharge, recent upper respiratory infection, ear trauma or barotrauma
- Vomiting, unsteadiness, inability to walk

**Background**

- **Vascular risk factors — age \>60, hypertension, diabetes, smoking, atrial fibrillation, previous stroke**
- Recent neck manipulation or trauma; migraine history
- Medications — antihypertensives, aminoglycosides, furosemide, anticonvulsants, sedatives, alcohol
- Anaemia, dehydration, recent blood loss

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **RBS, temperature and postural blood pressure**
- **Blood pressure in both arms**; pulse for rate and rhythm

**The central examination — do all of it**

- **Nystagmus** — direction, whether it changes direction with gaze, and whether it is vertical or torsional
- **HINTS plus** examination in continuous vertigo of acute onset — see the box below
- Cranial nerves in full — especially V, VII, IX, X, and gaze
- Limb power, sensation, reflexes, plantars
- **Cerebellar signs** — finger–nose, dysdiadochokinesia, heel–shin, truncal ataxia
- **Gait — make the patient walk. Inability to stand or walk unaided is a central sign until proved otherwise**
- **Dix–Hallpike manoeuvre** for suspected BPPV — upbeating torsional nystagmus with latency and fatigability confirms it
- Ear examination — discharge, vesicles (**Ramsay Hunt**), tympanic membrane; **whispered voice or finger-rub hearing test in each ear**
- Cardiac auscultation; carotid bruit

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS; ECG** — arrhythmia, ischaemia, bradycardia, prolonged QT
- CBC (anaemia), RFT, electrolytes
- Postural blood pressure recorded formally

*In BPPV with a clearly positive Dix–Hallpike, a normal neurological examination and no vascular risk factors, no imaging is required.*

**When to image**

- **NCCT head** — first line if haemorrhage is suspected, but note that **CT misses the great majority of posterior fossa infarcts**
- ***MRI with diffusion-weighted imaging is the investigation of choice*** for suspected posterior circulation stroke — and even MRI can be falsely negative within the first 24–48 hours
- *MR or CT angiography of the vertebrobasilar system if dissection or basilar occlusion is suspected*

*A central HINTS examination is more sensitive than early MRI for stroke in acute vestibular syndrome — trust the examination and refer.*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Neurology / Physician informed if any central feature is present.***

**Immediate**

- IV access; **treat hypoglycaemia, dehydration and arrhythmia** if present
- Nurse in a quiet, dimly lit area; keep the head still; fall precautions

**If the picture is clearly peripheral**

- **Inj. Prochlorperazine (Stemetil) 12.5 mg IM** or **Tab. 5 mg PO**, *or* **Inj. Promethazine 25 mg IM**, *or* **Inj. Ondansetron 4 mg IV** for vomiting
- **Tab. Betahistine 8–16 mg TDS** for Ménière’s disease
- **IV fluids** if the patient is dehydrated from vomiting

***Vestibular sedatives are for 24–48 hours only.*** Prolonged use prevents central compensation and worsens chronic dizziness

- **BPPV → the treatment is a manoeuvre, not a drug: perform the Epley (canalith repositioning) manoeuvre**, which resolves symptoms in the majority at a single sitting
- *Vestibular neuritis → a short course of oral steroid may be considered on Consultant advice; early vestibular rehabilitation*

**If any central feature is present**

- ***Do not sedate. Treat as an acute stroke*** — activate the stroke pathway (protocol 4.4), obtain urgent imaging, and refer to Neurology within the thrombolysis window

*Basilar artery occlusion is the diagnosis most often missed here. It presents with vertigo, vomiting, dysarthria and fluctuating consciousness, and it is fatal without reperfusion*

**Disposition**

- Admit: any central feature, inability to walk, intractable vomiting, dehydration, new hearing loss with neurological signs, or an elderly patient with vascular risk factors and a first episode
- Discharge: clearly peripheral vertigo, able to walk, tolerating orally, with a short course of a vestibular sedative, **written return precautions**, and ENT or Neurology follow-up

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Inability to stand or walk without support**
- Any of the dangerous D’s — diplopia, dysarthria, dysphagia, dysmetria, dysaesthesia
- **Direction-changing, vertical or purely torsional nystagmus**
- Central HINTS examination — normal head impulse, direction-changing nystagmus, or skew deviation
- Severe headache or neck pain, especially occipital
- New unilateral hearing loss with neurological signs
- Age \>60 with vascular risk factors and a first episode of continuous vertigo
- Persistent vertigo not improving after 48 hours

### Separating central from peripheral vertigo at the bedside

#### HINTS plus — only valid in continuous, acute-onset vertigo with nystagmus

- **H – Head Impulse test:** turn the head rapidly 20° while the patient fixates on your nose
  - **Corrective saccade present (abnormal test)** → *peripheral* — reassuring
  - **No corrective saccade (normal test)** → ***central*** — dangerous
- **N – Nystagmus:** unidirectional, horizontal, suppressed by fixation → *peripheral*. **Direction-changing, vertical or torsional** → ***central***
- **TS – Test of Skew:** alternate cover test; **any vertical corrective movement** → ***central***
- **Plus – new hearing loss:** finger rub or whispered voice beside each ear; **new unilateral loss raises suspicion of AICA infarction**

***A single central finding on any of the four components makes this a stroke until proved otherwise.*** *The mnemonic INFARCT captures the dangerous pattern: Impulse Normal, Fast-phase Alternating, Refixation on Cover T*est

#### Peripheral causes

- **Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo** — seconds, positional, fatigable, positive Dix–Hallpike; treated with the **Epley manoeuvre**
- **Vestibular neuritis** — continuous for days, severe, no hearing loss, often after a viral illness; unidirectional nystagmus, abnormal head impulse
- **Labyrinthitis** — as above **with hearing loss**
- **Ménière’s disease** — episodic, minutes to hours, with fluctuating hearing loss, tinnitus and aural fullness
- **Ramsay Hunt syndrome** — vesicles in the ear canal with facial palsy and vertigo; needs acyclovir and steroid
- **Otitis media, cholesteatoma, perilymph fistula, acoustic neuroma, ototoxic drugs**

*Peripheral vertigo is usually more severe and more nauseating than central vertigo. Severity is not a reassuring feature.*

#### Central and systemic causes not to miss

- **Cerebellar or brainstem infarction** — PICA, AICA or basilar territory
- **Cerebellar haemorrhage** — sudden vertigo, vomiting, headache, ataxia; a neurosurgical emergency because of brainstem compression and hydrocephalus
- **Vertebral artery dissection** — young patient, neck pain, recent neck manipulation or trauma
- **Basilar artery occlusion** — fluctuating consciousness, cranial nerve signs, crossed deficits; very high mortality
- **Vestibular migraine**; multiple sclerosis; posterior fossa tumour

**Systemic mimics**

- **Orthostatic hypotension, arrhythmia, anaemia, hypoglycaemia, dehydration, drug side-effect, carbon monoxide, sepsis, cervical spondylosis**

*In an elderly patient, “dizziness” is often multifactorial — look for a fall risk, polypharmacy and postural drop before labelling it vertigo.*

## 4.7 Headache

Most headache in the ED is primary and benign. The work is to **identify the small number of secondary headaches that are lethal**, and to do it from the history and examination rather than by scanning everyone. **The single most useful question is: how quickly did it reach maximum intensity?**

### Salient history to be taken

**The critical features**

- **Onset — thunderclap?** Maximum intensity within one minute means **subarachnoid haemorrhage until excluded**
- **"The worst headache of my life", or a headache different in character from the usual one**
- **Age of first onset over 50** — new headache in this group is secondary until proved otherwise
- **Progressive over days to weeks**; **worse in the morning or on lying flat, coughing, straining or bending** — raised intracranial pressure
- **Woken from sleep by the headache**
- **Positional — worse on standing, relieved by lying** — low CSF pressure, after a lumbar puncture or spontaneous

**Accompanying features**

- Fever, neck stiffness, rash, photophobia — meningitis
- Visual change, jaw claudication, scalp tenderness — giant cell arteritis
- **Red painful eye with a fixed mid-dilated pupil and haloes** — acute angle-closure glaucoma
- Focal weakness, speech disturbance, seizure, personality change
- **Neck pain after trauma or manipulation, with Horner syndrome** — carotid or vertebral dissection

**Context**

- **Pregnancy or the puerperium** — pre-eclampsia, cerebral venous sinus thrombosis, pituitary apoplexy
- **Immunosuppression, HIV, malignancy** — abscess, cryptococcal meningitis, metastasis
- **Anticoagulation, recent head injury, alcohol excess** — subdural haematoma
- **Recent exposure to a coal or wood fire, or a generator; other household members also unwell** — carbon monoxide
- Analgesic frequency — medication-overuse headache; previous identical headaches and what relieved them

### Salient clinical examination

- **Full vital signs including temperature and blood pressure** — and blood pressure in both arms if dissection is possible
- **Fundoscopy — papilloedema, subhyaloid haemorrhage, retinal changes**
- Neck stiffness, Kernig and Brudzinski signs, jolt accentuation
- **Full neurological examination:** GCS, pupils, cranial nerves (especially III and VI), visual fields, power, coordination, gait, plantars
- **Palpate the temporal arteries** — tenderness, thickening, absent pulsation
- **Examine the eye:** acuity, pupil, red eye, and **palpate for a hard globe** if glaucoma is suspected
- Skin for a non-blanching rash; scalp and skull for injury; sinuses and temporomandibular joint for tenderness
- Cherry-red skin is unreliable; **pulse oximetry reads falsely normal in carbon monoxide poisoning**

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS; ECG if the patient is over 50 or has cardiovascular risk factors**
- **NCCT HEAD** for any red flag — and **immediately** for thunderclap headache
- **Lumbar puncture at 6–12 hours after onset if the CT is normal and subarachnoid haemorrhage is still suspected** — look for xanthochromia and a non-falling red cell count across tubes
- **ESR and CRP urgently if giant cell arteritis is suspected** — but do not wait for the result before starting steroid
- CBC, electrolytes, RFT, coagulation profile
- Blood cultures and then lumbar puncture if meningitis is suspected — **antibiotics first, before the CT and the LP**
- *CT or MR venography for suspected cerebral venous sinus thrombosis — pregnancy, puerperium, thrombophilia, dehydration, oral contraceptive use*
- *CT or MR angiography of the neck for suspected dissection*
- **Carboxyhaemoglobin on a blood gas** if carbon monoxide is possible
- **Intraocular pressure measurement** if angle-closure glaucoma is suspected — call Ophthalmology
- Urine protein and platelet count in the pregnant or recently delivered patient

### Basic initial management

**General**

- Quiet, dimly lit area; antiemetic; IV fluid if dehydrated or vomiting
- **Treat the pain while the workup proceeds — analgesia does not obscure the diagnosis**

**Primary headache — after red flags are excluded**

- **Migraine:** Inj. Metoclopramide 10 mg IV *or* Inj. Prochlorperazine 10 mg IV **plus** IV fluid; **Inj. Diclofenac 75 mg IM** or paracetamol; **Inj. Magnesium sulphate 1–2 g IV** in refractory cases; **Inj. Dexamethasone 8 mg IV once reduces early recurrence**. *Triptans if not already taken and not contraindicated*
- **Tension-type:** simple analgesia, reassurance, and advice on analgesic frequency
- **Cluster:** **high-flow oxygen 12–15 L/min by non-rebreathe mask for 15 minutes** — this is specific and effective; subcutaneous or intranasal triptan

*Avoid opioids in primary headache — they cause rebound and medication-overuse headache.*

**Secondary headache — specific action**

- **Subarachnoid haemorrhage:** BP control, analgesia, antiemetic, **Tab. Nimodipine 60 mg 4-hourly**, neurosurgical referral, ICU
- **Bacterial meningitis:** **antibiotics within the hour** — Ceftriaxone 2 g IV BD + Vancomycin + Dexamethasone with the first dose; Ampicillin if elderly, pregnant or immunosuppressed; Acyclovir if encephalitis is possible. **Do not wait for the LP**
- **Giant cell arteritis:** **Prednisolone 40–60 mg PO immediately** (IV methylprednisolone if there is visual loss) — **do not wait for the biopsy**; urgent rheumatology and ophthalmology referral
- **Raised intracranial pressure or a mass lesion:** head up 30°, treat seizures, dexamethasone for vasogenic oedema from tumour, neurosurgical referral
- **Cerebral venous sinus thrombosis:** anticoagulation, even in the presence of haemorrhagic infarction — with neurology
- **Acute angle-closure glaucoma:** **call Ophthalmology immediately**; topical beta-blocker, pilocarpine and apraclonidine, plus acetazolamide; lie the patient supine
- **Carbon monoxide poisoning:** **100% oxygen by non-rebreathe mask**, and check the other people in the house
- **Pre-eclampsia:** magnesium sulphate and BP control (protocol 6.1)

**Disposition**

- **Admit** any secondary headache, any abnormal neurology, or an incomplete workup
- **Discharge** primary headache once treated, with a written plan, an analgesic-use limit, and **specific instructions to return for a sudden severe headache, fever, weakness or visual change**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Thunderclap — maximum intensity within one minute**
- **Fever with neck stiffness, or with a non-blanching rash**
- **New headache over the age of 50**, or a headache different from the usual pattern
- **Focal neurological deficit, seizure, papilloedema or a falling GCS**
- **Worse on lying flat, coughing or straining; or waking the patient from sleep**
- **Headache in pregnancy or the puerperium**
- **Headache in an immunosuppressed, HIV-positive or cancer patient**
- **Headache on anticoagulation, or after head injury**
- **Red painful eye with visual blurring and haloes**
- **Jaw claudication, scalp tenderness or visual loss over the age of 50**
- **Other household members with the same symptoms** — carbon monoxide

### Quick guide — thunderclap headache, and the pattern of the primary headaches

#### Thunderclap headache — the differential

- **Subarachnoid haemorrhage** — the diagnosis to exclude first. CT sensitivity is very high within 6 hours and falls thereafter
- **Cerebral venous sinus thrombosis** — pregnancy, puerperium, dehydration, thrombophilia, oral contraceptive
- **Arterial dissection** — neck pain, Horner syndrome, recent trauma or manipulation
- **Reversible cerebral vasoconstriction syndrome** — recurrent thunderclap headaches, often postpartum or drug-related
- **Pituitary apoplexy** — headache with visual field loss and ophthalmoplegia
- **Hypertensive emergency or PRES**
- **Colloid cyst of the third ventricle** — positional headache with drop attacks
- **Spontaneous intracranial hypotension** — postural, relieved by lying flat

*A normal CT does not close the case. If the story is thunderclap, the lumbar puncture or angiography follows.*

#### Recognising the primary headaches

- **Migraine:** unilateral, throbbing, moderate to severe, 4–72 hours, with nausea and photophobia or phonophobia, **worse with movement**; aura in a third
- **Tension-type:** bilateral, pressing or tight, mild to moderate, **not worsened by routine activity**, no vomiting
- **Cluster:** strictly unilateral, orbital or temporal, excruciating, 15–180 minutes, **with ipsilateral lacrimation, nasal congestion, ptosis or miosis**; the patient is restless and paces — unlike the migraineur, who lies still
- **Medication-overuse:** daily or near-daily headache in a patient taking analgesia on more than 10–15 days a month
- **Trigeminal neuralgia:** brief electric-shock pains in a trigeminal distribution, triggered by touch, chewing or cold air

*A primary headache diagnosis in the ED is a diagnosis of exclusion in anyone presenting for the first time.*

# 5.0 Poisoning, Envenomation and Animal Bite

Punjab has one of the highest rates of pesticide self-poisoning in India. **Every case of poisoning is a Medico-Legal Case.** Complete the MLC and inform the police — but never let paperwork delay atropinisation, decontamination or the airway.

## 5.1 Poisoning — General Approach

The general approach is the same regardless of the agent: **resuscitate, identify, decontaminate, give the antidote, enhance elimination, and provide supportive care.** Specific agents follow in the boxes below.

### Salient history to be taken

**From the patient if conscious, and always from a witness, the container and the attendants**

- **Name of the substance** — ask for the **container, bottle, strip, wrapper or a photograph of it**. Send an attendant home for it
- **Amount** — number of tablets, volume swallowed, concentration
- **Time of ingestion** — the single most important number for decontamination and for antidote timing
- **Route** — ingestion, inhalation, dermal, injection, ocular
- **Intent** — suicidal, accidental, homicidal, occupational, recreational. Ask gently and directly
- Co-ingestants, **especially alcohol**; multiple drug ingestion is common
- Vomiting since ingestion; any treatment or lavage already given elsewhere

**Background**

- Psychiatric illness, previous attempts, ongoing stressors, availability of the agent at home or work
- Occupation — **farming, spraying, pesticide retail, factory work**
- Regular medication (which may be the agent), co-morbidity, pregnancy

*Where history and clinical presentation disagree, trust the clinical presentation. Patients and families frequently misidentify the agent.*

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **RBS, temperature and full set of vitals including respiratory rate**

**The toxidrome examination**

- **Pupils** — pinpoint (opioid, organophosphate, clonidine, pontine) versus dilated (anticholinergic, sympathomimetic, TCA)
- **Skin** — dry and flushed (anticholinergic) versus diaphoretic (cholinergic, sympathomimetic); cyanosis; blistering
- **Mucous membranes** — dry versus salivating; corrosive burns in the mouth and oropharynx
- **Bowel sounds** — absent (anticholinergic) versus hyperactive with incontinence (cholinergic)
- **Bladder** — distended (anticholinergic)
- **Neuromuscular** — fasciculation, tremor, rigidity, clonus, myoclonus, seizure
- **Odour** — kerosene, garlic (organophosphate, phosphide), bitter almond (cyanide), acetone, alcohol
- Respiratory rate and pattern; **bronchorrhoea and wheeze**
- Injection marks, corrosive skin burns, contaminated clothing
- Signs of aspiration; GCS and airway reflexes

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS; ECG — QRS width, QT interval, rhythm, blocks** (a QRS \>100 ms suggests sodium-channel blockade; a QT \>500 ms risks torsades)
- **ABG with lactate, electrolytes and anion gap**; **serum osmolality with calculated osmolar gap** in suspected toxic alcohol
- CBC, RFT, electrolytes, **LFT, PT/INR**, RBS, serum calcium and magnesium
- **Serum cholinesterase (plasma pseudocholinesterase and RBC acetylcholinesterase)** in suspected organophosphate — useful for confirmation and prognosis, **but do not wait for the result to start atropine**
- Serum paracetamol at 4 hours post-ingestion; serum salicylate; serum lithium, digoxin, phenytoin, valproate as indicated
- Urine toxicology screen; blood alcohol
- Urine output, myoglobin and CK where rhabdomyolysis is possible
- Chest X-ray — aspiration, pulmonary oedema, hydrocarbon pneumonitis
- *Abdominal X-ray for radio-opaque tablets — iron, heavy metals, enteric-coated preparations, body packers*
- *Endoscopy within 12–24 hours for corrosive ingestion — Gastroenterology*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Critical Care informed for every significant poisoning. MLC completed and police informed. Suicide precautions and one-to-one observation from the outset.***

**1. Resuscitate**

- **Airway** — the commonest cause of death is airway loss and aspiration. *Intubate for GCS ≤8, absent gag, bronchorrhoea that cannot be cleared, or respiratory failure*
- Left lateral position with the head lower than the feet if not intubated; suction at the bedside
- **Breathing** — oxygen, ventilatory support as needed
- **Circulation** — IV access; **Inj. Normal saline** to maintain **SBP \>90 mmHg and urine output \>0.5 mL/kg/h**; vasopressor if fluid-unresponsive
- **Disability** — correct hypoglycaemia; **Inj. Thiamine 100 mg IV**; treat seizures with a benzodiazepine
- **Exposure** — **remove all contaminated clothing (double-bag it), wash the skin and hair with copious soap and water**, irrigate eyes with saline for 15 minutes. **Staff must wear gloves, apron and eye protection**

**2. Identify**

- Match the toxidrome to the history; use the container, the WHO colour code on pesticide labels, and photographs of common agents
- Consult the **National Poisons Information Centre, AIIMS New Delhi: 1800-116-117 (24 hours)**

**3. Decontaminate**

- **Activated charcoal 1 g/kg (50 g in adults) orally or by NG tube — within 1 hour of ingestion, in a patient with a protected airway.** Most useful for tablets

*Charcoal does not bind: iron, lithium, alcohols, hydrocarbons, corrosives, potassium. It is contraindicated in corrosive ingestion, hydrocarbon ingestion, ileus, and an unprotected airway*

- **Gastric lavage — rarely indicated.** Consider only if: a large amount of a highly toxic agent, presentation **within 1–2 hours**, and the patient is conscious and cooperative or already intubated. **Use aliquots of 200–300 mL** — larger volumes push the poison into the intestine

***Never lavage a corrosive ingestion or a hydrocarbon ingestion.*** Never lavage an unprotected airway

- *Whole-bowel irrigation with polyethylene glycol for iron, lithium, sustained-release preparations and body packers*

**4. Antidote**

- See the antidote table in Section 11 and the agent-specific boxes below

**5. Enhance elimination**

- *Urinary alkalinisation with sodium bicarbonate for salicylate and phenobarbitone*
- *Multi-dose activated charcoal for carbamazepine, dapsone, phenobarbitone, quinine, theophylline*
- *Haemodialysis for methanol, ethylene glycol, salicylate, lithium, valproate, metformin-associated lactic acidosis, theophylline*

**6. Supportive care and psychiatric assessment**

- Continuous monitoring; hourly vitals, pupils and GCS; urinary catheter with hourly output; DVT and pressure-area care
- **Every patient with a deliberate ingestion needs a psychiatric assessment before discharge, and constant supervision until then.** Remove access to further means; counsel the family; document the risk assessment and the follow-up plan

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- GCS ≤8, absent gag, or an inability to clear secretions
- Respiratory rate \<10 or \>30; bronchorrhoea; SpO₂ \<92%
- Hypotension unresponsive to fluid; arrhythmia; **QRS \>100 ms or QT \>500 ms**
- Seizure, or a rising temperature with rigidity
- Severe metabolic acidosis, lactate \>4 mmol/L, or a raised anion or osmolar gap
- **Aluminium phosphide (celphos), paraquat, or a corrosive ingestion** — all have specific high-mortality pathways
- Corrosive burns in the oropharynx, drooling, stridor or hoarseness
- A patient who denies ingestion but has an unexplained toxidrome

### Organophosphate and carbamate poisoning — the commonest severe poisoning in this region

#### Recognition and classification

**Cholinergic toxidrome**

- **DUMBBELS** — **D**iarrhoea, **U**rination, **M**iosis, **B**ronchorrhoea, **B**radycardia, **E**mesis, **L**acrimation, **S**alivation
- Plus nicotinic effects — **fasciculation, weakness, tachycardia, hypertension**
- Plus central effects — confusion, seizure, coma, respiratory depression
- **Bronchorrhoea and bronchospasm are what kill** — the patient drowns in secretions

**Identify the compound**

- Identify the pesticide **at admission**, from history, the container, the WHO colour code, and by showing the patient photographs of common products
- **Monocrotophos, dimethoate, phorate and methomyl produce early, rapid respiratory paralysis, sometimes within hours** — these patients need early intubation

**Classes commonly encountered**

- **Organophosphate:** monocrotophos, chlorpyrifos, quinalphos, dichlorvos, phorate, dimethoate, acephate, phosphamidon, triazophos, profenofos
  - *Diethyl compounds: quinalphos, chlorpyrifos, triazophos, phorate*
  - *Dimethyl compounds: monocrotophos, dichlorvos, acephate, phosphamidon*
- **Carbamate:** methomyl, carbaryl — **self-limiting, reversible inhibition; atropine only, do not give pralidoxime**
- **Organochlorine:** endosulfan, endrin — seizures predominate
- **Pyrethroid:** cypermethrin, deltamethrin, cyhalothrin — usually mild; paraesthesia, vomiting; supportive care only
- **Neonicotinoid:** imidacloprid, acetamiprid — usually mild

**The atropine test**

- If unsure whether the patient has taken an organophosphate, give **Inj. Atropine 0.6–1 mg IV**. If the pulse rises by 25/min or the skin flushes, toxicity is mild or absent

#### Atropine — dose, titration and the three-point check

**Immediate measures**

- Assess and record the **15-point GCS**; measure pulse and blood pressure; **auscultate the lungs**
- Left lateral position, head lower than the feet; **start oxygen**
- **Intubate early if there is respiratory distress or copious secretions**
- **Start atropine quickly** — it is the drug that dries the bronchial tree
- Infusion of 0.9% normal saline; aim for **SBP \>80 mmHg and urine output \>30 mL/h**

**Bolus and doubling**

- **Inj. Atropine 1.8–3 mg (3–5 mL) IV bolus**
- **Check three things after 5 minutes: pulse, blood pressure and chest crackles**
- **Targets: heart rate \>80/min, SBP \>80 mmHg, and a clear chest** (atropine will not dry focal areas of aspiration)
- **Double the dose every 5 minutes until the targets are met.** Review the patient every 5 minutes
- Once the parameters begin to improve, repeat the last dose or a smaller one. When improvement is sustained at 5 minutes, move to an infusion
- **Ignore the pupils** — they take time to dilate and are not a guide to dosing

**Infusion**

- Calculate the **total dose required for atropinisation**, and start an **hourly infusion at 20% of that total dose**
- Most patients need no more than **3–5 mg per hour**
- Use the three-point check to **reduce the infusion rate by 20% every 4 hours** once stable

**Cautions**

- Do not use oral secretions to guide therapy in a patient who is intubated, unconscious, has an oropharyngeal airway, or has intermediate syndrome
- **Ignore sweating** when adjusting the dose
- A stable patient with a clear chest and a heart rate just below target does not need more atropine
- **Bronchorrhoea is the key sign for titration once the patient is stable**
- **Atropine toxicity = absent bowel sounds + fever + confusion** (with a distended bladder and dilated pupils). **Stop the infusion for 60 minutes**, then restart at 80% of the previous rate once the temperature settles and the patient is calm

#### Pralidoxime, sedation, and the intermediate syndrome

**Pralidoxime (PAM)**

- **Only for organophosphate poisoning. Never for carbamate poisoning**
- **Bolus: 30–45 mg/kg IV over 30 minutes** (about 2 g for a 50 kg adult). **Maintenance: continuous infusion of 8–12 mg/kg/h** (about 500–600 mg/h)
- **Must be given as an infusion.** A rapid bolus causes vomiting, hypertension, arrhythmia or cardiac arrest
- **Continue until atropine is no longer required**
- **Never give pralidoxime without atropine**
- Effectiveness varies with the compound: it works for up to **12 hours in dimethyl** compounds and up to **5 days in diethyl** compounds, and is **ineffective in profenofos**

*The evidence for pralidoxime is genuinely uncertain and trials have not shown clear mortality benefit. It remains standard practice in India; use it, but do not allow it to substitute for adequate atropinisation and ventilation, which are what actually save lives.*

**Sedation and seizures**

- **Inj. Diazepam 10 mg slow IV, repeated as necessary**, up to 30–40 mg in 24 hours; infusion for status epilepticus
- *Propofol or midazolam if seizures are not controlled by diazepam*

***Do not use phenytoin, haloperidol or atracurium***

**Decontamination**

- Wash the skin and hair thoroughly; remove and bag the clothing; protect staff
- *Consider gastric lavage only within 1–2 hours of a large ingestion, with a protected airway, using aliquots of 200–300 mL*
- **Do not use activated charcoal in organophosphate poisoning** — there is no evidence of benefit and it adds aspiration risk

**Intermediate syndrome**

- Appears **12 to 96 hours after exposure**, after the acute cholinergic phase has settled
- **Early signs: action tremor and pharyngeal weakness** — difficulty swallowing, pooling of secretions
- Progresses to inability to flex the neck, loss of deep tendon reflexes, cranial neuropathies, proximal muscle weakness and **respiratory muscle paralysis**
- Not every patient progresses to needing ventilation, but **those with tremor and pharyngeal weakness are at high risk** — watch them in a monitored bed
- Treatment is **entirely supportive**; ventilate if respiratory muscles fail. **Do not give atropine unless signs of cholinergic excess are present**

*An altered sensorium persisting beyond 12 hours, with no fever, no absent bowel sounds and no dilated pupils, should be attributed to the pesticide and may be an early sign of intermediate syndrome. Alcohol-intoxicated patients settle within 12 hours.*

**Weaning**

- **Assess respiratory muscle performance before weaning.** Thresholds: PaCO₂ \<50 mmHg, spontaneous minute ventilation \<10–15 L/min, tidal volume \>5 mL/kg, respiratory rate 6–35/min
- Wean by gradually reducing pressure support; extubate when breathing comfortably with a tidal volume \>5 mL/kg at a pressure support of 3–5 cm H₂O

*The commonest cause of death in organophosphate poisoning is respiratory failure and the complications of managing it.*

### Other agents commonly seen in this region — recognition and specific treatment

#### Aluminium phosphide (celphos, “rice tablet”)

***The single most lethal poisoning in Punjab. Mortality is 60–80%. There is no antidote.***

- A tablet releases **phosphine gas** on contact with moisture, which blocks mitochondrial cytochrome oxidase

**Recognition**

- **Garlicky odour** of the breath and vomitus; vomiting, epigastric pain
- **Profound, refractory hypotension and shock** — the hallmark
- Severe **metabolic acidosis**; myocarditis, arrhythmia, pulmonary oedema, ARDS, hepatic and renal failure
- A **silver nitrate–impregnated paper test** on gastric aspirate turns black if phosphine is present

**Management — supportive, aggressive and early**

- **Do not give water or anything that generates more gas**
- Airway protection; high-flow oxygen; **ICU admission for every case**
- *Gastric lavage with coconut oil and potassium permanganate 1:10,000 is widely practised in India, on the basis that oil retards phosphine release — evidence is weak but it is standard local practice; Consultant decision*
- **Aggressive fluid resuscitation with normal saline; early noradrenaline** — do not delay the vasopressor
- **Inj. Sodium bicarbonate** for severe acidosis
- **Inj. Magnesium sulphate 1 g IV** — widely used for arrhythmia; Consultant decision
- *Intravenous lipid emulsion, trimetazidine and N-acetylcysteine have been used with uncertain benefit*
- Continuous ECG; treat arrhythmia; serial ABG, electrolytes, RFT, LFT
- **Counsel the family honestly and early about the prognosis**

#### Snake bite — the Indian approach

**Recognise envenomation, not just the bite**

- **Neurotoxic** (cobra, krait): ptosis, ophthalmoplegia, diplopia, dysphagia, **descending paralysis, respiratory failure**. **Krait bites are often painless and present in the early morning with abdominal pain and paralysis**
- **Haemotoxic** (Russell’s viper, saw-scaled viper): local swelling, blistering, bleeding from the site and gums, haematuria, **acute kidney injury**
- **20-minute whole blood clotting test (20WBCT)** — 2 mL of blood in a clean, dry glass tube left undisturbed for 20 minutes; **failure to clot indicates envenomation**. Repeat 6-hourly
- **Single-breath count \<10** indicates significant neuromuscular weakness

**Management**

- **Immobilise the limb in a splint at heart level; remove rings and bangles; do not apply a tourniquet, do not incise, do not suck, do not apply ice**
- **Polyvalent Anti-Snake Venom only if there are signs of envenomation** — **10 vials in 100–200 mL normal saline IV over 1 hour**; repeat 10 vials 6-hourly until clotting normalises or neurological signs improve
- **Keep adrenaline drawn up at the bedside** — anaphylaxis to ASV is common; treat with **Inj. Adrenaline 0.5 mg IM** and continue the ASV once controlled
- **Inj. Neostigmine 0.5–2.5 mg IV with Inj. Atropine 0.6 mg IV** for neurotoxic envenomation — assess the response with the ptosis and single-breath count
- **Intubate early** for bulbar weakness; ventilate as required — krait envenomation may need days of ventilation but recovery is usually complete
- Tetanus prophylaxis; antibiotics only for established local infection; **watch for compartment syndrome**
- Monitor urine output, RFT and coagulation; dialysis for AKI

#### Other important agents

**Corrosive (acid or alkali) ingestion**

- **No lavage, no charcoal, no neutralisation, no induced vomiting, no nasogastric tube blindly**
- Look for **airway compromise — hoarseness, stridor, drooling**; intubate early, ideally over a bronchoscope
- Look for **perforation — guarding, rigidity, subcutaneous emphysema, haematemesis**
- Keep NPO, IV fluids, analgesia, PPI; erect chest X-ray and CT; **endoscopy within 12–24 hours**; Gastroenterology and Surgery referral

**Paracetamol overdose**

- Often asymptomatic for the first 24 hours — **do not be reassured by a well-looking patient**
- **Serum paracetamol at 4 hours** post-ingestion, plotted on the Rumack–Matthew nomogram
- **Inj. N-acetylcysteine**: 150 mg/kg in 200 mL 5% dextrose over 1 hour, then 50 mg/kg in 500 mL over 4 hours, then 100 mg/kg in 1 L over 16 hours. **Start empirically if the ingestion is staggered, the timing is unknown, or presentation is late**

**Opioid overdose**

- Pinpoint pupils, respiratory depression, coma. **Inj. Naloxone 0.4–2 mg IV**, repeated and titrated to respiratory rate, not to consciousness; may need an infusion because naloxone is shorter-acting than most opioids

**Benzodiazepine overdose**

- Supportive care and airway protection. ***Flumazenil is generally avoided*** — it precipitates seizures in mixed ingestion and in the benzodiazepine-dependent

**Tricyclic antidepressant overdose**

- Anticholinergic toxidrome, **wide QRS, seizures, hypotension, arrhythmia**
- **Inj. Sodium bicarbonate 1–2 mEq/kg IV bolus if QRS \>100 ms**, repeated to achieve a pH of 7.45–7.55. Benzodiazepines for seizures. **Avoid class Ia, Ic and III antiarrhythmics and physostigmine**

**Methanol and ethylene glycol**

- **High anion-gap metabolic acidosis with a raised osmolar gap**; visual disturbance in methanol
- **Ethanol or fomepizole; sodium bicarbonate; folinic acid (methanol) or thiamine and pyridoxine (ethylene glycol); urgent haemodialysis**

**Rodenticide (superwarfarin)**

- Delayed coagulopathy over days. Check INR at presentation and again at 24–48 hours. **Vitamin K 10–25 mg orally, often for weeks**; FFP or PCC only for active bleeding

## 5.2 Animal Bite, Scratch and Rabies Prophylaxis

Rabies is **almost universally fatal once symptomatic and almost completely preventable** by correct post-exposure prophylaxis. The two things that matter most are done in the first hour: **thorough wound washing, and correct categorisation of the exposure**. For snake bite, see protocol 5.1.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Animal involved:** dog, cat, monkey, bat, mongoose, jackal, cattle, rodent, pig, or human
- **Provoked or unprovoked bite** — an unprovoked attack raises the suspicion of rabies
- **Is the animal available for observation?** Vaccination status, and whether it is a stray or a pet
- **Time since the bite** — and what has already been applied to the wound
- **Site of the bite** — head, neck, face, hand and genitalia carry the highest risk because of dense innervation and short distance to the CNS
- **Previous rabies vaccination**, with dates and the number of doses — this changes the whole regimen
- Tetanus immunisation status
- Comorbidity, immunosuppression, asplenia, diabetes, cirrhosis; anticoagulation

*Ask specifically about bat exposure: a bite or scratch may be unnoticed, and any direct contact with a bat is treated as Category III.*

### Salient clinical examination

- **Categorise the exposure — this determines everything that follows** (see the guide below)
- Describe every wound: site, number, depth, contamination, devitalised tissue
- **Assess for deep structure injury** — tendon, joint capsule, bone, nerve, vessel
- **Hand bites: examine in the position of injury** (a clenched-fist injury over the knuckle may have penetrated the joint)
- Distal neurovascular examination; range of movement of every joint distal to the wound
- Signs of established infection: erythema, swelling, purulent discharge, lymphangitis, regional lymphadenopathy, fever

*A clenched-fist injury over the metacarpophalangeal joint is a human bite until proved otherwise, and is a surgical problem.*

### Point-of-care investigations

- **No investigation is required to decide on rabies prophylaxis — do not delay it for any test**
- **X-ray** if there is a possibility of a retained tooth, a fracture, or joint penetration
- CBC, CRP, blood cultures and RFT if the patient is systemically unwell
- Wound swab for culture **only if the wound is already infected** — not from a fresh bite
- RBS; HIV and hepatitis B status in human bites, for both persons where possible

*Rabies cannot be excluded by any test available in the ED. The decision is clinical and is based on the category of exposure.*

### Basic initial management

**1. Wound care — the first and most important step**

- **WASH THE WOUND WITH SOAP AND RUNNING WATER FOR AT LEAST 15 MINUTES.** This alone substantially reduces the risk of rabies
- Then apply a virucidal agent — povidone-iodine or 70% alcohol
- **Do NOT suture a fresh bite wound if it can be avoided.** If closure is unavoidable for cosmetic reasons on the face, it is done loosely, after thorough washing, and **only after rabies immunoglobulin has been infiltrated**
- **Do not cauterise, and do not apply chilli, turmeric, oil, lime or any traditional application** — ask, because it is common, and wash it off
- Debride devitalised tissue; leave the wound open with a simple dressing; elevate a bitten limb

**2. Rabies post-exposure prophylaxis — by category**

- **Category I** — touching or feeding an animal, licks on intact skin: **no prophylaxis; wash the area**
- **Category II** — nibbling of uncovered skin, minor scratches or abrasions **without bleeding**: **wound care + VACCINE**
- **Category III** — single or multiple transdermal bites or scratches, licks on broken skin, contamination of mucous membrane with saliva, **any bat exposure**: **wound care + VACCINE + RABIES IMMUNOGLOBULIN**

**Vaccine schedules (cell-culture vaccine)**

- **Intramuscular, Essen: one dose on days 0, 3, 7 and 14** (plus day 28 if immunocompromised), given in the deltoid — **never in the gluteal region**; anterolateral thigh in a small child
- *Intradermal (Updated Thai Red Cross), where the centre is equipped for it: 0.1 mL at two sites on days 0, 3, 7 and 28*
- **Previously fully vaccinated: two doses only, on days 0 and 3 — and NO immunoglobulin**

**Rabies immunoglobulin — Category III only**

- **Human RIG 20 IU/kg, or Equine RIG 40 IU/kg** — **infiltrate as much as anatomically possible into and around every wound**
- **The infiltration is the point.** Any remainder may be given intramuscularly at a site distant from the vaccine
- **Give it on day 0. If it was missed, it can still be given up to day 7 of the vaccine course — after that it is not given**
- *Equine RIG requires a test dose as per the product insert; keep adrenaline at the bedside*

***There is no contraindication to rabies prophylaxis — not pregnancy, not infancy, not immunosuppression. Never withhold it.***

**3. Tetanus and antibiotics**

- **Tetanus toxoid ± immunoglobulin** as per immunisation status
- **Prophylactic antibiotics for: all cat bites, all human bites, all hand and foot bites, bites near a joint or prosthesis, deep puncture wounds, crush injury, wounds needing closure, and the immunosuppressed, asplenic, diabetic or cirrhotic patient**
- **Tab. Amoxicillin–clavulanate 625 mg PO TDS for 3–5 days** (prophylaxis) or 5–7 days (established infection)
- *Penicillin allergy: doxycycline plus metronidazole, or a fluoroquinolone plus clindamycin*
- **Established or severe infection: Inj. Amoxicillin–clavulanate 1.2 g IV TDS or Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam**, admit, elevate and mark the erythema

**4. Disposition**

- **Admit:** established infection with systemic features, hand infection, joint or tendon involvement, failed outpatient therapy, or an immunosuppressed patient
- **Refer to surgery or orthopaedics:** clenched-fist injury, joint penetration, tendon or nerve injury, extensive tissue loss, or any facial wound needing repair
- **Discharge:** written instructions, the vaccine schedule with dates, wound care advice, and clear return criteria
- **Record the vaccination schedule on a card given to the patient**, and register the case with the hospital’s animal-bite record

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Category III exposure** — immunoglobulin is required and is frequently omitted
- **Bite to the face, head, neck or hand** — short incubation; treat as high risk
- **Any bat exposure**, including a possible unnoticed contact during sleep
- **Unprovoked bite by a stray or a wild animal**, or an animal that dies or behaves strangely within 10 days
- **Clenched-fist injury over the knuckle** — septic arthritis of the metacarpophalangeal joint follows if it is treated as a simple laceration
- **Rapidly spreading cellulitis within hours of a cat bite** — *Pasteurella multocida*
- **Bite in an asplenic patient** — *Capnocytophaga* sepsis can be fulminant
- **Established hand infection with pain on passive extension of a finger** — flexor tenosynovitis, a surgical emergency
- **Established rabies** — hydrophobia, aerophobia, agitation, autonomic instability; isolate, treat palliatively, counsel the family, and protect the staff

### Quick guide — categorising the exposure, and organisms by animal

#### WHO exposure categories — memorise these

**Category I — NO prophylaxis**

- Touching or feeding animals \| Licks on **intact** skin
- **Action:** wash the area. Nothing further

**Category II — VACCINE**

- Nibbling of uncovered skin \| Minor scratches or abrasions **without bleeding**
- **Action:** wash 15 minutes + vaccine (days 0, 3, 7, 14)

**Category III — VACCINE + IMMUNOGLOBULIN**

- Single or multiple **transdermal** bites or scratches \| Licks on **broken** skin \| Saliva contamination of mucous membrane \| **Any bat exposure**
- **Action:** wash 15 minutes + vaccine + RIG infiltrated into the wound

*If in doubt between II and III, treat as III. The cost of over-treating is money; the cost of under-treating is death.*

*Prophylaxis may be started at any interval after exposure — late is far better than never.*

#### Organisms, and the bites that behave badly

- **Dog** — *Pasteurella*, *Staphylococcus*, *Streptococcus*, anaerobes, *Capnocytophaga*; crush injury with a lower infection rate but more tissue damage
- **Cat** — **deep puncture wounds with a high infection rate (30–50%)**; *Pasteurella multocida* causes cellulitis within hours. Also cat-scratch disease (*Bartonella*)
- **Human** — *Eikenella corrodens*, oral streptococci and anaerobes; **the highest infection rate of all bites**; assess hepatitis B, hepatitis C and HIV risk for both parties
- **Monkey** — rabies prophylaxis as for dog; *B virus (Herpesvirus simiae)* is a consideration in macaque exposures — wash thoroughly and seek specialist advice
- **Rodent and squirrel** — rabies prophylaxis is generally not indicated in India for rats and mice, but wound care and tetanus still apply
- **Cattle, pig, horse** — rabies prophylaxis if the animal is suspected; usually heavily contaminated wounds

*Never close a cat bite or a human hand bite primarily.*

# 6.0 Obstetric and Gynaecological Presentations

Two rules apply throughout. **Every woman of reproductive age with abdominal pain, bleeding, collapse or vomiting gets a urine pregnancy test** — no exceptions, whatever the stated history. And **ectopic pregnancy is excluded before any other diagnosis is accepted** in early pregnancy with pain or bleeding.

## 6.1 Patient in Labour, and the Obstetric Emergency

This hospital is not a designated obstetric unit. The role of the ED is to **recognise the stage of labour, identify the obstetric emergency, stabilise, and arrange immediate obstetric review or safe transfer** — while being prepared to conduct an imminent delivery if there is no time to move the patient.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Gravida, para, living, abortions**; period of gestation by LMP and by scan
- **Expected date of delivery**; booked or unbooked; place of antenatal care

**Current episode**

- Onset, frequency, duration and intensity of pains
- **Leaking per vaginam** — time of onset, colour (**meconium staining**), quantity
- **Bleeding per vaginam** — amount, colour, clots, with or without pain
- **Fetal movements — present, reduced or absent**
- Urge to bear down or to pass stool (**suggests the second stage**)

**Risk factors that change the pathway**

- **Antepartum haemorrhage**: painless bright bleeding (**placenta praevia — do not perform a vaginal examination**) versus painful bleeding with a tense tender uterus (**abruptio placentae**)
- **Headache, visual disturbance, epigastric pain, vomiting, swelling** — pre-eclampsia
- Previous caesarean section or uterine surgery; multiple pregnancy; malpresentation; post-term
- Hypertension, diabetes, anaemia, heart disease, thyroid disease, epilepsy
- Rh negative status; blood group; previous obstetric complications
- Current medications; any prostaglandin or oxytocic already given elsewhere

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **blood pressure recorded carefully in every pregnant woman**
- Pallor, oedema, hydration, temperature

**Obstetric examination**

- Fundal height, lie, presentation, **liquor volume, estimated fetal weight**
- **Uterine contractions** — frequency, duration, intensity; **a tense, tender, woody uterus suggests abruption**
- **Fetal heart rate by Doppler or stethoscope — normal 110–160/min**. Record it
- **Per-vaginal examination — only after placenta praevia has been excluded**; assess cervical dilatation, effacement, station, membranes, presenting part, cord
- **Look for a visible presenting part or crowning** — delivery is imminent
- **Examine for cord prolapse** — a cord felt or seen at the introitus is an immediate emergency

**Pre-eclampsia assessment**

- Blood pressure, **urine dipstick for protein**, reflexes and clonus, epigastric tenderness, fundus

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Urine pregnancy test where the pregnancy is unconfirmed; urine dipstick for protein in every pregnant woman**
- **CBC, blood group and Rh typing with cross-match**
- RFT, LFT, **uric acid, PT/INR, platelet count** (for pre-eclampsia and HELLP)
- RBS; urine routine and microscopy; urine culture
- HIV, HBsAg, HCV, VDRL where not already documented
- **Ultrasound abdomen and pelvis** — fetal viability, presentation, liquor, **placental site and any retroplacental clot**, estimated weight
- *Cardiotocography where available*

*Do not delay obstetric referral for investigations in a woman who is bleeding, fitting or fully dilated.*

### Basic initial management

***Inform the Consultant Obstetrician immediately. Arrange transfer to the labour room, or to a designated obstetric facility, with a doctor and a delivery kit accompanying the patient.***

**General**

- **Left lateral position** (relieves aorto-caval compression); oxygen if hypoxic or if there is fetal distress
- **Two wide-bore IV cannulae**; cross-matched blood arranged where there is bleeding
- IV fluids — Ringer’s lactate; antiemetic, analgesia and antacid as advised
- Hourly vitals, fetal heart rate and contraction charting

**If delivery is imminent and transfer is not possible**

- Call for obstetric and paediatric help; open the emergency delivery kit; warm the resuscitaire
- Support the perineum, control the head, check for a cord around the neck, deliver the shoulders, **dry and stimulate the baby, keep it warm, delay cord clamping by 1–3 minutes if the baby is vigorous**
- Active management of the third stage: **Inj. Oxytocin 10 IU IM after delivery**, controlled cord traction, uterine massage
- **Postpartum haemorrhage**: uterine massage and bimanual compression, **Inj. Oxytocin 10–20 IU in 500 mL NS infusion**, **Inj. Tranexamic acid 1 g IV within 3 hours**, **Inj. Carboprost 250 mcg IM** (*avoid in asthma*), **Tab. Misoprostol 800 mcg per rectum**, resuscitate with blood, examine for tears and retained placenta

**Specific obstetric emergencies**

- ***Eclampsia*** *→ left lateral, airway, oxygen; Inj. Magnesium sulphate 4 g IV over 20 minutes plus 5 g IM into each buttock (Pritchard), or 4 g IV then 1 g/h infusion; control BP with Inj. Labetalol 20 mg IV or oral nifedipine to a target of 140–150/90–100; monitor reflexes, respiratory rate and urine output; keep Inj. Calcium gluconate ready as the antidote*; urgent delivery is the definitive treatment
- ***Antepartum haemorrhage*** *→ no vaginal examination until praevia is excluded by ultrasound*; resuscitate, cross-match, urgent obstetric review
- ***Cord prolapse*** *→ knee–chest or exaggerated Sims position, elevate the presenting part with a gloved hand and keep it elevated, fill the bladder with 500 mL saline, do not handle the cord, arrange immediate caesarean section*
- ***Shoulder dystocia*** *→ call for help, McRoberts position and suprapubic pressure, internal rotational manoeuvres; never apply fundal pressure*
- ***Trauma in pregnancy*** *→ resuscitate the mother first; manual left uterine displacement*; anti-D if Rh negative; fetal monitoring for at least 4 hours
- ***Cardiac arrest in pregnancy beyond 20 weeks*** *→ continuous manual left uterine displacement, standard ACLS, and perimortem caesarean section within 4 minutes* if there is no return of circulation

**Rh negative mother**

- **Inj. Anti-D immunoglobulin 300 mcg IM within 72 hours** of any bleeding, trauma or delivery

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Heavy bleeding per vaginam, or bleeding with a tense tender uterus
- **Blood pressure ≥160/110 mmHg, or seizure, or headache with visual disturbance in pregnancy**
- Absent fetal movements or fetal heart rate outside 110–160/min
- Cord visible or palpable at the introitus
- Crowning, or an urge to bear down — delivery is imminent
- Meconium-stained liquor
- Previous caesarean section with severe continuous abdominal pain (**scar rupture**)
- Shock in a pregnant woman — concealed abruption or ruptured ectopic

## 6.2 Bleeding in Early Pregnancy — Abortion and Medical Termination

**Every woman with bleeding in early pregnancy has an ectopic pregnancy until it is excluded.** The distinction is made on ultrasound and β-hCG, not on the amount of bleeding or the severity of pain.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Last menstrual period; period of amenorrhoea; whether the pregnancy has been confirmed and by what**
- Gravida, para, living, abortions; previous ectopic pregnancy, tubal surgery, PID, IUCD in situ, assisted conception

**The bleeding**

- Onset, duration, amount (**pads used per day, clots**), colour
- **Passage of any tissue or products** — was it kept?

**The pain**

- Site, severity, character; **unilateral lower abdominal pain suggests ectopic**
- **Shoulder-tip pain, fainting, or an urge to defecate** — haemoperitoneum

**Other**

- Fever, offensive discharge, recent instrumentation or attempted termination elsewhere (**septic abortion**)
- Any drugs taken to induce abortion, and where they were obtained
- Blood group and Rh status; previous anti-D
- Co-morbidity, anticoagulants, bleeding disorders

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **pulse, blood pressure, and postural change** — **hypotension out of proportion to visible bleeding means concealed intra-abdominal bleeding**
- Pallor, temperature

**Abdomen**

- Tenderness, guarding, rebound, distension, **fullness in the pouch of Douglas**
- Uterine size in relation to dates

**Per speculum and per vaginal examination**

- Amount and source of bleeding; **products of conception at the os**
- **Cervical os — open or closed** (this defines the type of abortion)
- **Cervical motion tenderness and adnexal tenderness or mass** — suggests ectopic or PID
- Offensive discharge, uterine tenderness (septic abortion)

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Urine pregnancy test; quantitative serum β-hCG**
- **CBC, blood group and Rh typing, cross-match**
- RFT, LFT, PT/INR, RBS
- Urine routine and microscopy; urine culture
- HIV, HBsAg, HCV, VDRL
- **Ultrasound of the abdomen and pelvis — preferably transvaginal**
  - Location of the gestational sac — **intrauterine or not**
  - Fetal pole and cardiac activity
  - **Retained products of conception**
  - **Free fluid in the pouch of Douglas; adnexal mass**
  - Ovarian or other pelvic pathology

*An empty uterus with a β-hCG above the discriminatory zone (about 1500–2000 mIU/mL) is an ectopic pregnancy until proved otherwise.*

- **Bedside FAST in an unstable patient — free fluid confirms rupture and takes the patient to theatre, not to further imaging**

### Basic initial management

***Inform the Consultant Gynaecologist for every case. A haemodynamically unstable woman with a positive pregnancy test goes to theatre, not to the ultrasound room.***

**If unstable**

- Resuscitation bay; **two wide-bore cannulae; Ringer’s lactate bolus; cross-matched blood urgently; activate massive transfusion if needed**
- Urinary catheter, hourly output; oxygen; **immediate Gynaecology and theatre alert**

**Classification and the corresponding plan**

- **Threatened** — bleeding, closed os, viable intrauterine pregnancy → rest, reassurance, follow-up scan; no drug is proven to prevent miscarriage
- **Inevitable** — bleeding with an open os → admit for evacuation
- **Incomplete** — open os with retained products → resuscitate, **evacuation of retained products of conception**
- **Complete** — closed os, empty uterus, settling bleeding → observe and follow up β-hCG
- **Missed** — closed os, non-viable pregnancy retained → medical or surgical management, planned
- **Septic** — fever, offensive discharge, tender uterus → **blood and endocervical cultures, broad-spectrum IV antibiotics (Inj. Ceftriaxone + Inj. Metronidazole ± Inj. Gentamicin), aggressive resuscitation and urgent evacuation.** Watch for renal failure and DIC
- **Ectopic** → Gynaecology emergency; methotrexate or surgery as decided by the Gynaecologist

**Supportive care**

- Analgesia — Inj. Paracetamol 1 g IV or Inj. Drotaverine 40 mg IV
- Antiemetic — Inj. Ondansetron 4 mg IV; **Inj. Pantoprazole 40 mg IV**
- *Antibiotics only where infection is suspected or instrumentation is planned*
- **Inj. Anti-D immunoglobulin 300 mcg IM within 72 hours in every Rh-negative woman**

**Medical termination of pregnancy — legal position**

*MTP is performed only by a registered medical practitioner qualified under the MTP Act, at an approved facility, with the woman’s written consent on the prescribed form, and within the gestational limits and grounds set out in the Act as amended in 2021. The consent of the husband or family is not required. Confidentiality is a statutory obligation*

*The ED does not initiate termination. Where a woman presents having taken drugs obtained outside the hospital, treat the clinical consequences and refer to Gynaecology without judgement*

**Counselling and disposition**

- Explain the diagnosis and the plan in the woman’s own language; offer privacy and, where wanted, a chaperone or family member
- Discharge only if haemodynamically stable, ectopic excluded, haemoglobin acceptable and follow-up arranged; give **written return precautions for heavy bleeding, fever, severe pain or fainting**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Hypotension, tachycardia or syncope with a positive pregnancy test** — ruptured ectopic until excluded
- Shoulder-tip pain; free fluid on FAST
- Unilateral adnexal tenderness or mass with cervical motion tenderness
- Empty uterus with β-hCG \>1500–2000 mIU/mL
- Heavy bleeding — soaking more than one pad an hour, or clots
- Fever with offensive discharge and uterine tenderness — septic abortion
- History of instrumentation or drugs obtained outside a medical facility
- Haemoglobin \<7 g/dL

## 6.3 Abnormal Uterine Bleeding

The ED task is to decide whether the woman is **haemodynamically compromised or anaemic enough to need admission and transfusion**, to exclude pregnancy, and to start bleeding control while gynaecological assessment is arranged.

### Salient history to be taken

**Characterise the bleeding using the PALM-COEIN framework**

- Duration in days or months; **number of pads per day and whether they are soaked**; passage of clots
- Cycle — regular or irregular, length, intermenstrual or postcoital bleeding, postmenopausal bleeding
- **Fainting, giddiness, breathlessness, palpitation** — markers of significant anaemia

**Obstetric and gynaecological history**

- Gravida, para, living, abortions; **last menstrual period; contraception, IUCD, recent hormonal therapy**
- Known fibroid, adenomyosis, polyp, endometrial hyperplasia, PCOS, previous D\&C
- Cervical screening history; postcoital bleeding or discharge (**cervical malignancy**)

**Systemic causes — ask deliberately**

- **Bleeding from other sites, easy bruising, family history of a bleeding disorder** (von Willebrand disease is under-diagnosed and common)
- **Anticoagulants, antiplatelets, NSAIDs**
- Thyroid symptoms; features of chronic liver disease; chronic kidney disease
- Malignancy and chemotherapy; **thrombocytopenia**

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **postural blood pressure and pulse**
- **Pallor — conjunctival, palmar, tongue**; features of iron deficiency
- Temperature; thyroid examination; features of chronic liver disease

**Abdomen**

- Soft or tender; **palpable mass arising from the pelvis (fibroid or gravid uterus)**

**Per speculum and per vaginal examination**

- Amount and source of bleeding — **confirm it is uterine and not cervical, vaginal, urethral or rectal**
- **Cervix — growth, polyp, ulcer, friability, bleeding on touch**
- Uterine size, position, mobility, tenderness; adnexal mass

**Bleeding manifestations elsewhere**

- Petechiae, purpura, gum bleeding, epistaxis — suggest a systemic bleeding disorder

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Urine pregnancy test in every woman of reproductive age — without exception**
- **CBC with platelet count; blood group and cross-match**
- **PT/INR, aPTT** (and von Willebrand screening on Gynaecology advice in an adolescent with heavy periods since menarche)
- RFT, LFT, **thyroid function**, serum ferritin
- Urine routine and microscopy
- **Ultrasound of the pelvis** — endometrial thickness, fibroid, adenomyosis, polyp, ovarian pathology, retained products
- *Endometrial biopsy or hysteroscopy — Gynaecology, not an ED procedure*
- *Pap smear and cervical biopsy for any suspicious cervical lesion — do not biopsy a bleeding cervical growth in the ED*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Gynaecologist informed. A woman soaking a pad an hour, or with a haemoglobin below 7 g/dL, is admitted.***

**If unstable or severely anaemic**

- Two wide-bore cannulae; **Ringer’s lactate bolus; cross-matched PRBC transfusion**
- Urinary catheter and hourly output; monitored bed
- *Vaginal packing or a Foley’s catheter balloon tamponade in the uterine cavity as a temporising measure, on Gynaecology instruction*

**Medical control of bleeding**

- **Inj. Tranexamic acid 1 g IV TDS**, or **Tab. Tranexamic acid 1 g TDS** — first line, reduces blood loss by around a third
- *Hormonal control on Gynaecology advice: Tab. Norethisterone 5 mg TDS, or a combined oral contraceptive, or Inj. Medroxyprogesterone; high-dose oestrogen for acute severe bleeding*
- **Tab. Mefenamic acid 500 mg TDS** for pain and to reduce loss — *avoid if a bleeding disorder or peptic ulcer is suspected*
- Antiemetic and antacid as required
- **Oral or IV iron** for iron deficiency once acute bleeding is controlled
- *Correct coagulopathy and thrombocytopenia; discuss anticoagulant management with the prescribing team*

**Definitive management**

- *Dilatation and curettage, hysteroscopic resection, uterine artery embolisation or hysterectomy — Gynaecology decision*

**Disposition and counselling**

- Admit for haemodynamic instability, haemoglobin \<7 g/dL, ongoing heavy bleeding, suspected malignancy, or a bleeding disorder
- Discharge with iron, tranexamic acid, a **written pad chart**, and an early Gynaecology appointment
- **Any postmenopausal bleeding is malignancy until excluded and requires prompt gynaecological referral, whatever the amount**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Hypotension, tachycardia, postural drop, or syncope
- Soaking more than one pad per hour, or passing large clots
- Haemoglobin \<7 g/dL, or a rapid fall in haemoglobin
- **Positive pregnancy test** — manage as protocol 6.2
- Fever with offensive discharge — sepsis
- **Postmenopausal bleeding**, or a visible cervical growth
- Bleeding from multiple sites, petechiae, or platelet count \<50,000
- Patient on an anticoagulant, or with known liver or kidney disease

# 7.0 Other Presentations

## 7.1 Acute Retention of Urine

Acute retention is painful, distressing and easy to relieve. **The single decision that matters is whether catheterisation is safe** — and the answer is no if there is any suspicion of urethral injury or recent urethral surgery.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Duration of inability to pass urine; time of the last void; volume passed**
- Preceding **lower urinary tract symptoms** — hesitancy, poor stream, terminal dribbling, frequency, nocturia, incomplete emptying
- **Precipitants** — constipation, alcohol, cold weather, immobility, a long journey, recent surgery or anaesthesia
- **Drugs** — anticholinergics, antihistamines, tricyclics, opioids, alpha-agonists in cough remedies, antipsychotics, calcium channel blockers

**Look for the cause**

- Fever, dysuria, pus in the urine, perineal pain (**prostatitis, urinary infection**)
- **Haematuria and clots** (clot retention)
- Back pain, leg weakness, saddle anaesthesia, faecal incontinence (**cauda equina syndrome — a neurosurgical emergency**)
- Known **benign prostatic hyperplasia, prostate or bladder malignancy, urethral stricture**
- Previous urethral instrumentation, TURP, catheterisation difficulty, urethroplasty
- **Recent pelvic trauma or blood at the meatus** → see protocol 1.3 — **do not catheterise**
- In women: pelvic mass, gravid uterus, recent childbirth, pelvic surgery
- Diabetes (neuropathic bladder), hypertension, antiplatelets and anticoagulants

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **pulse, blood pressure, temperature** — pain and distension can cause marked hypertension and tachycardia, which settle after drainage
- **Palpable, tender, dull suprapubic swelling that the patient recognises as the urge to void**
- Confirm with **bedside ultrasound of the bladder** — volume in millilitres
- **Per-rectal examination** — prostate size, consistency, nodularity, tenderness; **anal tone and perianal sensation if cauda equina is possible**
- External genitalia — **meatal stenosis, phimosis, paraphimosis, blood at the meatus**
- Lower limb power, sensation, reflexes and plantars where a neurological cause is suspected
- In women: abdominal and pelvic examination for a mass or a gravid uterus
- Look for signs of uraemia and fluid overload in long-standing obstruction

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Bedside bladder ultrasound before and after catheterisation**
- Urine routine and microscopy, **urine culture** — from the catheter specimen
- CBC; **RFT and serum electrolytes — look for post-renal acute kidney injury and hyperkalaemia**
- RBS; ABG if uraemic or unwell
- *Serum PSA — not in the acute setting; retention, catheterisation and infection all raise it falsely. Defer for 6 weeks*
- **Ultrasound of the abdomen, kidneys and pelvis** — hydronephrosis, prostate size, bladder wall, calculi, residual volume, pelvic mass
- *Urgent MRI whole spine if cauda equina syndrome is suspected — do not wait until morning*

### Basic initial management

***Inform the Consultant Urologist. The Issue 01 escalation to an infant feeding tube is withdrawn — it risks urethral trauma and false passage.***

**Catheterisation — the correct technique**

- Explain the procedure and take verbal consent; maintain privacy and use a strict aseptic technique
- **Instil the entire tube of 2% lignocaine jelly (about 10–11 mL) into the urethra and wait 3–5 minutes.** This is the step most often skipped and most often the reason for failure
- **Use a 16 Fr Foley’s catheter as standard.** A larger, stiffer catheter often negotiates a prostatic obstruction better than a smaller one — **do not reflexly step down in size**
- **Advance the catheter to the hilt (the bifurcation) before inflating the balloon** — inflating in the urethra causes serious injury
- Confirm urine flow **before** inflating the balloon; then inflate with 10 mL sterile water
- **If it does not pass: stop after one or at most two gentle attempts and call the Urologist.** Do not force, do not use a stylet, do not try progressively smaller tubes
- *The Urologist may use a Coudé-tip catheter, a guidewire, flexible cystoscopy, or proceed to suprapubic catheterisation*

***Absolute contraindications to per-urethral catheterisation in the ED: blood at the meatus, suspected urethral injury, recent urethral or prostatic surgery.*** Call Urology instead

**After drainage**

- **Record the residual volume drained**

*Rapid complete decompression is safe in the great majority; clamping is no longer recommended routinely. However, watch for two complications:*

  - **Post-obstructive diuresis** — urine output \>200 mL/h. **Monitor hourly output, replace 50–75% of losses with 0.45% or 0.9% saline, and check electrolytes 6-hourly**
  - **Haematuria ex vacuo** — usually self-limiting; maintain hydration
- Analgesia if still uncomfortable; **Tab. Tamsulosin 0.4 mg at night** started before a trial without catheter improves success
- *Antibiotics only for proven or strongly suspected infection — not routine prophylaxis after catheterisation*

**Disposition**

- Admit for acute kidney injury, hyperkalaemia, urosepsis, clot retention, failed catheterisation, or **cauda equina syndrome (immediate neurosurgical referral)**
- Discharge with a **leg bag, catheter care instructions, a documented residual volume, tamsulosin, and a urology appointment for a trial without catheter in 1–2 weeks**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Blood at the urethral meatus, or a history of pelvic trauma** — do not catheterise
- **Back pain with leg weakness, saddle anaesthesia or reduced anal tone** — cauda equina syndrome
- Fever with rigors and suprapubic or perineal pain — urosepsis or prostatic abscess
- Acute kidney injury, hyperkalaemia or severe acidosis on the initial panel
- Clot retention with ongoing haematuria
- Bilateral hydronephrosis on ultrasound
- Post-obstructive diuresis with urine output \>200 mL/h
- Failed catheterisation after one or two gentle attempts

## 7.2 Haematuria

Most haematuria is not an emergency; **clot retention, haemodynamic instability and infection are.** The other essential task is to make sure that painless visible haematuria in an adult over 40 is not dismissed as a urinary infection, because it is urothelial malignancy until proved otherwise.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Timing within the stream** — initial (urethral), terminal (bladder neck or prostate), or throughout (upper tract or bladder)
- Colour — bright red, cola-coloured (**glomerular**), with clots (**never glomerular — clots indicate a urological source**)
- Duration, number of episodes, previous similar episodes

**Painful or painless?**

- **Painless visible haematuria in an adult over 40** — malignancy of the bladder, kidney or prostate until excluded
- **Loin-to-groin colic** — ureteric calculus
- **Dysuria, frequency, urgency, suprapubic pain, fever** — urinary tract infection
- **Inability to pass urine with clots** — clot retention

**Background**

- Recent sore throat or skin infection (**post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis**); rash, joint pain, haemoptysis (**vasculitis, Goodpasture**)
- Trauma; recent instrumentation, catheterisation, biopsy, lithotripsy or surgery
- **Anticoagulants, antiplatelets** — note that anticoagulation **unmasks** rather than causes haematuria; it still needs investigation
- Smoking, occupational exposure to dyes or rubber (bladder cancer), analgesic abuse
- Known stones, BPH, prostate or bladder malignancy, polycystic kidney disease, sickle cell trait
- Recent strenuous exercise; menstruation; beetroot or rifampicin (pseudo-haematuria)
- Family history of renal disease or deafness (Alport syndrome)

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **pulse, blood pressure, temperature; postural drop if bleeding is heavy**
- Pallor; oedema (**suggests a glomerular cause**); rash, purpura, joint swelling

**Abdomen and pelvis**

- **Palpable bladder** — clot retention
- Renal angle tenderness; ballotable kidney or flank mass
- Suprapubic tenderness
- **Per-rectal examination** — prostate size, nodularity, tenderness
- External genitalia and meatus — local cause, trauma, catheter site
- **Confirm the source** — in a woman, exclude vaginal or rectal bleeding by direct inspection before labelling it haematuria

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Urine routine and microscopy** — red cell morphology; **dysmorphic red cells and red cell casts indicate a glomerular source and need Nephrology, not Urology**
- **Urine culture and sensitivity**
- CBC with haemoglobin and platelet count; **blood group and cross-match if bleeding is heavy**
- **RFT and serum electrolytes** — renal function and potassium
- **Coagulation profile — essential if on an anticoagulant or antiplatelet**
- RBS; LFT
- *Urine cytology; ASO titre, complement, ANA, ANCA where a glomerular cause is suspected*
- **Ultrasound of the abdomen, kidneys, ureters, bladder and prostate** — calculus, mass, hydronephrosis, clots in the bladder, residual volume
- *CT urography — the definitive imaging for visible haematuria in an adult; NCCT KUB for suspected calculus*
- *Flexible cystoscopy — Urology; mandatory for visible haematuria in an adult over 40*

### Basic initial management

***Inform the Consultant Urologist. Nephrology instead if the picture is glomerular — dysmorphic red cells, casts, proteinuria, oedema, hypertension and renal impairment.***

**Mild haematuria, passing urine normally, no clots**

- **Do not catheterise.** Catheterisation adds trauma and infection risk with no benefit
- **Maintain a good oral or IV fluid intake** to keep the urine dilute and reduce clot formation
- Treat the cause — antibiotics for a proven infection, analgesia and medical expulsive therapy for a calculus
- Arrange urological follow-up and imaging; **explain clearly that follow-up is essential even if the bleeding stops**

**Haematuria with clots, or clot retention**

- **Insert a 22 Fr or 24 Fr three-way catheter** and evacuate clots by manual bladder washout with a bladder syringe and sterile normal saline
- **Start continuous bladder irrigation with normal saline**, titrating the rate to keep the effluent pale pink
- **Monitor the input and output of irrigation fluid separately from the true urine output** — a positive balance means the outflow is blocked
- Watch for **catheter blockage, bladder distension and TUR-type hyponatraemia** if large volumes are absorbed
- *If clots cannot be cleared, or bleeding is heavy, the Urologist will proceed to cystoscopy and clot evacuation under anaesthesia*

**Resuscitation**

- Wide-bore IV access; **crystalloid and cross-matched PRBC transfusion for haemodynamic instability or haemoglobin \<7 g/dL**
- *Correct coagulopathy; discuss whether to withhold anticoagulants with the prescribing team — do not stop anticoagulation unilaterally in a patient with a metallic valve or recent stent*
- *Inj. Tranexamic acid is relatively contraindicated in upper-tract bleeding because it promotes clot retention — Consultant decision only*

**Disposition**

- Admit for clot retention, haemodynamic instability, significant anaemia, acute kidney injury, urosepsis, or uncontrolled bleeding on anticoagulants
- Discharge with hydration advice, **written instructions to return if unable to pass urine or if bleeding worsens**, and a definite urology appointment

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Inability to pass urine with a palpable bladder — clot retention
- Hypotension, tachycardia, or haemoglobin \<7 g/dL
- Fever with rigors — urosepsis
- Acute kidney injury or hyperkalaemia
- **Painless visible haematuria in an adult over 40** — needs full urological evaluation, not reassurance
- Dysmorphic red cells, red cell casts, proteinuria, oedema or hypertension — glomerulonephritis
- Haematuria with haemoptysis or a rash — systemic vasculitis
- Haematuria after trauma — assess for renal and bladder injury

## 7.3 Fever

The task in the ED is not to name the organism. It is to **identify sepsis, identify the vulnerable patient, and start the right treatment within the hour** — while avoiding the reflex antibiotic for every febrile patient.

### Salient history to be taken

- Onset, duration and **pattern** — continuous, remittent, intermittent, with rigors
- Documented temperature at home; **\>100.4°F (38°C) is fever, \>105.8°F (41°C) is hyperpyrexia**

**Localise the source — go through the systems**

- **Respiratory:** cough, sputum, breathlessness, pleuritic pain, sore throat, coryza
- **Urinary:** dysuria, frequency, urgency, flank pain, offensive urine
- **Abdominal:** pain, vomiting, loose stools, jaundice, distension
- **CNS:** headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, altered sensorium, seizure
- **Skin and soft tissue:** rash, boil, cellulitis, ulcer, **diabetic foot**, IV line site, bed sore
- **ENT and dental:** ear discharge, sinus pain, dental abscess
- Joint pain and swelling; back pain

**Epidemiology and exposure**

- **Travel** — within India and abroad; **season** — dengue and malaria in the post-monsoon months, scrub typhus, leptospirosis after flooding
- Contact with a febrile or tuberculous person; animal or tick exposure; unpasteurised milk
- Recent hospitalisation, surgery, catheter, or antibiotic course

**Host factors that change everything**

- **Diabetes, malignancy and chemotherapy, HIV, post-transplant, steroids or immunosuppressants, CKD, CLD, asplenia, pregnancy, extremes of age**
- Current medications — including any that cause **drug fever** or **neuroleptic malignant syndrome**

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **record the actual temperature, and calculate the shock index**
- **qSOFA:** altered mentation, respiratory rate ≥22, systolic BP ≤100 — **two of three means high risk**

**Full head-to-toe examination — undress the patient**

- Oral cavity, teeth, throat, tonsils, ears, sinuses
- Neck stiffness, Kernig’s and Brudzinski’s signs
- Chest — crepitations, bronchial breathing, pleural rub, dullness
- Cardiac — **new murmur (infective endocarditis)**, pericardial rub
- Abdomen — tenderness, guarding, organomegaly, renal angle tenderness
- **Skin — rash (describe it: maculopapular, purpuric, vesicular), eschar of scrub typhus, cellulitis, abscess, IV cannula site, pressure areas**
- **Feet in every diabetic; perineum and perianal region; the back of every immobile patient**
- Joints for effusion and restricted movement
- Lymphadenopathy; conjunctival suffusion (leptospirosis); jaundice

### Point-of-care investigations

- **CBC with differential and platelet count** — **thrombocytopenia in dengue and malaria; neutropenia in the oncology patient**
- **ABG with lactate** — **a lactate above 2 mmol/L defines high-risk sepsis**
- **Blood cultures — two sets from separate sites, before antibiotics** (but never delaying them beyond an hour)
- RFT, electrolytes, LFT, RBS, PT/INR
- Urine routine, microscopy and culture
- **Malaria — peripheral smear and rapid antigen test; Dengue NS1, IgM; Widal or typhidot; scrub typhus IgM; leptospira serology** as the season and history dictate
- CRP and procalcitonin where the distinction between viral and bacterial matters
- Chest X-ray; **POCUS** — lung, abdomen, IVC, cardiac function, source of infection
- *NCCT / CECT and CSF analysis if meningitis or encephalitis is suspected — do not delay antibiotics for the lumbar puncture*
- *Ultrasound or CT of any suspected collection or abscess*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant informed. If sepsis is suspected, activate the SEPSIS ALERT and complete the one-hour bundle.***

**The Sepsis One-Hour Bundle**

- **1. Measure lactate** (and remeasure if the initial value is \>2)
- **2. Obtain blood cultures before antibiotics**
- **3. Administer broad-spectrum antibiotics**
- **4. Begin rapid crystalloid, 30 mL/kg, for hypotension or lactate ≥4 mmol/L**
- **5. Start vasopressors if the patient remains hypotensive during or after fluid resuscitation, to a MAP of ≥65 mmHg**

**Supportive care**

- Monitored bed; oxygen to target SpO₂ \>94%
- **Inj. Paracetamol 1 g IV** or **Tab. Paracetamol 650 mg PO** for fever and comfort; tepid sponging
- *Avoid NSAIDs where dengue is possible, or where there is renal impairment or a bleeding risk*
