- IV fluids guided by perfusion, urine output, lactate clearance and IVC assessment — **not by a fixed volume in the cardiac or renal patient**
- Urinary catheter and hourly output where the patient is septic

**Antibiotics — targeted where possible**

- *Choose by the syndrome; see the antibiotic table in Section 11. Take cultures first. Review and de-escalate at 48–72 hours*

***Do not start an antibiotic for an undifferentiated fever of two days in a well patient with a normal examination.*** Explain, give paracetamol, arrange review and provide written return advice

- *Broad-spectrum cover only where the source is unknown and the patient is septic, immunosuppressed or deteriorating*

**Special situations**

- ***Suspected meningitis*** *→ Inj. Ceftriaxone 2 g IV BD + Inj. Vancomycin 15 mg/kg IV BD + Inj. Dexamethasone 10 mg IV just before the first antibiotic dose*; add ampicillin in the elderly or pregnant, and acyclovir if encephalitis is possible
- ***Suspected severe malaria*** *→ Inj. Artesunate 2.4 mg/kg IV at 0, 12 and 24 hours, then daily*
- ***Suspected dengue with warning signs*** *→ careful isotonic fluid, monitor haematocrit and platelets, avoid NSAIDs and intramuscular injections*
- ***Fever with a purpuric rash*** *→ meningococcaemia — antibiotics immediately, do not wait for imaging or the lumbar puncture*
- ***Fever with recent chemotherapy*** → protocol 7.4, antibiotics within the hour
- ***Fever with rigidity and a raised creatine kinase on antipsychotics*** → neuroleptic malignant syndrome; stop the drug, cool actively, consider dantrolene

**Disposition**

- Admit for sepsis, hypoxia, altered sensorium, inability to tolerate orally, significant thrombocytopenia or bleeding, an identified serious focus, immunosuppression, or a persistent fever without a source in a vulnerable patient

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- qSOFA ≥2, shock index \>1, or lactate \>2 mmol/L
- Altered sensorium, neck stiffness, seizure, or a **purpuric rash**
- Respiratory rate \>30 or SpO₂ \<94%
- Persistent vomiting with no oral intake, or decreased urine output
- Abdominal pain with guarding or tenderness
- **Platelet count \<20,000, bleeding manifestations, or a positive tourniquet test**
- **Fever with chemotherapy in the last 14 days, aplastic anaemia or acute leukaemia**
- Vulnerable host — pregnancy, infancy, old age, diabetes, immunosuppression, renal failure, post-transplant, asplenia

## 7.4 Oncology Patient with Fever — Febrile Neutropenia

**Febrile neutropenia is an oncological emergency with a mortality that rises measurably with every hour of delay in the first antibiotic dose.** The target at this hospital is **the first dose within 60 minutes of arrival**, and it is given on suspicion — not after the neutrophil count comes back.

### Salient history to be taken

**Definition**

- **Fever:** a single oral temperature ≥38.3°C (101°F), **or** ≥38.0°C (100.4°F) sustained over one hour
- **Neutropenia:** absolute neutrophil count \<500 cells/mm³, **or** \<1000 with an expected fall below 500 within 48 hours

**Oncological history**

- **Type, site and stage of cancer; treatment intent**
- **Last chemotherapy — date, regimen, cycle number.** The nadir is usually 7–14 days after the dose
- **Were growth factors (G-CSF) given?** Date of the last dose
- Recent radiotherapy; recent surgery; recent blood products
- **Chemoport, PICC or central line — site, duration, any recent difficulty or pain**
- Previous episodes of febrile neutropenia; **known colonisation or previous culture with a resistant organism (ESBL, MRSA, carbapenem-resistant)**
- Antibiotic and antifungal prophylaxis already being taken

**Localising symptoms — ask about all of them**

- Cough, breathlessness, sore throat, sinus pain
- Dysuria, frequency, flank pain
- Diarrhoea, abdominal pain, **perianal pain**
- **Mouth pain, ulcers, difficulty swallowing (mucositis)**
- Line site pain, redness or discharge; skin lesions; headache; rash

### Salient clinical examination

- Complete primary survey; **temperature, and calculate the shock index. Neutropenic patients decompensate suddenly**

***Classical signs of inflammation are absent because there are no neutrophils.*** *A pneumonia may have a clear chest and a normal chest X-ray; an abscess may have no pus and no fluctuance. Examine for subtle tenderness and erythema*

**Systematic search for a focus**

- **Oral cavity — mucositis, ulcers, candidiasis, dental sepsis**
- **Chemoport, PICC and all line sites — erythema, tenderness, discharge, tunnel tenderness**
- Chest — any crepitation, however localised
- Abdomen — tenderness, particularly **right iliac fossa (neutropenic enterocolitis)**
- **Perianal region — inspect only. Tenderness may be the only sign of a perianal abscess**

***No per-rectal examination, no rectal thermometer, no suppository and no enema in a neutropenic patient***

- Skin over the whole body — cellulitis, ecthyma gangrenosum, herpes, cannula sites
- Neurological — sensorium, neck stiffness, focal deficit

### Point-of-care investigations

- **CBC with absolute neutrophil count — sent immediately, but the antibiotic does not wait for it**
- **Blood cultures — one set from a peripheral vein AND one set from each lumen of the central line, before antibiotics**
- Urine routine, microscopy and **urine culture**
- **ABG with lactate**; RBS
- RFT and electrolytes, LFT, **serum magnesium**, PT/INR
- **Procalcitonin and CRP** for trend
- **Chest X-ray** — remember that it may be normal in neutropenic pneumonia
- Sputum, stool, wound or line-site swabs for culture as indicated; **stool C. difficile toxin if diarrhoea**
- *CT chest and abdomen — far more sensitive than plain films; consider early in persistent fever*
- *Serum galactomannan and beta-D-glucan for suspected invasive fungal disease in prolonged neutropenia*
- *Echocardiography if endocarditis or persistent line-related bacteraemia is suspected*

### Basic initial management

***Consultant Medical Oncology informed at once. Antibiotic within 60 minutes of arrival, after cultures. Do not wait for the counts, the chest X-ray or the Consultant to arrive.***

**Immediate**

- Monitored bed, **protective isolation, strict hand hygiene, mask for staff and visitors**
- IV access; oxygen to target SpO₂ \>94%
- **Draw cultures → give the first antibiotic dose → then complete the workup**

**Empirical antibiotics — anti-pseudomonal cover is mandatory**

- **Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6–8h**, *or* **Inj. Cefoperazone–Sulbactam 3 g IV q12h**, *or* **Inj. Cefepime 2 g IV q8h**
- **Inj. Meropenem 1 g IV q8h** — *for haemodynamic instability, prior ESBL colonisation, recent broad-spectrum exposure, or a general or gastrointestinal malignancy with suspected intra-abdominal source*
- *Add Inj. Vancomycin 15 mg/kg IV q12h or Inj. Teicoplanin 400 mg IV if there is: catheter-related infection, skin or soft tissue infection, severe mucositis, known MRSA colonisation, haemodynamic instability, or pneumonia*
- *Add Inj. Amikacin 15 mg/kg IV OD for severe sepsis or suspected resistant Gram-negative infection*
- *Add antifungal cover (Inj. Caspofungin or liposomal Amphotericin B) for fever persisting beyond 4–7 days of broad-spectrum antibiotics, or where invasive fungal disease is suspected — Oncology decision*
- *Add Inj. Metronidazole for suspected intra-abdominal or perianal source*

*Adjust the regimen to the patient’s previous culture history and to unit antibiogram — there is no single correct empirical regimen*

**Resuscitation and supportive care**

- **Inj. Normal saline or Ringer’s lactate 1000 mL IV** for hypotension; **30 mL/kg if septic; noradrenaline if fluid-unresponsive; ICU evaluation if the blood pressure does not improve**
- **Inj. Paracetamol 1 g IV** or **Tab. Paracetamol 650 mg** for fever — *record the temperature before giving it, because antipyretics mask the trend*
- *Granulocyte colony-stimulating factor — Inj. Filgrastim 5 mcg/kg SC daily — for profound or prolonged neutropenia, sepsis, or pneumonia; Oncology decision*
- Mouth care with saline or chlorhexidine rinses; treat mucositis and candidiasis
- Transfuse platelets for counts \<10,000, or \<20,000 with fever, or \<50,000 with active bleeding
- *Remove the central line for tunnel infection, port pocket infection, septic thrombophlebitis, persistent bacteraemia despite 72 hours of appropriate therapy, or infection with S. aureus, Candida, Pseudomonas or a rapidly growing mycobacterium*

**Risk stratification — MASCC score**

- Burden of illness: none or mild **5**  •  moderate **3**  •  severe **0**
- No hypotension (SBP \>90) **5**  •  No COPD **4**  •  Solid tumour, or no previous fungal infection **4**
- No dehydration **3**  •  Outpatient at onset of fever **3**  •  Age \<60 years **2**
- **Score ≥21 → low risk.** May be considered for oral antibiotics (Amoxicillin–clavulanate 625 mg TDS + Levofloxacin 750 mg OD) with **close daily follow-up and reliable access to the hospital**
- **Score \<21 → high risk. Admit for IV antibiotics**

*In practice, the great majority of patients presenting to this ED are admitted. Outpatient management requires a reliable patient, a telephone, transport, and a documented oncology plan*

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- Hypotension, shock index \>1, or lactate \>2 mmol/L
- Respiratory rate \>24, or SpO₂ \<94%
- Altered sensorium; new confusion
- Chemoport or line site erythema, tenderness or discharge
- **Right iliac fossa pain with fever — neutropenic enterocolitis**
- Perianal pain — abscess (inspect only)
- Severe mucositis preventing oral intake
- Absolute neutrophil count \<100, or profound neutropenia expected to last more than 7 days
- Fever persisting beyond 4–7 days on broad-spectrum antibiotics — consider invasive fungal disease

## 7.5 Anxiety, Panic Attack and Acute Psychological Distress

**Anxiety is a diagnosis of exclusion in the Emergency Department.** A panic attack and a pulmonary embolism can look identical at the door, and the label, once written in the notes, tends to follow the patient. Exclude the organic cause, then treat the distress properly — it is real, and it responds to unhurried, structured care.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Onset and duration** — a panic attack peaks within 10 minutes and usually settles within 30
- **Previous identical episodes, and whether they were ever investigated** — a first episode deserves more scrutiny than the fifth
- The symptoms themselves: palpitations, chest tightness, breathlessness, dizziness, **perioral and peripheral tingling, carpopedal spasm**, tremor, sweating, a sense of impending doom, derealisation
- **Trigger or context** — bereavement, job loss, examination, relationship breakdown, an anniversary, a recent medical diagnosis

**Ask directly about the organic mimics**

- **Substance use:** stimulants, cannabis, caffeine, **alcohol or benzodiazepine withdrawal**, decongestants, thyroxine, steroids, salbutamol, SSRIs recently started or stopped
- Symptoms of thyrotoxicosis: weight loss, heat intolerance, diarrhoea, tremor
- Episodic headache with sweating and palpitations — phaeochromocytoma
- Risk factors for pulmonary embolism: immobility, surgery, malignancy, oral contraceptive, pregnancy, previous VTE
- Diabetes — hypoglycaemia presents exactly like a panic attack

**Ask directly about risk**

- **"Have you had thoughts of harming yourself, or of ending your life?"** — asking does not plant the idea; not asking misses it
- **Ask about safety at home**, and about whether the patient feels able to keep themselves safe today
- **Ask what support they have** — who is with them, and who they can call

### Salient clinical examination

- **A full set of vital signs, recorded and repeated** — this is not a formality; persistent tachycardia or hypoxia after the episode settles is organic until proved otherwise
- **SpO₂ — hypoxia is not a feature of a panic attack**
- **RBS in every patient**
- Cardiovascular: rhythm, murmur, JVP, peripheral perfusion
- Respiratory: air entry, wheeze, calf swelling or tenderness
- **Thyroid: goitre, tremor, lid lag, warm moist skin, atrial fibrillation**
- Pupils, tremor, sweating, agitation, clonus — stimulant toxicity, withdrawal, serotonin syndrome
- Mental state: appearance, speech, mood, thought content, insight, orientation

*Carpopedal spasm and perioral tingling reflect hyperventilation-induced alkalosis and can occur in any cause of hyperventilation, organic included.*

### Point-of-care investigations

- **ECG in every patient with chest symptoms or palpitations, and in every patient over 40**
- **RBS**; **SpO₂**
- **Urine hCG in every woman of childbearing age**
- Electrolytes including calcium and magnesium; CBC; RFT
- **Thyroid function** — have a low threshold, particularly for a first episode, weight loss or atrial fibrillation
- *ABG only if there is genuine diagnostic doubt — a respiratory alkalosis with normal oxygenation supports hyperventilation, but does not exclude a cause for it*
- *D-dimer or CTPA only if pulmonary embolism is genuinely suspected on a validated score — not as reassurance*
- Toxicology screen where stimulant use or withdrawal is suspected

***Order investigations to answer a question, not to reassure. A negative test rarely reassures an anxious patient for long, and the next episode brings them back.***

### Basic initial management

**1. Exclude the organic cause first**

- Complete the assessment above **before** the word "anxiety" appears anywhere in the notes
- **Treat hypoglycaemia, hypoxia, arrhythmia, thyrotoxicosis, withdrawal, stimulant toxicity or sepsis on their own protocols**

**2. Manage the acute episode**

- **Move the patient to a quiet, low-stimulus area with one staff member and a relative if they wish**
- **Sit with them and talk them through it.** Slow, coached breathing — in through the nose, a longer breath out, hand on the abdomen. Name what is happening: *"this is a panic attack; it is frightening, it is not dangerous, and it will pass"*
- Grounding: ask them to name what they can see, hear and feel in the room

***Do not use a paper bag.*** It is unsafe, and it is harmful if the cause turns out to be hypoxic

- **Most episodes settle within 20–30 minutes with this alone, and no drug at all**

**3. Medication — if required**

- **Tab. Lorazepam 0.5–1 mg PO** (or **Inj. Lorazepam 1–2 mg IM/IV** in severe distress)

*Use the smallest effective dose, give one dose in the ED, and do not send the patient home with a benzodiazepine prescription — dependence develops quickly and the ED is not the place to start long-term therapy*

- *Avoid benzodiazepines where intoxication or respiratory compromise is possible; halve the dose in the elderly*
- **Do not start an antidepressant from the ED** — that decision belongs with the family physician or psychiatrist who will follow the patient up

**4. Risk assessment and disposition before discharge**

- **Every patient is asked directly about self-harm and suicidal thoughts, and the answer is recorded**
- **Refer to psychiatry the same visit if:** there is any suicidal ideation or intent, self-harm, psychosis, a first panic attack with severe functional impairment, substance dependence, or the patient cannot be kept safe at home
- **Discharge only when:** vital signs are normal and stable, the organic workup is complete, the patient is calm, a responsible adult is with them, and a follow-up appointment is arranged
- **Give written information about panic attacks and breathing technique**, and a named follow-up — family physician, psychologist or psychiatry OPD
- **Name the diagnosis honestly and without dismissiveness.** "Your heart and lungs are normal, and what you had was a panic attack — it is a real condition and it is treatable" is a far better discharge than "there is nothing wrong with you"

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **First episode over the age of 40** — look harder for an organic cause
- **Hypoxia, or a persistently abnormal vital sign after the episode settles**
- **Chest pain with ECG changes, or with risk factors for ACS or pulmonary embolism**
- **Fever, or focal neurological signs**
- **Thyrotoxic features, or new atrial fibrillation**
- **Suspected alcohol or benzodiazepine withdrawal** — this is a medical emergency, not anxiety; treat with benzodiazepines and admit
- **Any suicidal ideation, plan, intent, or act of self-harm** — do not discharge; refer to psychiatry the same visit
- **Psychotic features, or a patient who cannot be kept safe at home**
- **Frequent re-attendance with the same presentation** — indicates an unmet need, not attention-seeking; arrange proper follow-up

### Quick guide — what anxiety is mistaken for, and how to talk the patient through the episode

#### Organic conditions mislabelled as anxiety

- **Pulmonary embolism** — the classic and most dangerous misdiagnosis; breathlessness with a sense of doom, often with a normal chest examination
- **Acute coronary syndrome** — especially in women, the diabetic and the elderly
- **Arrhythmia** — SVT, atrial fibrillation; the patient reports "anxiety" but the ECG has the answer
- **Hypoglycaemia** — tremor, sweating, palpitations, confusion
- **Thyrotoxicosis** — and thyroid storm at the severe end
- **Asthma** — breathlessness attributed to anxiety, particularly in the young
- **Alcohol or benzodiazepine withdrawal** — tremor, sweating, tachycardia; **untreated, this kills**
- **Stimulant intoxication**; **serotonin syndrome**; **anticholinergic toxicity**
- **Phaeochromocytoma**; **carcinoid**; **hypercalcaemia**
- **Sepsis and hypoxia of any cause** — agitation is an early sign

#### Talking a patient through a panic attack

- **Sit down, at eye level.** Standing over a frightened patient makes it worse
- **Say what you have found:** "your heart tracing is normal, your oxygen is normal"
- **Name it:** "this is a panic attack"
- **Explain it briefly:** the body’s alarm system has fired without a real threat; the fast breathing causes the tingling and the dizziness, which is why it feels worse
- **Reassure about safety, not about triviality:** "this is not dangerous" — never "there is nothing wrong with you"
- **Coach the breathing:** breathe with them, out longer than in, for two minutes
- **Ground them:** five things you can see, four you can hear, three you can touch
- **Stay, or have a nurse stay.** Leaving the patient alone in a cubicle prolongs the episode
- **Close the loop:** what happened, what was excluded, what to do next time, and who to see

# 8.0 Additional Protocols (new in Revision 02)

These thirteen presentations were not covered in Issue 01 and are added in this revision. They are grouped together as Section 8 so that Sections 1 to 7 continue to follow the presentation groups of the original manual. **Diabetic ketoacidosis and the hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state are covered in full at protocol 4.3** and are not repeated here.

## 8.1 Anaphylaxis

Anaphylaxis is a **clinical diagnosis made at the bedside**. Adrenaline is the only drug that changes the outcome, and the commonest error in every audit is giving it late, giving it intravenously by mistake, or giving an antihistamine instead.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Exposure** — drug (especially beta-lactams, NSAIDs, contrast, neuromuscular blockers), food (peanut, tree nut, shellfish, egg, milk), insect sting, latex
- **Time from exposure to symptoms** — the shorter the interval, the more severe the reaction
- Route of exposure; dose; whether this drug has been given before
- **Previous reactions** and whether adrenaline was needed
- Known asthma — **a strong predictor of fatal anaphylaxis**
- **Current beta-blocker or ACE inhibitor** — may blunt the response to adrenaline
- Cofactors that lower the threshold: exercise, alcohol, NSAIDs, acute infection

*In an idiopathic or recurrent case ask about mastocytosis.*

### Salient clinical examination

**Recognise the pattern — acute onset with two or more systems, or hypotension after a known allergen**

- **Airway** — tongue and lip swelling, hoarseness, stridor, a sense of throat closing
- **Breathing** — tachypnoea, wheeze, cyanosis, exhaustion, SpO₂ falling
- **Circulation** — hypotension, tachycardia, poor capillary refill, collapse
- **Skin** — urticaria, flushing, angio-oedema (**absent in up to 20%** — its absence does not exclude the diagnosis)
- **GI** — vomiting, cramping abdominal pain, incontinence

*In a child, persistent cough, vomiting and drowsiness may be the whole presentation.*

### Point-of-care investigations

- **No investigation is needed to make the diagnosis or to give adrenaline**
- Continuous ECG, SpO₂, non-invasive BP, capnography if intubated
- RBS; ABG if severe
- *Serum tryptase at 1–2 hours and again at 24 hours confirms the diagnosis retrospectively — useful for the allergy clinic, useless in the moment*
- ECG — for Kounis syndrome (allergic coronary spasm) if chest pain accompanies the reaction
- Chest X-ray only if the picture is unclear

### Basic initial management

**Immediate — in this order**

- **1. Remove the trigger** — stop the infusion, remove the sting
- **2. INJ. ADRENALINE 0.5 mg (0.5 mL of 1:1000) INTRAMUSCULARLY into the anterolateral thigh**
  - **Child 6–12 years: 0.3 mg  \|  6 months–6 years: 0.15 mg  \|  \<6 months: 0.1–0.15 mg**
  - **Repeat every 5 minutes** if there is no improvement
  - ***Never give 1:1000 adrenaline intravenously as a bolus***
- **3. Position:** lie flat with the legs raised; sit up only if breathing is the problem; left lateral in pregnancy. **Do not stand the patient up or walk them — sudden upright posture has caused death**
- **4. High-flow oxygen** through a non-rebreathe mask
- **5. Fluid: crystalloid 500–1000 mL rapid bolus** in an adult (**20 mL/kg** in a child), repeated as needed — large volumes are often required

**Refractory anaphylaxis — no response to two IM doses**

- **Inj. Adrenaline infusion 0.05–0.5 mcg/kg/min**, titrated, with continuous monitoring — by the Consultant
- **Add noradrenaline or vasopressin** if still hypotensive
- **Inj. Glucagon 1–2 mg IV** if the patient is on a beta-blocker and not responding
- Nebulised adrenaline 5 mL of 1:1000 for stridor as an adjunct; nebulised salbutamol for bronchospasm
- **Prepare for a difficult airway early** — senior anaesthetist, smaller tube, surgical airway kit open

**Second-line — after adrenaline, never instead of it**

- **Inj. Hydrocortisone 200 mg IV** (child 4 mg/kg) — no effect on the acute event
- **Inj. Pheniramine 22.5–45 mg IV** or chlorpheniramine; add an H2 blocker

**Observation and discharge**

- **Observe for at least 6 hours; 12–24 hours if severe, if the reaction was biphasic, if two or more adrenaline doses were needed, or if the patient is asthmatic or lives far away**
- **Discharge with two adrenaline auto-injectors where available, a written action plan, and an allergy clinic referral**
- **Record the culprit agent prominently in the case sheet and on the discharge card**; enter it in the hospital drug-allergy record

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Stridor, hoarseness or tongue swelling** — the airway is closing; call for anaesthesia now
- **Hypotension after any parenteral drug or contrast in the department**
- **No response after two IM doses of adrenaline** — refractory anaphylaxis, start an infusion
- **Known asthmatic with an allergic reaction** — highest risk of a fatal outcome
- **Sudden collapse on standing or sitting up** after apparent improvement
- **Return of symptoms 1–10 hours later** — biphasic reaction
- **Chest pain during the reaction** — Kounis syndrome, do an ECG

### Quick guide — mimics, and errors that recur

#### What else looks like this

- **Vasovagal syncope** — bradycardia, pallor, no urticaria, recovers on lying flat
- **Acute severe asthma** — no urticaria, no hypotension, a known pattern
- **Scombroid poisoning** — flushing and headache after oily fish; responds to antihistamine
- **Hereditary angio-oedema** — no urticaria, no itch, family history; adrenaline does not work, needs C1-esterase inhibitor or icatibant
- **ACE-inhibitor angio-oedema** — isolated lip and tongue swelling, may appear years after starting the drug
- **Panic attack** — a diagnosis of exclusion in the ED
- **Red-man syndrome** — vancomycin infused too fast; slow the rate, antihistamine

#### Errors seen repeatedly

- **Giving hydrocortisone and pheniramine first and adrenaline late** — this sequence is the commonest contributor to death
- **Waiting for hypotension** before giving adrenaline — airway or breathing involvement alone is enough
- **Giving IV 1:1000 adrenaline** — causes arrhythmia and myocardial ischaemia
- **Using the deltoid or subcutaneous route** — absorption is unreliable; use the anterolateral thigh
- **Under-filling** — anaphylactic shock is profoundly vasodilated and needs litres
- **Discharging at 2 hours** without an action plan or a prescription
- **Not documenting the culprit drug** — the next admission repeats the exposure

## 8.2 Sepsis and Septic Shock

Sepsis is **organ dysfunction caused by a dysregulated host response to infection**. Septic shock is sepsis with persisting hypotension requiring vasopressors to keep MAP ≥65 mmHg **and** a lactate above 2 mmol/L despite adequate fluid. Every hour of delay to effective antibiotics costs measurable survival.

### Salient history to be taken

- Site-directed symptoms: cough, dysuria, abdominal pain, headache, skin or joint pain, diarrhoea
- **Duration of fever, rigors, and any recent antibiotic** — partially treated sepsis presents atypically
- **Recent hospitalisation, surgery, catheter, line, prosthesis or instrumentation** — changes the likely organism
- **Immunosuppression:** chemotherapy, steroids, biologics, HIV, transplant, diabetes, cirrhosis, asplenia, CKD
- Travel and season — dengue, malaria, scrub typhus, leptospirosis, enteric fever
- Baseline functional status and comorbidity — this drives the ceiling-of-care discussion

*In the elderly, sepsis presents as a fall, confusion or "not himself", often without fever.*

### Salient clinical examination

- **Full set of vitals including a manual respiratory rate** — tachypnoea is the earliest sign and the most often unrecorded
- **qSOFA:** altered mentation, respiratory rate ≥22, systolic BP ≤100 — **two of three is a red flag, not a diagnostic test**
- **Perfusion:** capillary refill, mottling, peripheral temperature, urine output
- **Search every source:** chest, abdomen, flanks, skin and soft tissue including the perineum and between the toes, joints, neck stiffness, lines and cannula sites, any prosthesis
- **Look under the dressings and turn the patient** — sacral and perineal necrotising infection is missed by an examination done from the foot of the bed
- Purpura or a non-blanching rash — meningococcaemia, DIC

### Point-of-care investigations

- **ABG or VBG with LACTATE — the single most useful bedside test**
- **Blood cultures, two sets from separate sites, BEFORE antibiotics** — but never delay antibiotics beyond a few minutes to obtain them
- CBC with differential and platelets, RFT, LFT, coagulation profile, RBS
- Urine routine and culture; sputum; pus or wound swab; CSF if indicated
- Chest X-ray; POCUS for a source, for fluid responsiveness (IVC), and for cardiac function
- Procalcitonin where available — for de-escalation, not for the decision to start
- Malaria smear and rapid test, dengue NS1 and serology, scrub typhus IgM as the season dictates
- ECG — sepsis precipitates ischaemia and arrhythmia

### Basic initial management

**The one-hour bundle — start the clock at triage**

- **1. Measure lactate. Remeasure if the initial value is above 2 mmol/L**
- **2. Obtain blood cultures before antibiotics**
- **3. Give broad-spectrum antibiotics within 60 minutes** — see the syndromic guide at 11.5
- **4. Crystalloid 30 mL/kg for hypotension or lactate ≥4 mmol/L**, given in weighed boluses with reassessment after each
- **5. Vasopressors if MAP remains \<65 mmHg** despite fluid — **Inj. Noradrenaline 0.05–0.5 mcg/kg/min is first line**

*Balanced crystalloid is preferred to 0.9% saline. Do not use starches. Albumin only in selected cirrhotic or high-volume cases.*

**Beyond the bundle**

- **Source control within 6–12 hours** — drain the abscess, remove the line, decompress the obstructed kidney, debride the necrotising infection. **No antibiotic works around an undrained collection**
- **Add vasopressin** if noradrenaline requirement is escalating; **add dobutamine** for myocardial dysfunction
- **Inj. Hydrocortisone 200 mg/day** in shock refractory to fluid and vasopressors
- Target MAP ≥65 mmHg, urine output ≥0.5 mL/kg/h, falling lactate, improving capillary refill
- Glycaemic control 140–180 mg/dL; stress-ulcer and DVT prophylaxis; early enteral nutrition
- Lung-protective ventilation if intubated — 6 mL/kg predicted body weight

**Disposition and communication**

- **Vasopressor requirement, lactate \>4, or a falling GCS = ICU**
- **Discuss the ceiling of care early** with the family in the frail, the very elderly and the advanced-malignancy patient, and record the decision
- Hand over the antibiotic start time, cultures sent and source-control plan explicitly

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Lactate ≥4 mmol/L** — cryptic shock even with a normal blood pressure
- **MAP \<65 mmHg after 30 mL/kg fluid** — start noradrenaline, do not keep filling
- **Mottled skin, prolonged capillary refill, or cold peripheries with a normal BP**
- **Pain out of proportion, crepitus, bullae or skin necrosis** — necrotising fasciitis, surgical emergency
- **Purpuric rash with fever** — meningococcaemia; antibiotics immediately
- **Neutropenic, asplenic or cirrhotic patient with fever** — antibiotics on suspicion
- **Falling urine output or a rising creatinine** despite fluid
- **Any patient who deteriorates after apparently responding** — reassess the source

### Quick guide — fluid, vasopressors and the sources that get missed

#### Fluid and vasopressors

- **30 mL/kg is a starting point, not a prescription** — reassess after every 500 mL
- **Assess fluid responsiveness:** IVC collapsibility, passive leg raise, stroke volume change, or simply the response to a bolus
- **Stop filling** when the lungs develop B-lines or the IVC becomes plethoric — further fluid is now harmful
- **Noradrenaline is first line.** Start it through a good peripheral line if central access will take time, and watch the site
- Vasopressin 0.03 U/min added when noradrenaline exceeds about 0.25–0.5 mcg/kg/min
- **Cardiogenic component?** POCUS the heart before assuming it is all vasodilation
- **Hydrocortisone** for shock that persists on rising vasopressor doses

#### Sources found late, and mimics

**Sources found late**

- **Necrotising soft-tissue infection** — perineum, abdominal wall, limb; requires theatre, not antibiotics alone
- **Obstructed infected kidney** — needs urgent nephrostomy or stenting
- **Cholangitis** — Charcot triad; drainage is the treatment
- **Infected line or prosthesis**; **empyema**; **psoas or perinephric abscess**; **infective endocarditis**
- **Perianal or ischiorectal abscess** in the diabetic — examine the perineum

**Sepsis mimics**

- Pulmonary embolism \| Adrenal crisis \| Thyroid storm \| Pancreatitis \| DKA \| Anaphylaxis \| Haemorrhagic shock \| Drug reaction with eosinophilia \| Neuroleptic malignant and serotonin syndromes

## 8.3 Hyperkalaemia

Hyperkalaemia kills quickly and silently. **The ECG, not the laboratory value, decides how fast you act** — and a normal ECG does not exclude danger when the potassium is rising rapidly.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Known chronic kidney disease, missed dialysis session, or reduced urine output**
- **Drugs:** ACE inhibitors, ARBs, spironolactone, amiloride, NSAIDs, trimethoprim, heparin, tacrolimus, beta-blockers, digoxin, potassium supplements, salt substitutes
- **Tissue breakdown:** crush injury, burns, rhabdomyolysis, tumour lysis, massive haemolysis, prolonged immobility after a fall
- Diabetes — type 4 renal tubular acidosis, and DKA
- Addison disease or steroid withdrawal
- Recent large transfusion of stored blood

*Symptoms are non-specific and late: weakness, palpitations, paraesthesia, nausea. Cardiac arrest may be the first presentation.*

### Salient clinical examination

- **Attach the cardiac monitor before anything else**
- Pulse — bradycardia or an irregular rhythm
- **Muscle power** — ascending flaccid weakness, hyporeflexia
- Volume status, urine output, bladder scan for retention
- Dialysis access — examine the fistula for a thrill
- Look for the cause: crush injury, ischaemic limb, large haematoma, sacral pressure sore

### Point-of-care investigations

- **ECG IMMEDIATELY — and repeat it after treatment**
  - **Tall, peaked, narrow-based T waves → flattened or absent P waves → prolonged PR → widened QRS → sine wave → asystole or VF**
- **VBG or ABG gives a potassium result in minutes** — do not wait for the laboratory sample
- Serum potassium, sodium, calcium, magnesium, bicarbonate, urea, creatinine
- RBS; CPK if rhabdomyolysis is possible; urine output measurement
- Digoxin level if on digoxin

*Consider pseudohyperkalaemia — haemolysed sample, prolonged tourniquet, fist clenching, very high platelet or white cell count. Repeat the sample, but treat the patient in the meantime if the ECG is abnormal.*

### Basic initial management

**Step 1 — Stabilise the myocardium (immediately, if there are ECG changes)**

- **Inj. Calcium gluconate 10%, 10 mL IV over 10 minutes** — repeat every 5–10 minutes until the ECG improves
- *Calcium chloride 10%, 5–10 mL, through a central line if peri-arrest*

*Calcium does not lower potassium. It buys 30–60 minutes. In digoxin toxicity give it slowly and diluted, but do give it.*

**Step 2 — Shift potassium into cells**

- **Inj. Regular Insulin 10 units IV with Inj. Dextrose 25%, 100 mL IV over 30 minutes** — omit the dextrose only if RBS \>250 mg/dL
  - **Recheck RBS at 30, 60 and 120 minutes. Hypoglycaemia is the commonest complication and is often delayed**
- **Nebulised Salbutamol 10–20 mg (4–8 respules) over 10 minutes** — additive to insulin
- *Inj. Sodium bicarbonate 50–100 mEq IV over 30 minutes — only when there is significant metabolic acidosis*

**Step 3 — Remove potassium from the body**

- **Inj. Furosemide 40–80 mg IV** — only if the patient makes urine and is not hypovolaemic
- **Oral or rectal potassium binder** — sodium polystyrene sulphonate, or a newer binder where available
- **HAEMODIALYSIS — the only reliable removal in oliguria or anuria. Inform Nephrology early, not after everything else has failed**

**Then**

- **Stop every contributing drug** and record why
- Treat the cause: fluid for rhabdomyolysis, relieve obstruction, treat the crush injury, steroid for adrenal insufficiency
- **Repeat the potassium at 2 and 4 hours** — the shift is temporary and rebound is expected
- Admit any patient with ECG changes, a potassium above 6.5, or renal failure

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Any QRS widening, sine-wave pattern, or bradycardia with a high potassium** — give calcium now, and call for dialysis
- **Potassium ≥6.5 mEq/L**, whatever the ECG shows
- **Anuria or a missed dialysis session** — medical measures will only postpone the problem
- **Crush injury, compartment syndrome or rhabdomyolysis** — potassium will keep rising
- **Bradycardia unresponsive to atropine** — think hyperkalaemia
- **Cardiac arrest in a dialysis patient** — give calcium, insulin–dextrose and bicarbonate during CPR

### Quick guide — timing, and the pitfalls

#### How fast each measure works

- **Calcium:** onset 1–3 minutes, lasts 30–60 minutes, **does not lower potassium**
- **Insulin with dextrose:** onset 15–30 minutes, peak at 1 hour, lasts 4–6 hours, lowers potassium by roughly 0.5–1.2 mEq/L
- **Salbutamol nebulisation:** onset 30 minutes, lowers by roughly 0.5–1 mEq/L; about 20% of patients do not respond
- **Bicarbonate:** slow and unreliable unless acidotic
- **Furosemide:** depends entirely on urine output
- **Binders:** hours; useful for the next 24 hours, not for the next 20 minutes
- **Dialysis:** definitive — potassium falls within the first hour

#### Pitfalls

- **Treating the number and ignoring the ECG**, or the reverse — both matter
- **Giving insulin and sending the patient to the ward without glucose monitoring** — delayed hypoglycaemia is a recognised cause of death
- **Forgetting to repeat the potassium** — shifting is temporary
- **Assuming a haemolysed sample** when the patient has renal failure and an abnormal ECG
- **Using calcium chloride peripherally** — severe tissue necrosis on extravasation
- **Delaying the call to Nephrology** until the third failed medical attempt
- **Succinylcholine for intubation in a hyperkalaemic or crush-injury patient** — use rocuronium instead

## 8.4 Burns

A burn is a trauma call. **Airway and inhalational injury kill early; under-resuscitation and infection kill later.** Estimate the burn area honestly — over-estimation leads to fluid overload, which is now a commoner cause of harm than under-resuscitation.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Mechanism:** flame, scald, contact, chemical, electrical, friction
- **Closed space, smoke, or loss of consciousness at the scene** — predicts inhalational injury and carbon monoxide poisoning
- **Time of injury** — the fluid clock runs from the time of burn, not from arrival
- **Chemical:** the exact agent, concentration and contact time
- **Electrical:** voltage, entry and exit points, whether the patient was thrown, tetany or loss of consciousness
- First aid already given — cooling, ice (harmful), traditional applications
- Tetanus status; comorbidity; pregnancy
- **Circumstances — is the history consistent with the pattern?** Consider assault, self-harm and non-accidental injury, and document objectively

### Salient clinical examination

**Airway first**

- **Hoarseness, stridor, soot in the nostrils or mouth, singed nasal hairs, facial burns, carbonaceous sputum, dysphagia, a burn sustained in an enclosed space**
- **Estimate the burn area** — Wallace rule of nines in adults; the patient’s own palm with fingers is about 1%; use a Lund and Browder chart in children
- **Count only partial and full-thickness burns. Do not count simple erythema**

**Depth**

- **Superficial:** erythema, painful, blanches, no blister
- **Superficial partial:** blisters, moist, very painful, blanches
- **Deep partial:** mottled, less painful, sluggish blanching
- **Full thickness:** dry, leathery, white or charred, painless, does not blanch
- **Circumferential burns** of a limb, digit, neck or chest — check distal pulses and chest expansion
- Complete a full trauma secondary survey — burns distract from fractures and blast injury

### Point-of-care investigations

- ABG **with carboxyhaemoglobin and lactate** — a high lactate with a normal-looking patient suggests cyanide toxicity in a house fire
- **Pulse oximetry is falsely normal in carbon monoxide poisoning**
- CBC, RFT, electrolytes, RBS, coagulation, group and cross-match
- **ECG and cardiac monitoring for every electrical burn**; CPK and urine myoglobin
- Chest X-ray; other trauma imaging as indicated
- Weigh the patient, or use a documented estimate — all fluid calculations depend on it
- **Photograph the burns with consent for the record and for the referral**

### Basic initial management

**Immediate**

- **Stop the burning process** — remove clothing and jewellery; brush off dry chemical, then **irrigate chemical burns copiously for at least 20 minutes**
- **Cool the burn with running water at about 15°C for 20 minutes — within the first 3 hours. NEVER use ice**
- **Then keep the patient warm** — hypothermia is a real and rapid danger
- **Intubate early if there is any airway concern** — the airway will only get worse, and late intubation is far more difficult. Use an uncut tube
- **High-flow oxygen for every enclosed-space fire** — it is the treatment for carbon monoxide

**Fluid — Parkland formula**

- **Total for the first 24 hours = 4 mL × body weight (kg) × %TBSA burned, of Ringer’s lactate**
  - **Half in the first 8 hours from the TIME OF BURN**, the remaining half over the next 16 hours
  - **Children additionally need maintenance fluid with dextrose**
  - **The formula is a starting point. Titrate to urine output: 0.5 mL/kg/h in adults, 1 mL/kg/h in children, and 1–1.5 mL/kg/h with myoglobinuria**
- **Two large-bore cannulae, through unburned skin if possible; urinary catheter for any burn above 20%**

**Wound and other care**

- **Analgesia — IV opioid, titrated. Burns are extremely painful and are routinely under-treated**
- Cover with clean cling film lengthwise or a sterile sheet; do not wrap circumferentially
- **Tetanus prophylaxis**; **no prophylactic systemic antibiotic**
- **Escharotomy for circumferential burns** compromising perfusion or ventilation — surgical decision, do not delay
- Nasogastric tube for burns above 20%; early enteral feeding
- Cyanide antidote (hydroxocobalamin) for a house fire with unexplained acidosis and a normal saturation

**Referral to a burns unit**

- **Partial thickness \>10% (\>5% in a child or the elderly); any full-thickness burn; burns of face, hands, feet, genitalia, perineum or over a major joint; circumferential burns; electrical or chemical burns; inhalational injury; burns with trauma or significant comorbidity; any suspicion of non-accidental injury**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Hoarseness, stridor, soot or facial burns** — intubate before transfer
- **Burn sustained in an enclosed space, or a period of unconsciousness** — carbon monoxide and cyanide
- **Circumferential burn of chest, neck or limb** — escharotomy
- **High-voltage electrical injury** — arrhythmia, rhabdomyolysis, compartment syndrome, occult deep injury
- **Dark or tea-coloured urine** — myoglobinuria; increase fluid and target a higher urine output
- **Hypotension in the first hour** — burns do not cause early shock; **look for haemorrhage or another injury**
- **A pattern that does not fit the history**, delayed presentation, or a child with a symmetrical immersion pattern — escalate for safeguarding

### Quick guide — special burns, and errors that recur

#### Special situations

- **Electrical, high voltage:** the visible wound underestimates the injury. Cardiac monitoring, aggressive fluid for rhabdomyolysis, watch for compartment syndrome, look for a fall-related fracture
- **Chemical:** irrigate first and irrigate long. **Do not attempt neutralisation.** Hydrofluoric acid needs calcium gluconate gel and possibly intra-arterial calcium
- **Carbon monoxide:** cherry-red skin is unreliable; oximetry reads normal. Treat with 100% oxygen; hyperbaric therapy is rarely available and rarely needed urgently
- **Cyanide:** house fire, coma, profound lactic acidosis — hydroxocobalamin
- **Bitumen:** cool it, then remove with paraffin — never peel it off
- **Paediatric:** different surface proportions, faster hypothermia, low threshold for safeguarding

#### Errors that recur

- **Applying ice** — deepens the burn
- **Counting erythema in the %TBSA** — leads to gross over-resuscitation
- **Starting the fluid clock at arrival** rather than at the time of injury
- **Following the formula blindly** instead of titrating to urine output — "fluid creep" causes compartment syndrome and pulmonary oedema
- **Delaying intubation** in a facial or inhalational burn until the airway is already swollen
- **Prophylactic antibiotics** — they select resistance and do not prevent infection
- **Under-dosing analgesia**, particularly in children
- **Not documenting the mechanism and the pattern** where the history is inconsistent

## 8.5 Acute Limb Ischaemia

A time-critical, limb-threatening diagnosis made **entirely on clinical examination**. The window is roughly **6 hours** from onset. Do not send an ischaemic limb for a scan before informing the vascular surgeon.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Exact time of onset** — sudden onset suggests embolus; more gradual onset on a background of claudication suggests thrombosis
- **Atrial fibrillation, recent myocardial infarction, prosthetic valve, aneurysm** — embolic sources
- Previous claudication, rest pain, bypass graft or stent — suggests thrombosis in situ
- Trauma, arterial puncture, intra-arterial injection, recent angiography
- Anticoagulant use and compliance; known thrombophilia or malignancy
- **Back or abdominal pain with bilateral leg symptoms** — think aortic dissection or saddle embolus
- Intravenous drug use — inadvertent intra-arterial injection

### Salient clinical examination

**The six P’s — in order of appearance**

- **Pain** — constant, severe, worse on passive movement
- **Pallor** — then mottling; **fixed, non-blanching mottling means the limb is already lost**
- **Pulselessness** — examine and mark every level, and compare with the other limb
- **Perishing cold** — note where the temperature change begins; it marks the level
- **Paraesthesia** — loss of light touch is the earliest reliable sign of threat
- **Paralysis** — a late and ominous sign

*Also examine: the heart for atrial fibrillation, the abdomen for an aneurysm, and both limbs — always compare.*

- **Handheld Doppler at every level, arterial and venous** — an absent arterial signal with a present venous signal means threatened; both absent means irreversible

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Handheld Doppler is the key bedside investigation**
- **ECG** — atrial fibrillation, recent infarct
- CBC, RFT, electrolytes, **CPK**, coagulation profile, group and cross-match, ABG with lactate
- **Potassium and CPK before and after any revascularisation** — reperfusion injury is predictable
- *CT angiography if the limb is viable or marginally threatened and the diagnosis is uncertain — but never delay a vascular referral to obtain it*
- Echocardiography later for the embolic source; POCUS the aorta at the bedside

### Basic initial management

**Immediate — within minutes**

- **INFORM THE VASCULAR SURGEON BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE. Time is the only variable you control**
- **Inj. Heparin 5000 units IV bolus, then an infusion** targeting an APTT of 2–2.5 times control — unless there is active bleeding or suspected dissection, and after discussion
- **Analgesia — IV opioid, titrated**
- **Oxygen; IV fluid to maintain perfusion and renal output**
- **Keep the limb dependent, at room temperature, and protected. Do NOT elevate it, and do NOT apply heat or ice**
- Nil by mouth; prepare for theatre; consent and cross-match

**Classification determines the pathway (Rutherford)**

- **Class I — viable:** no sensory or motor loss, both Doppler signals audible → imaging and planned revascularisation
- **Class IIa — marginally threatened:** minimal sensory loss, no motor deficit, arterial signal absent → urgent revascularisation
- **Class IIb — immediately threatened:** sensory loss beyond the toes, mild to moderate weakness, rest pain → **emergency revascularisation, no time for a scan**
- **Class III — irreversible:** profound anaesthesia, paralysis, fixed mottling, muscle rigidity, absent venous signal → **revascularisation is dangerous; amputation, and counsel the family**

**Anticipate after revascularisation**

- **Reperfusion syndrome:** hyperkalaemia, acidosis, myoglobinuria and acute kidney injury — monitor potassium, alkalinise, maintain a brisk urine output
- **Compartment syndrome** — fasciotomy is often needed; check compartments repeatedly
- Treat the underlying source: anticoagulation for atrial fibrillation, aneurysm repair, secondary prevention

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Sensory loss or weakness** — the limb is immediately threatened; theatre, not radiology
- **Fixed, non-blanching mottling with muscle rigidity** — irreversible; revascularisation may kill the patient
- **Absent venous Doppler signal**
- **Bilateral leg ischaemia with back or abdominal pain** — aortic dissection or saddle embolus
- **A cold, pulseless limb in a patient with a bypass graft** — graft occlusion, still an emergency
- **Rising potassium or a falling urine output after revascularisation**
- **Pain out of proportion with a tense compartment after reperfusion** — fasciotomy

### Quick guide — what else it could be, and how the pitfalls arise

#### Differential diagnosis

- **Acute deep vein thrombosis (phlegmasia cerulea dolens)** — swollen, blue, painful; pulses may be lost secondarily
- **Aortic dissection with limb malperfusion** — check blood pressure in all four limbs
- **Compartment syndrome** after trauma or a crush
- **Acute cord compression or cauda equina** — bilateral weakness but warm, well-perfused limbs with pulses present
- **Peripheral neuropathy or radiculopathy** — pulses present
- **Vasospasm** — ergot, cocaine, inadvertent intra-arterial injection
- **Necrotising infection** — systemically unwell, skin changes, crepitus

#### How limbs are lost in the ED

- **Attributing the pain to arthritis, gout or "poor circulation"** in the elderly patient who cannot describe the onset
- **Not palpating the other limb** — the comparison is the examination
- **Not using the handheld Doppler** because the pulse "felt present"
- **Sending the patient for CT angiography before calling the vascular surgeon**
- **Elevating the limb** or applying a warm compress — both worsen ischaemia
- **Withholding analgesia** while awaiting the surgical opinion
- **Missing the reperfusion phase** — the potassium rises after the limb is saved
- **Failing to anticoagulate** while arrangements are being made

## 8.6 Hypertensive Emergency and Urgency

The number on the monitor is not the diagnosis. **A hypertensive emergency is a high blood pressure with acute target-organ damage; without organ damage it is urgency, and it is treated slowly and orally.** Rapid lowering of an asymptomatic high reading causes strokes.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Symptoms of target-organ damage:** chest pain, breathlessness, orthopnoea, headache, visual disturbance, focal weakness, seizure, confusion, oliguria, tearing back pain
- Antihypertensive drugs — **and specifically whether doses have been missed**; abrupt clonidine or beta-blocker withdrawal
- Sympathomimetics: cocaine, amphetamine, decongestants, MAO inhibitor with tyramine
- NSAIDs, steroids, erythropoietin, oral contraceptives
- **Pregnancy or delivery within the last 6 weeks** — pre-eclampsia and eclampsia
- Renal disease, previous stroke, known aortic disease
- Episodic headache with palpitations and sweating — phaeochromocytoma
- Pain, anxiety, urinary retention or alcohol withdrawal — **common reversible causes of a high reading in the ED**

### Salient clinical examination

- **Repeat the blood pressure yourself, in both arms, with a correctly sized cuff, after the patient has rested** — an undersized cuff and an anxious patient explain many "emergencies"
- **Fundoscopy** — flame haemorrhages, exudates, papilloedema define hypertensive retinopathy and change the diagnosis
- Neurological examination — GCS, focal deficit, visual fields
- Cardiovascular — third and fourth heart sounds, murmur, JVP, basal crackles, **radio-radial and radio-femoral delay, pulse deficit**
- Abdomen — renal bruit, palpable kidneys, bladder distension
- Peripheral oedema; signs of pregnancy

### Point-of-care investigations

- **ECG** — ischaemia, left ventricular hypertrophy, strain
- **Urine routine** — proteinuria, haematuria, red cell casts
- RFT and electrolytes; CBC with a **peripheral smear for schistocytes** (microangiopathic haemolysis)
- Troponin; chest X-ray for pulmonary oedema and a widened mediastinum
- **NCCT head** if there are neurological symptoms or signs
- **CT aortogram** if the pain suggests dissection
- Urine pregnancy test in every woman of childbearing age; **urine protein in pregnancy**
- POCUS — B-lines, cardiac function, IVC, aorta

### Basic initial management

**First decide which of the two this is**

- **EMERGENCY — acute target-organ damage present:** admit to a monitored bed and use IV therapy
- **URGENCY — no target-organ damage:** **do NOT lower it rapidly**. Restart or adjust oral therapy, treat pain, anxiety and retention, and arrange outpatient review within 24–72 hours

***Sublingual nifedipine is not to be used.*** It causes an uncontrolled fall in pressure and has caused stroke and infarction.

**General target in a hypertensive emergency**

- **Reduce the mean arterial pressure by no more than 20–25% in the first hour**, then to about 160/100 over the next 2–6 hours, then normalise over 24–48 hours

**Condition-specific targets — these override the general rule**

- **Aortic dissection:** **the most aggressive target — systolic 100–120 mmHg and heart rate \<60 within 20 minutes.** Rate control FIRST with esmolol or labetalol, then a vasodilator. Never a vasodilator alone
- **Acute pulmonary oedema (SCAPE):** **nitroglycerine infusion plus NIV** — these are the treatment; a diuretic is secondary
- **Acute coronary syndrome:** nitroglycerine and a beta-blocker; avoid a fall in diastolic pressure below about 60
- **Acute ischaemic stroke:** **permissive hypertension — treat only if \>220/120, or \>185/110 when thrombolysis is planned**
- **Intracerebral haemorrhage:** lower systolic carefully to 130–140 mmHg
- **Hypertensive encephalopathy:** lower MAP by 20–25% in the first hour; the diagnosis is confirmed by improvement
- **Eclampsia and pre-eclampsia:** **Inj. Magnesium sulphate for seizure prophylaxis and treatment**, plus labetalol, hydralazine or oral nifedipine; target 140–150/90–100. **Delivery is definitive**
- **Phaeochromocytoma or cocaine:** **alpha blockade first (phentolamine); a beta-blocker alone is dangerous.** Benzodiazepines are first line in cocaine toxicity

**Drugs**

- **Inj. Labetalol 20 mg IV bolus, doubled every 10 minutes to a maximum of 300 mg, or an infusion 0.5–2 mg/min** — avoid in asthma, bradycardia and decompensated failure
- **Nitroglycerine infusion 5–200 mcg/min** — pulmonary oedema and ACS
- **Inj. Hydralazine 5–10 mg IV** — pregnancy
- **Inj. Esmolol** — dissection, when rapid titration is needed
- *Sodium nitroprusside where available, with cyanide caution*

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Tearing chest or back pain with a pulse deficit or blood pressure differential** — aortic dissection until excluded
- **Breathlessness with basal crackles and a sudden rise in pressure** — SCAPE; nitrates and NIV, not just frusemide
- **Papilloedema, seizure or confusion** — hypertensive encephalopathy
- **Focal neurological deficit** — do not lower the pressure until the CT is done
- **Pregnancy or the puerperium with a high blood pressure** — magnesium sulphate and obstetric involvement
- **Schistocytes with a rising creatinine** — hypertensive microangiopathy
- **A very high reading in an asymptomatic patient** — the red flag here is the temptation to treat it fast

### Quick guide — sorting emergency from urgency, and the errors

#### What makes it an emergency

- **Brain:** encephalopathy, intracerebral haemorrhage, subarachnoid haemorrhage, ischaemic stroke with an indication to treat
- **Heart:** acute coronary syndrome, acute pulmonary oedema
- **Aorta:** dissection
- **Kidney:** acute kidney injury, haematuria, red cell casts
- **Eye:** grade III or IV retinopathy, papilloedema
- **Blood:** microangiopathic haemolysis with thrombocytopenia
- **Pregnancy:** pre-eclampsia with severe features, eclampsia, HELLP

*If none of these is present, the patient does not need an intravenous drug today.*

#### Errors that cause harm

- **Treating the number** in an asymptomatic patient and causing watershed infarction
- **Sublingual nifedipine** — abandoned for good reason
- **Lowering the pressure in an acute ischaemic stroke** outside the accepted thresholds
- **Giving a vasodilator before rate control in dissection** — reflex tachycardia increases aortic shear stress
- **Beta-blocker alone in cocaine or phaeochromocytoma** — unopposed alpha stimulation
- **Missing pain, retention, anxiety or alcohol withdrawal** as the actual cause of the reading
- **Missing pregnancy** — always do the test
- **Discharging without a follow-up plan or a clear medication instruction**

## 8.7 The Agitated, Aggressive or Violent Patient

Agitation is a **symptom, not a diagnosis**. The first duty is to keep the patient, the staff and other patients safe; the second is to find the medical cause, because assuming a psychiatric cause is how hypoglycaemia, hypoxia and head injury are missed.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Obtain a collateral history — from family, police, ambulance staff or the accompanying person**. The patient may be unable to give one
- Onset and course — **abrupt onset with fluctuation suggests delirium and a medical cause**
- **Head injury, fall, or recent illness**
- **Substance use:** alcohol (intoxication and withdrawal), cannabis, opioids, stimulants, benzodiazepine withdrawal, inhalants
- Prescribed drugs — anticholinergics, steroids, dopaminergics, recent changes, missed antipsychotic doses
- Known psychiatric illness, previous episodes, previous violence, and what worked before
- Diabetes, epilepsy, dementia, HIV, liver or renal disease, hypoxic lung disease

### Salient clinical examination

**Before approaching**

- **Position yourself between the patient and the exit; remove ligatures and objects that can be thrown; call security early rather than late; never see the patient alone**
- **CAPILLARY GLUCOSE AND SpO₂ IN EVERY AGITATED PATIENT** — these two tests are non-negotiable
- Vital signs including temperature — **fever with agitation is a medical emergency until proved otherwise**
- Pupils, focal neurological signs, gait, tremor, myoclonus, rigidity, nystagmus
- Head and scalp for injury; smell for alcohol or ketones; skin for track marks, patches, rash
- **Assess the level of agitation objectively** and record it, so response to treatment can be judged

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS and pulse oximetry first**
- ECG — **QTc before and after any antipsychotic**; also for stimulant toxicity
- Electrolytes including sodium and calcium, RFT, LFT, CBC, ammonia if liver disease
- ABG if hypoxia or hypercapnia is possible; CPK if restraint has been prolonged or rigidity is present
- Urine routine and culture — **urinary infection is a very common precipitant in the elderly**
- Toxicology screen and alcohol level where it will change management
- **NCCT head** for head injury, focal signs, a first episode, anticoagulation, or failure to settle
- Thyroid function; consider lumbar puncture if fever with altered sensorium

### Basic initial management

**Step 1 — De-escalation, always first**

- **Move to a quiet, low-stimulus area; one person speaks; introduce yourself by name; keep a calm tone and open posture; maintain a safe distance**
- Offer food, water, a blanket, a phone call, analgesia and the toilet — unmet needs drive much of the agitation seen in the ED
- Offer oral medication as a choice before compulsion

**Step 2 — Oral medication if the patient will accept it**

- **Tab. Lorazepam 1–2 mg PO**, *or* **Tab. Olanzapine 5–10 mg (dispersible)**, *or* **Tab. Risperidone 1–2 mg**

**Step 3 — Parenteral, when there is a risk of harm**

- **Inj. Lorazepam 1–2 mg IM/IV** — the safest choice when the cause is unknown, and the drug of choice in **alcohol and benzodiazepine withdrawal and in stimulant toxicity**
- **Inj. Haloperidol 2.5–5 mg IM** — *check the QTc; avoid in the elderly, in Parkinson disease and in Lewy body dementia*
- **Inj. Olanzapine 10 mg IM** — *do not combine with parenteral benzodiazepine, because of respiratory depression*
- **Inj. Ketamine 4–5 mg/kg IM** — for extreme agitation endangering life, **with airway equipment and monitoring immediately available, and by the Consultant**
- **Halve the dose in the elderly**

**Step 4 — Physical restraint, if unavoidable**

- **Restraint is a medical intervention, and it needs an order, a reason and a time limit** — record all three
- **Five trained persons, one per limb and one for the head. Never restrain prone or with weight on the chest — positional asphyxia is fatal**
- **Continuous observation with monitoring; document neurovascular checks, position and vital signs every 15 minutes**
- **Review and release at the earliest safe moment.** Restraint is not a place to leave a patient
- Give parenteral medication so that restraint can be released quickly

**Then**

- **Complete the medical workup that agitation prevented** — sedation is not the end of the assessment
- Treat the cause: glucose, oxygen, thiamine, antibiotics, analgesia, catheterisation
- **Psychiatric assessment for every deliberate self-harm, every first psychotic episode, and every patient expressing intent to harm themselves or others**
- **Document objectively, in behavioural terms, the events, the intervention, the times and the response.** Follow hospital policy on informing security and, where required, the police

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Hypoglycaemia or hypoxia** — the two causes never to be missed
- **Fever with agitation** — meningitis, encephalitis, sepsis, thyroid storm, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, serotonin syndrome, anticholinergic toxicity, heat stroke
- **Abnormal vital signs of any kind** — this is not a primary psychiatric presentation
- **Focal neurological signs, a new headache, or a head injury**
- **First episode of agitation over the age of 40** — look hard for an organic cause
- **Rigidity with fever after an antipsychotic** — neuroleptic malignant syndrome; stop the drug, cool, dantrolene
- **Autonomic instability with clonus** — serotonin syndrome
- **Prolonged restraint with a rising CPK** — rhabdomyolysis and acidosis

### Quick guide — organic causes to exclude, and the standard for restraint

#### Organic causes of agitation

- **Metabolic:** hypoglycaemia, hyponatraemia, hypercalcaemia, uraemia, hepatic encephalopathy, thyroid storm
- **Hypoxia and hypercapnia** — including COPD and pulmonary embolism
- **Infection:** urinary tract infection in the elderly, pneumonia, meningitis, encephalitis, sepsis, HIV, cerebral malaria
- **Neurological:** head injury, subdural haematoma, stroke, seizure and the post-ictal state, non-convulsive status, raised intracranial pressure, dementia with delirium
- **Toxicological:** alcohol intoxication and withdrawal, stimulants, anticholinergics, serotonin and neuroleptic malignant syndromes, steroid psychosis
- **Other:** pain, urinary retention, faecal impaction, hypothermia, heat stroke, postoperative delirium

#### Restraint — the standard the department is held to

- **Least restrictive measure that is effective**, for the shortest possible time
- **A doctor’s written order with a stated indication and time limit**; verbal orders documented immediately
- **Never prone, never with pressure on the chest or neck**
- **Continuous one-to-one observation**, with monitoring where sedation has been given
- **Documented checks every 15 minutes:** limb perfusion, position, vital signs, level of consciousness, hydration and toileting
- **Regular review for release**, recorded each time
- **Debrief the patient afterwards** and record the explanation given
- **Inform the family**, and report the episode through the hospital incident system

*NABH assesses this documentation directly. A restrained patient without an order and observation record is a finding against the department.*

## 8.8 Acute Transfusion Reaction

**The first action in every suspected transfusion reaction is the same: stop the transfusion, keep the line open with normal saline through a fresh set, and check the identity on the unit against the patient.** Only then decide what kind of reaction it is.

### Salient history to be taken

- Which component, unit number, and how long it has been running
- **How much volume has been transfused** — severity in haemolytic reactions correlates with volume
- Timing of symptom onset relative to the start of the transfusion
- Previous transfusions and previous reactions; number of prior pregnancies
- Premedication given, if any
- Indication for the transfusion, and whether the patient is bleeding
- Known IgA deficiency or a history of anaphylaxis to blood products

### Salient clinical examination

- **Check the patient’s identity band against the unit label and the cross-match report — clerical error is the commonest cause of a fatal reaction**
- Vitals before, during and after; **compare with the pre-transfusion baseline**
- **Fever and rigors; hypotension; tachycardia; tachypnoea**
- Chest, back or infusion-site pain; a feeling of impending doom
- Urticaria, angio-oedema, wheeze, stridor
- **Colour of the urine — red or brown suggests haemoglobinuria**
- Chest auscultation for crackles; JVP; SpO₂
- Oozing from puncture sites — disseminated intravascular coagulation

### Point-of-care investigations

- **STOP the transfusion first. Do not discard the unit and giving set — return them to the blood bank with a fresh sample**
- **Repeat blood group and direct Coombs test; send a post-transfusion sample from the opposite arm**
- CBC, LDH, bilirubin (indirect), haptoglobin, reticulocyte count, peripheral smear
- **Free haemoglobin in plasma and urine; urine dipstick for blood with no red cells on microscopy**
- Coagulation profile, fibrinogen, D-dimer — for DIC
- RFT, electrolytes, potassium; ABG with lactate
- Culture the unit and the patient if sepsis is suspected
- Chest X-ray if breathless — **to distinguish TRALI from TACO**

### Basic initial management

**Universal first steps**

- **1. STOP the transfusion**
- **2. Maintain the line with normal saline using a NEW giving set**
- **3. Recheck the identity of the patient and the unit**
- **4. ABC, oxygen, monitor, inform the Consultant and the blood bank**
- **5. Send the samples above, and return the unit and set**

**Acute haemolytic reaction — ABO incompatibility**

- Fever, rigors, loin or chest pain, hypotension, dark urine, oozing
- **Aggressive fluid resuscitation; maintain urine output at 1–2 mL/kg/h**
- **Treat hyperkalaemia and DIC; vasopressors if needed; early dialysis for renal failure**
- **Report it as a serious incident — a clerical error affects the next patient too**

**Febrile non-haemolytic reaction**

- Temperature rise of 1°C or more with rigors, but no haemolysis — the commonest reaction
- **Inj. Paracetamol; avoid aspirin in thrombocytopenia**. Transfusion may be restarted cautiously after review; use leucodepleted components in future

**Allergic and anaphylactic reaction**

- Urticaria alone → antihistamine; the transfusion may be resumed slowly if it settles
- **Anaphylaxis → stop permanently, ADRENALINE 0.5 mg IM, and manage as protocol 8.1. Consider IgA deficiency and use washed components in future**

**TACO — circulatory overload**

- Breathlessness with **hypertension**, raised JVP, basal crackles, positive fluid balance; commonest in the elderly, small, or renal patient
- **Sit up, oxygen, Inj. Furosemide 40 mg IV, consider NIV.** Future transfusions slowly, one unit at a time, with a diuretic

**TRALI — transfusion-related acute lung injury**

- Breathlessness within 6 hours with **hypotension**, bilateral infiltrates and a **normal JVP**
- **Supportive: oxygen, lung-protective ventilation. Diuretics do NOT help and may harm.** Report to the blood bank — the donor must be deferred

**Bacterial contamination**

- **High fever, rigors and shock beginning very soon after starting the unit — especially platelets**
- **Broad-spectrum antibiotics immediately; culture the patient and the unit; treat as septic shock**

**Documentation**

- **Complete the hospital transfusion-reaction form for every reaction, however minor, and record it in the case sheet.** This is a mandatory NABH record

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Fever with loin or chest pain and dark urine** — acute haemolytic reaction, stop immediately
- **Hypotension within minutes of starting a unit** — haemolysis, anaphylaxis or bacterial contamination
- **Any reaction in an unconscious or anaesthetised patient** — hypotension and oozing may be the only signs
- **Breathlessness during or shortly after transfusion** — distinguish TACO from TRALI before giving a diuretic
- **Oozing from cannula sites** — DIC
- **Rigors and shock with a platelet transfusion** — assume bacterial contamination
- **A discrepancy between the identity band and the unit label** — stop, and check every other patient receiving blood at that moment

### Quick guide — telling the reactions apart

#### By timing and dominant feature

- **Minutes, with hypotension and loin pain:** acute haemolytic — ABO error
- **Minutes, with urticaria and wheeze:** allergic or anaphylactic
- **Within 30–60 minutes, fever and rigors alone:** febrile non-haemolytic
- **Within 30–60 minutes, high fever with shock:** bacterial contamination
- **Within 1–6 hours, breathless and hypertensive with a raised JVP:** TACO
- **Within 6 hours, breathless and hypotensive with a normal JVP:** TRALI
- **Days to weeks, falling haemoglobin with jaundice:** delayed haemolytic reaction
- **7–10 days, profound thrombocytopenia:** post-transfusion purpura

#### TACO versus TRALI — the distinction that changes treatment

**TACO**

- **Blood pressure: raised**  •  **JVP: raised**  •  **Fluid balance: positive**
- Responds to diuretics and sitting up \| Often elderly, cardiac or renal
- BNP typically elevated

**TRALI**

- **Blood pressure: low**  •  **JVP: normal**  •  **Fever often present**
- **Diuretics do not help; the patient needs oxygen and often ventilation**
- Bilateral infiltrates with no cardiac cause \| Usually resolves in 48–96 hours

*If you cannot tell, treat the airway and breathing, get a chest X-ray and an echocardiogram, and ask the Consultant — do not give a diuretic to a hypotensive patient.*

## 8.9 Needle-Stick and Blood or Body-Fluid Exposure

This protocol applies to **our own staff as well as to patients**. HIV post-exposure prophylaxis is most effective when started within hours — **ideally within 2 hours, and certainly within 72 hours**. The exposed person is usually distressed; handle the encounter accordingly.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Time of exposure** — the single most important item
- **Type of exposure:** percutaneous (needle, scalpel), mucous membrane (eye, mouth), non-intact skin, or bite
- **Type of device:** hollow-bore needle carries the highest risk; solid suture needle lower
- **Depth of injury, visible blood on the device, and whether the needle had been in the source’s artery or vein**
- Volume and type of fluid — **blood, semen, vaginal, CSF, synovial, pleural, peritoneal, pericardial and amniotic fluid are infectious; urine, faeces, sweat, tears, saliva and vomit are not, unless visibly bloodstained**
- **Source patient status:** HIV, hepatitis B, hepatitis C — known, unknown or untestable; viral load and treatment if known
- **Exposed person:** hepatitis B vaccination status and anti-HBs titre, pregnancy or breastfeeding, current medication, renal and liver function

### Salient clinical examination

- Examine the injury — site, depth, bleeding, contamination
- Assess for other injuries and for tetanus-prone wounds
- Baseline vital signs
- **Assess the psychological state — anxiety is universal and needs to be addressed openly**

*Document the incident factually and without attributing blame.*

### Point-of-care investigations

**From the exposed person, at baseline**

- **HIV, HBsAg with anti-HBs titre, anti-HCV; CBC, RFT, LFT; urine pregnancy test in a woman of childbearing age**

**From the source patient — with informed consent**

- **HIV, HBsAg, anti-HCV — request an urgent rapid HIV test**
- *If the source is HIV positive: viral load, current regimen and any known resistance*

*If the source is unknown or refuses testing, treat according to the risk of the exposure, not according to hope.*

**Follow-up testing of the exposed person**

- **HIV at 6 weeks, 3 months and 6 months; hepatitis C RNA at 4–6 weeks and antibody at 3 and 6 months; hepatitis B markers at 3 and 6 months**

### Basic initial management

**Immediate first aid — in the first minute**

- **Wash the wound with soap and running water; let it bleed gently**
- **Irrigate a mucous membrane or the eye with copious water or saline for 10–15 minutes; remove contact lenses and irrigate again**

***Do NOT squeeze the wound, do NOT scrub, and do NOT apply bleach, alcohol, iodine or any caustic agent to the puncture site.***

**HIV post-exposure prophylaxis**

- **Start within 2 hours if possible; up to 72 hours is still worthwhile. Do not wait for the source result if it will take hours — start, and stop later if the source is negative**
- **Standard regimen: Tenofovir 300 mg + Lamivudine 300 mg + Dolutegravir 50 mg, once daily for 28 days** (or the current regimen specified by the hospital ART policy)
- Counsel about nausea, headache and fatigue, and provide antiemetics — **the commonest reason for non-completion is untreated side effects**
- Advise barrier contraception and no blood donation until the 3-month test
- **Refer to the ART centre for continuation, adherence support and follow-up**

**Hepatitis B**

- **Vaccinated with a documented anti-HBs ≥10 mIU/mL: no action needed**
- **Unvaccinated, or a non-responder, with an HBsAg-positive or unknown source: Hepatitis B immunoglobulin 0.06 mL/kg IM as soon as possible — ideally within 24 hours and certainly within 7 days — plus start the vaccine series at a different site**
- **Vaccinated but titre unknown: check the titre; give a booster and, if the source is positive, immunoglobulin**

**Hepatitis C**

- **There is no prophylaxis and no vaccine.** Monitor with RNA testing at 4–6 weeks; **early treatment of acute infection is highly effective**, so the follow-up matters

**Administrative**

- **Report the incident on the hospital needle-stick form the same day**; inform the infection-control nurse
- Tetanus prophylaxis as indicated
- **Provide counselling and, where needed, time off. Maintain confidentiality about both the exposed person and the source**
- **Review the circumstances for prevention** — recapping, sharps disposal, availability of safety devices

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Deep percutaneous injury from a hollow-bore needle used in the source’s vein or artery** — highest transmission risk
- **Source known HIV positive with a high viral load, or with advanced disease**
- **Presentation beyond 72 hours** — prophylaxis is no longer indicated; arrange testing and counselling
- **Exposed person is pregnant or breastfeeding** — prophylaxis is still given; discuss the regimen with the ART centre
- **Unvaccinated staff member** — immunoglobulin is time-critical
- **A bite with blood exposure** — assess both persons
- **Repeat incidents in the same area of the department** — a system problem, not an individual one

### Quick guide — risk figures, and what not to do

#### Transmission risk per exposure

- **Hepatitis B, HBeAg positive source, percutaneous: up to 30%** — the highest risk of the three, and the one that is entirely preventable by vaccination
- **Hepatitis C, percutaneous: about 1.8%**
- **HIV, percutaneous: about 0.3%**
- **HIV, mucous membrane: about 0.09%**
- **HIV, intact skin: negligible**

**Factors that raise HIV risk**

- Deep injury \| Visible blood on the device \| Hollow-bore needle \| Needle from a vein or artery \| Source with advanced disease or a high viral load

#### What not to do

- **Do not squeeze or scrub the wound** — it increases local trauma and does not reduce risk
- **Do not apply bleach, spirit or iodine into the puncture**
- **Do not delay prophylaxis waiting for the source result** when the test will take hours
- **Do not test the source without consent**, and do not disclose the source’s status beyond those who need to know
- **Do not tell a colleague to "wait and see"** — the window closes
- **Do not recap needles**, and do not carry uncapped sharps across a room
- **Do not leave the incident unreported** because it seems minor — the record protects the staff member later

## 8.10 Common Paediatric Emergencies

Children compensate well and then decompensate suddenly. **Assess with the Paediatric Assessment Triangle from the doorway — appearance, work of breathing, circulation to skin — before touching the child.** Weight drives every dose: use a length-based tape, or **weight (kg) = 2 × (age in years + 4)** for ages 1 to 10.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Feeding and urine output — the most useful markers of illness severity in an infant** (wet nappies in 24 hours)
- Fever — height, duration, method of measurement; response to antipyretics
- Activity, alertness, and whether the child is "not himself" as judged by the parent — **take parental concern seriously**
- Vomiting, diarrhoea, rash, cough, stridor, work of breathing, seizure
- Birth history, prematurity, immunisation status, comorbidity
- Sick contacts; recent antibiotics; possible ingestion or foreign body
- **Age of the child — it changes the entire differential**, particularly under 3 months

### Salient clinical examination

**Paediatric Assessment Triangle — from the doorway**

- **Appearance:** tone, interactivity, consolability, gaze, speech or cry
- **Work of breathing:** nasal flaring, grunting, retractions, head bobbing, abnormal sounds
- **Circulation to skin:** pallor, mottling, cyanosis

**Then**

- **Full vitals with an age-appropriate cuff, and a respiratory rate counted over a full minute**
- **Capillary refill, peripheral and central temperature difference, skin turgor, sunken eyes, sunken fontanelle, moist or dry mucosa**
- **Fully undress the child** — look for a non-blanching rash, bruising and injury patterns
- Chest, abdomen, ears, throat, neck stiffness, hips and joints
- **Weigh the child**

### Point-of-care investigations

- **RBS in every unwell child** — children become hypoglycaemic quickly
- SpO₂ and, if unwell, a blood gas with lactate
- CBC, CRP, blood culture, electrolytes, RFT as the presentation dictates
- **Urine — by a clean method; urinary infection is a common occult source of fever in the young child**
- Chest X-ray only where it will change management
- **Lumbar puncture and a full septic screen in any febrile infant under 3 months who is unwell, and in any child with suspected meningitis**
- Dengue and malaria testing as the season dictates

### Basic initial management

**Fever in the child**

- **Under 28 days with fever: full septic screen and admit for IV antibiotics, regardless of how well the baby appears**
- **1–3 months: low threshold for a full septic screen and admission**
- **Over 3 months and well-appearing with a source: treat the source, antipyretics, oral fluids, written safety-netting advice**
