- **Paracetamol 15 mg/kg every 6 hours; ibuprofen 10 mg/kg every 8 hours if well-hydrated. Do not alternate routinely, and do not use aspirin**

**Dehydration and gastroenteritis**

- **No dehydration:** ORS after each stool; continue breastfeeding; **zinc 20 mg daily for 14 days (10 mg if under 6 months)**
- **Some dehydration:** **ORS 75 mL/kg over 4 hours** under observation, then reassess
- **Severe dehydration or shock:** **Ringer’s lactate 20 mL/kg rapid bolus**, repeated with reassessment after each; then correct the deficit; check glucose and potassium

*Ondansetron 0.15 mg/kg orally, given once, improves the success of oral rehydration in a vomiting child.*

**Bronchiolitis — typically under 2 years**

- **Supportive care is the treatment: oxygen to keep SpO₂ ≥92%, nasal suction, small frequent feeds, or nasogastric or IV fluid if feeding poorly**
- **Salbutamol, steroids, antibiotics and chest physiotherapy are NOT routinely indicated**
- Admit for hypoxia, apnoea, poor feeding, marked distress, age under 3 months, prematurity or comorbidity

**Croup**

- **Inj. or oral Dexamethasone 0.15–0.6 mg/kg, single dose, for every child including mild cases**
- **Nebulised adrenaline 0.5 mL/kg of 1:1000 (maximum 5 mL) for stridor at rest**, then observe for at least 3 hours
- **Keep the child calm and with the parent — distress worsens the obstruction. Do not examine the throat**

**Febrile seizure**

- Usually 6 months to 5 years, brief and generalised, with a rapid return to normal
- Protect, position, check glucose, control the fever, and **treat as status epilepticus if it lasts beyond 5 minutes** (protocol 4.2)
- **Look for the source of fever; lumbar puncture if meningitis is suspected or the seizure is complex**
- Reassure and educate the parents; a simple febrile seizure does not require an antiepileptic

**Safe discharge**

- **Tolerating oral fluids, passing urine, alert, parents confident and able to return, and written danger-sign advice given**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Any fever in an infant under 28 days**
- **Non-blanching rash with fever** — meningococcaemia; antibiotics before anything else
- **Grunting, head bobbing, or a silent chest** — imminent respiratory failure
- **A sudden fall in respiratory rate or effort in a tiring child** — this is exhaustion, not improvement
- **Bulging fontanelle, neck stiffness, or a high-pitched cry**
- **Mottled, cold peripheries with a capillary refill beyond 3 seconds**
- **Bile-stained vomiting** — malrotation with volvulus in an infant is a surgical emergency
- **Reduced urine output, sunken eyes, lethargy, or an inability to feed**
- **Injury pattern inconsistent with the history, or a delayed presentation** — escalate for safeguarding

### Quick guide — numbers to have at hand, and the errors

#### Paediatric quick reference

- **Weight (kg) = 2 × (age in years + 4)**, ages 1–10, or use a length-based tape
- **Fluid bolus: 20 mL/kg** isotonic crystalloid (**10 mL/kg** in cardiac disease, DKA or malnutrition)
- **Dextrose: 0.5–1 g/kg** — 10% dextrose 5–10 mL/kg in infants
- **Adrenaline in arrest: 0.01 mg/kg (0.1 mL/kg of 1:10,000)**
- **Adrenaline in anaphylaxis, IM: 0.01 mg/kg of 1:1000**, maximum 0.5 mg
- **Defibrillation: 2 J/kg, then 4 J/kg**
- **Endotracheal tube size (uncuffed) = age/4 + 4**; depth at the lip = tube size × 3
- **Normal systolic BP (minimum) = 70 + 2 × age in years**
- **Maintenance fluid: 4-2-1 rule** (4 mL/kg/h for the first 10 kg, 2 for the next 10, 1 thereafter)

#### Errors that recur in paediatric ED care

- **Not weighing the child** and estimating doses by eye
- **Reassuring on a single set of vitals** — children compensate, then crash; reassess and record
- **Dismissing parental concern** — "she is not herself" is a validated warning sign
- **Not undressing the child** and missing the rash, the bruise or the injury
- **Giving salbutamol and antibiotics for bronchiolitis** — neither helps
- **Examining the throat in suspected epiglottitis or severe croup**
- **Missing hypoglycaemia** in the drowsy or seizing child
- **Interpreting a falling respiratory rate as improvement** in a tiring child
- **Discharging without written safety-netting advice** the parent can act on at 2 a.m.

## 8.11 Brought Dead, Death in the ED, and Medico-legal Documentation

This is a **procedural and legal protocol as much as a clinical one**. Errors here cause distress to families and difficulty for the hospital months later. Every step must be documented at the time, not reconstructed afterwards.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Circumstances of the death** — from the family, the ambulance crew, or the police, recorded verbatim in quotation marks where relevant
- **Last seen alive, and by whom; time of collapse; whether CPR was given and for how long**
- Known illness, recent hospital admissions, current medication, and whether the patient was under treatment locally
- **Any history of trauma, poisoning, burns, drowning, hanging, assault, or an unnatural circumstance**
- For a woman of reproductive age, ask specifically about pregnancy and recent delivery — **maternal death has separate reporting obligations**
- Identity of the informant and their relationship to the deceased, with a contact number

### Salient clinical examination

**Confirm death formally, and record how**

- **No carotid pulse, no heart sounds, and no respiratory effort for a continuous period; pupils fixed and dilated; no response to a central painful stimulus**
- **A flat ECG rhythm strip recorded and attached to the file** — do not rely on the monitor display alone
- **Record the exact time of confirmation and the name of the doctor confirming it**

**Examine the body**

- **Rigor mortis, postmortem lividity and its distribution, body temperature** — these indicate the approximate interval since death
- **Any injury, ligature mark, burn, injection site, froth at the mouth, or unusual smell — describe it objectively; do not interpret or attribute a cause**
- **Do not wash the body, do not remove clothing, and do not remove any ligature, tube or line in a medico-legal case**

### Point-of-care investigations

- **No investigation is required to confirm death, other than the documented ECG strip**

*In a suspected medico-legal death, do not send samples on your own initiative — sampling is for the forensic autopsy.*

**What must be recorded**

- Time of arrival; whether the patient arrived with signs of life; whether resuscitation was attempted and for how long; the time death was confirmed
- Identity of the deceased and how it was established
- Names and designations of every member of the resuscitation team
- Whether the case is medico-legal, and the time and name of the police station informed

### Basic initial management

**If the patient arrives with any sign of life**

- **Resuscitate.** Brought dead is a determination made after assessment, never at the door and never by the security staff or the ambulance crew
- Continue until the criteria for terminating resuscitation are met, and record the decision and the reason

**Decide: is this a medico-legal case?**

- **Every one of the following is medico-legal and must be reported to the police, and a death certificate must NOT be issued by the hospital:**
  - **All trauma, including road traffic accidents and falls**
  - **Poisoning, suspected poisoning, and all snake and animal bites**
  - **Burns and scalds, and all electrical injury**
  - **Drowning, hanging, asphyxia**
  - **Suspected suicide, homicide, or assault**
  - **Death in police or judicial custody**
  - **Death during or shortly after a procedure or anaesthesia**
  - **Sudden, unexpected or unexplained death in a person not under treatment**
  - **Death within 24 hours of admission where the cause is unclear**
  - **Suspected maternal death, and death after an unsafe abortion**
  - **Any death where the relatives allege foul play or negligence**

**Natural death with a known illness under treatment**

- **Issue the death certificate in the prescribed form, giving the immediate cause, the antecedent cause and the underlying cause**
- Do not certify a cause you cannot support from the record

**Medico-legal death**

- **Inform the police station in whose jurisdiction the hospital lies, and record the time, the station and the name of the officer**
- **Preserve the body and all clothing and effects; hand over to the police with a signed inventory**
- **Do not release the body to the family; the police authorise release after the autopsy**
- Explain clearly and kindly to the family why an autopsy is required and that it is not a choice the hospital is making

**Care of the family**

- **Break the news in a private space, seated, with a nurse present, using plain and unambiguous words — "he has died", not "we lost him"**
- Allow the family to see the body, accompanied, wherever it is possible without disturbing evidence
- Offer the hospital chaplain or a religious representative, and respect the family’s religious practices where the law permits
- **Explain the next administrative steps in writing** — mortuary, certificate, autopsy, transport

**Also**

- **Ask about organ and tissue donation where the circumstances permit** and hospital policy provides for it — corneal donation is possible in most natural deaths
- **Debrief the resuscitation team**, particularly after a paediatric or a violent death
- **Complete the mortality register and the departmental audit form**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Declaring a patient brought dead without an examination and a documented ECG strip**
- **Issuing a death certificate in a medico-legal case** — the single most consequential error in this protocol
- **Washing the body or removing a ligature, tube or clothing** before police handover
- **Any unnatural circumstance, however minor it seems** — when in doubt, treat it as medico-legal and inform the police
- **Relatives alleging negligence or foul play** — report, document and inform the administration
- **A death within 24 hours of admission with no clear cause**
- **Death of a woman during pregnancy, delivery or within 42 days of delivery** — mandatory maternal death reporting

### Quick guide — the record, and how to break the news

#### The medico-legal record — what an assessor or a court will look for

- **Written contemporaneously, timed, signed, with the name and designation legible**
- **Objective description of injuries** — site, size, shape, colour, margins; **never an opinion on the weapon, the manner or the intent**
- **No erasures, no overwriting, no use of correction fluid.** A correction is a single line through the entry, initialled and timed
- **The ECG strip attached and labelled with the time**
- **The name of the police station, the time of intimation, and the officer’s name**
- **A signed inventory of clothing, valuables and effects handed over**
- **Consent, refusal and discharge-against-advice entries signed and witnessed**

*A record written a day later, however accurate, carries very little weight.*

#### Breaking the news

- **Prepare:** know the name, the relationship and the sequence of events before you enter the room
- **Private space, everyone seated, phones silenced, a nurse with you**
- **Warning shot:** "I am afraid I have very difficult news."
- **Use the words "died" or "dead".** Euphemisms are misunderstood, particularly across a language barrier
- **Then stop talking.** Allow silence; expect and permit grief in whatever form it takes
- **Answer the questions actually asked**, briefly and honestly; do not speculate on the cause
- **Do not say "there was nothing we could do"** — say what was done
- **Give the practical next steps in writing** and name one person the family can come back to

*Where the family does not speak the doctor’s language, use a staff interpreter, never a child of the family.*

## 8.12 Sexual Assault — Examination and Care

**Medical treatment comes first and is never conditional on a police complaint.** Under Indian law every hospital must provide free first aid and medical treatment to a survivor of sexual assault, and refusal to do so is itself a punishable offence. The examination is conducted only with **informed consent**, by a doctor of the survivor’s preferred gender wherever possible, with dignity and without any comment on character.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Take the history once, in a private room, with a support person of the survivor’s choosing present if they wish**
- **Record the survivor’s own words in quotation marks.** Do not paraphrase and do not editorialise
- Time, place and nature of the assault; number of assailants; use of weapons, restraint or intoxicants
- Whether there was oral, vaginal or anal penetration, and whether a condom was used — asked matter-of-factly, because it determines prophylaxis and sampling
- **Actions since the assault:** bathing, washing, change of clothing, urination, defaecation, douching, eating or drinking — these affect evidence, not credibility
- Last menstrual period, contraception, current pregnancy, and any consensual intercourse in the preceding 5 days
- Medical, surgical and psychiatric history; current medication; allergies; HIV and hepatitis B status
- **For a survivor under 18 years, the POCSO Act applies and reporting to the police is mandatory** — explain this to the survivor and the guardian truthfully

### Salient clinical examination

**Consent first**

- **Separate informed consent is taken for: the examination, the collection of samples, and the release of information to the police.** Any of the three may be refused, and refusal of one does not affect the others or the treatment

**General examination**

- Mental state, orientation, signs of intoxication
- **Full body examination in a systematic order, documented on a body chart** — bruises, abrasions, bite marks, ligature marks, grip marks on the arms, injuries to the neck
- Describe each injury objectively: **site, size, shape, colour, margins, and age where it can be reasonably estimated**

**Genital examination**

- **Perform only with consent, gently, with adequate light and with the least instrumentation necessary**
- Examine the perineum, labia, hymen, fourchette, anus and perianal region
- **The absence of genital injury is common and does NOT mean the assault did not occur — record this fact explicitly in the report**

***The per-vaginum two-finger test is prohibited. It has no medical value, and its use has been held to be unconstitutional.***

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Use the government-issued sexual assault evidence collection kit where available, and follow its sequence**
- Samples as indicated by the history: clothing, vaginal, anal and oral swabs, nail clippings and scrapings, pubic hair combings, blood and urine
- **Urine and blood for drug-facilitated assault where a drug is suspected — the window is short**
- **Chain of custody:** every sample labelled, sealed, signed, timed, and handed over with a written acknowledgement. **Record the name of the person to whom it is handed**
- Baseline HIV, HBsAg, anti-HCV, VDRL
- **Urine pregnancy test**
- Other investigations as clinically indicated for injuries

### Basic initial management

**Treat first**

- **Resuscitate and treat injuries as with any other patient. Serious injury takes priority over evidence collection**
- **Tetanus prophylaxis; wound care; analgesia**

**Prophylaxis**

- **HIV post-exposure prophylaxis — start as soon as possible, within 72 hours, for 28 days** (protocol 8.9)
- **Hepatitis B vaccination, with immunoglobulin if unvaccinated and the risk is significant**
- **Emergency contraception — levonorgestrel 1.5 mg as a single dose, effective up to 72 hours; a copper intrauterine device may be considered up to 5 days.** Discuss and document
- **Presumptive treatment for sexually transmitted infection** — as per the current national protocol

**Support and disposition**

- **Psychological first aid: normalise the response, do not press for detail, and offer counselling. Refer to a counsellor or mental health service, and to a support organisation**
- **Provide clothing** if the survivor’s clothes have been retained as evidence — the hospital must not send a person home without clothes
- Arrange a safe place to go; involve the medical social worker; assess the risk of ongoing danger at home
- **Follow-up: HIV testing at 6 weeks, 3 and 6 months; pregnancy test at 2–3 weeks; counselling review**

**Legal**

- **Inform the police** — mandatory in every case of sexual assault, and mandatory under POCSO for anyone under 18
- **The survivor may decline a police complaint and still receive full medical care.** Record the refusal; it does not remove the hospital’s reporting duty
- **The medico-legal report is factual.** It records findings and the survivor’s account. **It does not offer an opinion on whether rape occurred — that is a legal determination, not a medical one**
- **Maintain strict confidentiality. Do not discuss the case outside the treating team, and do not permit any photography**

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Serious injury, head injury, or haemodynamic instability** — treat first, examine for evidence later
- **Suspected drug-facilitated assault** — collect urine and blood urgently; the window is measured in hours
- **Survivor under 18 years** — POCSO applies; mandatory reporting; a specially sensitive approach
- **Suspicion of ongoing danger at home or of trafficking** — involve the medical social worker and the police before discharge
- **Expressed suicidal ideation** — do not discharge without a mental health assessment
- **Any pressure on the survivor to make a complaint, or any delay in treatment pending police arrival** — both are unlawful
- **A request to perform the two-finger test** — refuse and record

### Quick guide — obligations, and what must never appear in the record

#### The department’s obligations

- **Free treatment, in every hospital, public or private** — non-negotiable
- **No refusal, and no delay, pending a police complaint or an FIR**
- **Informed consent, taken separately for examination, sampling and information release**
- **A doctor of the survivor’s preferred gender wherever it is possible; a female attendant present**
- **A private room, and the examination conducted once, not repeated for successive teams**
- **Mandatory police intimation; mandatory POCSO reporting for a minor**
- **Chain of custody maintained and documented**
- **Strict confidentiality; no photography; no disclosure of identity**
- **Clothing provided if the survivor’s own is retained**

#### What must never appear in the report

- **Any comment on "habituation to sexual intercourse"** — this is irrelevant, prejudicial and has been prohibited
- **Any finding from a two-finger test** — the test itself is prohibited
- **Any opinion on whether rape occurred**, or on the truthfulness of the account
- **Any comment on the survivor’s character, dress, conduct, or the delay in reporting**
- **Speculation about the weapon, the assailant or the sequence of events**
- **"No evidence of assault"** as a conclusion — the correct wording records the findings and states that the absence of injury does not exclude assault

*Write the findings. Let the court draw the conclusions.*

## 8.13 Mass Casualty Incident and Disaster Response

A mass casualty incident is one in which **the number or severity of casualties exceeds the resources immediately available**. The governing principle changes from doing everything for one patient to **doing the greatest good for the greatest number**. This protocol is activated by the senior-most doctor present, who does not wait for permission.

### Salient history to be taken

- **Information from the field, obtained from the first caller and recorded on the incident log:**
- **Nature of the incident** — road traffic, fire, building collapse, blast, chemical, stampede, mass poisoning
- **Number of casualties, and the estimated severity mix**
- **Time of the incident and the expected time of arrival of the first casualties**
- **Whether the scene is contained, ongoing, or hazardous** — a secondary device, an unstable structure, an ongoing chemical release
- **Any contamination — chemical, radiological, biological** — this determines whether patients are decontaminated before entering the department
- Which other hospitals are receiving casualties, and whether transfers are being coordinated

### Salient clinical examination

**Field-level triage — rapid, repeated, and by physiology alone**

- **RED — Immediate:** life-threatening but salvageable with immediate intervention. Airway obstruction, tension pneumothorax, exsanguinating haemorrhage, shock
- **YELLOW — Delayed:** serious injury that can safely wait 1–2 hours. Long-bone fracture, stable abdominal injury, moderate burns
- **GREEN — Minor, "walking wounded":** move them out of the resuscitation area immediately; they consume attention out of proportion to need
- **BLACK — Deceased or expectant:** no spontaneous respiration after a single airway-opening manoeuvre, or injuries incompatible with survival given the resources available

***Triage is repeated at every stage. A category is a snapshot, not a diagnosis.***

- **Spend no more than 30–60 seconds per patient in the initial sort. Do not begin definitive treatment during triage**

### Point-of-care investigations

- **Investigations are rationed. Only those that change the immediate disposition are done**
- **Bedside only:** eFAST, chest and pelvis X-ray, ABG, haemoglobin, blood group
- **CT is a bottleneck.** Reserve it for those whose management genuinely depends on it, and sequence access deliberately
- **Blood bank informed at the moment of activation**, with an estimate of requirement; activate the massive transfusion protocol early
- **Every casualty receives a disaster tag with a unique number** — use it for samples, imaging, notes and property until identity is established
- Maintain a **single master casualty register** at the entry point

### Basic initial management

**Activation**

- **The senior-most doctor present activates the plan and becomes the Incident Commander until relieved**
- **Inform: the Medical Superintendent, the Emergency Consultant on call, Surgery, Orthopaedics, Anaesthesia, ICU, OT, Blood Bank, Radiology, Nursing supervisor, Security, and the hospital administration**
- **Call in off-duty staff through the departmental call-out list**

**Space**

- **Clear the department.** Discharge, admit or move every existing patient who can be moved. **This is the single most valuable early action**
- **Designate and label the areas: RED (resuscitation), YELLOW (major treatment), GREEN (minor treatment, away from the main department), BLACK (a dignified, screened area), and a separate DECONTAMINATION area outside the entrance where relevant**
- **Establish one entry point and one exit point, controlled by Security**
- **Set aside a family reception and information area away from the treatment areas, staffed by a named person**

**Staff**

- **Assign roles, not tasks: a Triage Officer at the door, a Team Leader for each area, a Documentation Officer, a Communications Officer, a Blood Bank liaison, and a Family Liaison Officer**
- **Everyone wears an identifying vest or a written label. In a disaster, no one knows who is in charge unless it is visible**
- **The Triage Officer triages and does not treat.** The Incident Commander commands and does not treat

**Clinical approach**

- **Damage control:** stop the bleeding, open the airway, decompress the chest, splint, cover, and move on
- **Tourniquets, pelvic binders, chest drains and tranexamic acid are the high-yield interventions in blast and blunt trauma**
- **Reassess every patient at intervals — categories change in both directions**
- **Plan onward transfer early** for patients needing services this hospital cannot provide; do not hold them waiting for a bed

**Blast injury — anticipate the four mechanisms**

- **Primary (barotrauma):** blast lung, tympanic rupture, bowel perforation — may be occult and present late
- **Secondary (fragments):** penetrating injury, the commonest cause of death
- **Tertiary (displacement):** blunt and crush injury
- **Quaternary:** burns, inhalational injury, crush syndrome, exacerbation of existing disease

**Recovery**

- **Stand down formally, and announce it**
- **Restock, and record what ran out** — this is the most useful output of the whole event
- **Debrief within 24–48 hours, and again in writing**, with an honest account of what failed
- **Arrange psychological support for staff.** Mass casualty events affect the treating team, and the effect appears days later

### RED FLAG SIGNS— shift to Resuscitation/Red bed and inform Consultant immediately

- **Any suggestion of chemical, biological or radiological contamination** — do not allow casualties into the department; decontaminate outside, and protect the staff first
- **Reports of a secondary device or an ongoing threat** — secure the hospital perimeter
- **The department filling with relatives and bystanders** — this stops the response more effectively than the casualties do; control the entry point early
- **Green casualties self-presenting before the ambulances arrive** — expect this, and route them away from the resuscitation area
- **A single critically injured patient absorbing the whole team** — the commonest failure of discipline in a mass casualty
- **Blood bank stock falling below the estimated requirement** — escalate to regional supply immediately
- **Documentation abandoned under pressure** — without the tag numbers, patients and their samples become untraceable within an hour

### Quick guide — the first ten minutes, and the lessons that recur

#### The first ten minutes

- **1. Declare it.** Say the words out loud, so everyone knows the rules have changed
- **2. Take command,** and say who you are and that you are in charge
- **3. Call for help** — the call-out list, and the specialties, in one round of calls
- **4. Clear the department** — move every existing patient who can be moved
- **5. Set up the areas and label them**
- **6. Post the Triage Officer at the door**
- **7. Control the entrances** — Security, one way in
- **8. Alert the blood bank, the OT and the ICU** with numbers, not adjectives
- **9. Start the casualty register** and issue tag numbers
- **10. Nominate the Family Liaison Officer** before the families arrive

#### Lessons that recur in every review

- **Over-triage kills.** Sending too many to the RED area exhausts the resuscitation team and delays those who are genuinely salvageable
- **The walking wounded arrive first**, by private vehicle, before any ambulance — plan for them, not for an orderly arrival
- **Communication fails before clinical care does.** Nominate a Communications Officer and use one channel
- **Documentation collapses first under load** — the tag number system exists precisely for this
- **Staff do not eat, drink or rest** unless someone is given the job of making them
- **The media and the crowd will arrive within minutes** — one spokesperson, from the administration, and no clinical staff speaking to press
- **The debrief matters more than the plan.** Most departments fail the same way twice because nobody wrote down what happened the first time

# 9.0 Emergency Assessment Protocols and Resuscitation Algorithms

Algorithms in this section follow the **AHA 2020 Guidelines with the 2023–25 focused updates**. They are reproduced here in text so that they can be read at the bedside; the wall charts in the resuscitation bay remain the primary visual reference.

## 9.1 ABCDE Assessment — physiological thresholds by triage zone

| **Component** | **Parameter** | **RESUSCITATION BAY** | **RED** | **NORMAL** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **AIRWAY** | Patency | Obstructed | At risk / threatened | Clear and self-maintained |
| **BREATHING** | Respiratory rate /min | \>40 or \<10 | 30–40 or 10–12 | 12–20 |
|  | SpO₂ | \<80% | \<90% | \>94% (88–92% in COPD) |
|  | Cyanosis | Central cyanosis / blue | Pale | Pink |
|  | Effort | Accessory muscles, paradoxical, gasping | Mild distress | No increased effort |
| **CIRCULATION** | Temperature (°F) | \>104 or \<97 | High or low | 97–100.4 |
|  | Heart rate /min | \>150 or \<40 | \>130 or \<60 | 60–100 |
|  | Blood pressure (mmHg) | \>180/100 or \<90/60 | \<100/70 | Around 120/80 |
|  | Shock index (HR÷SBP) | \>1.0 | \>0.9 | \<0.7 |
| **DISABILITY** | Consciousness (AVPU) | Unresponsive or Pain response only | Voice response | Alert |
|  | Blood sugar (mg/dL) | \<50 | \<70 or \>250 | 70–140 |
|  | Pupils | Unequal, fixed, or non-reactive | Sluggish | Equal and reactive |
| **DEHYDRATION** | Skin pinch | \>2 seconds, lethargic | \<2 seconds, poorly nourished | Immediate recoil, alert |
| **EXPOSURE** | Injury / skin | Major trauma, florid whole-body rash, burns | Minor trauma, mild rash | No injury, no rash |

### 9.2  AVPU Scale   \|   9.3  Glasgow Coma Scale (adult and paediatric)

#### AVPU

- **A – Alert:** the patient is awake and oriented
- **V – Voice:** responds to a verbal stimulus
- **P – Pain:** responds only to a painful stimulus
- **U – Unresponsive:** no response to any stimulus

*AVPU is a rapid screen only. A patient who is P or U has a GCS of roughly 8 or less and needs the airway assessed immediately.*

*A formal GCS must be documented for every patient with an abnormal sensorium, head injury, poisoning or stroke, and repeated at intervals — the trend is what matters.*

#### GCS — adult (score out of 15)

**Eye opening (E, out of 4)**

- **4** Spontaneous  •  **3** To voice  •  **2** To pain  •  **1** None

**Verbal response (V, out of 5)**

- **5** Oriented  •  **4** Confused conversation  •  **3** Inappropriate words  •  **2** Incomprehensible sounds  •  **1** None

**Motor response (M, out of 6)**

- **6** Obeys commands  •  **5** Localises pain  •  **4** Withdraws from pain  •  **3** Abnormal flexion (decorticate)  •  **2** Extension (decerebrate)  •  **1** None

*Record as E\_V\_M\_ = total, never as a bare number. If the patient is intubated, record V as 1T and note it.*

- **Severity: mild 14–15  •  moderate 9–13  •  severe ≤8**

#### Paediatric GCS — verbal and motor modifications (\<2 years)

**Verbal (out of 5)**

- **5** Coos, babbles, smiles appropriately  •  **4** Irritable cry, consolable  •  **3** Cries to pain  •  **2** Moans to pain  •  **1** None

**Motor (out of 6)**

- **6** Spontaneous purposeful movement  •  **5** Withdraws to touch  •  **4** Withdraws to pain  •  **3** Abnormal flexion  •  **2** Extension  •  **1** None

**Eye opening is scored as in the adult**

*In practice, the motor score alone carries most of the prognostic information, and a single blown pupil with a falling motor score is more urgent than any total.*

## 9.4 Basic Life Support — Adult

### Adult BLS — healthcare provider sequence

#### Recognition and activation

- **Ensure the scene is safe**
- Check responsiveness — tap and shout
- **Shout for help. Announce CODE BLUE. Send someone for the defibrillator and the crash cart**
- **Check the carotid pulse and breathing simultaneously for no more than 10 seconds**
  - Normal breathing with a pulse → monitor and treat the underlying cause
  - **No normal breathing but a pulse present → rescue breathing, 1 breath every 6 seconds (10/min), recheck the pulse every 2 minutes**
  - **No breathing and no pulse → start CPR immediately**

*Gasping or agonal respiration is not normal breathing. Treat it as cardiac arrest.*

#### High-quality CPR — the numbers that matter

- **Rate: 100–120 compressions per minute**
- **Depth: at least 5 cm, not more than 6 cm** in an adult
- **Allow complete chest recoil** — do not lean on the chest between compressions
- **Minimise interruptions — aim for a chest compression fraction above 60%**, and keep any pause under 10 seconds
- **Compression-to-ventilation ratio 30:2** without an advanced airway
- **With an advanced airway: continuous compressions with 1 breath every 6 seconds (10/min)**
- **Avoid excessive ventilation** — it raises intrathoracic pressure and reduces coronary perfusion
- **Change the compressor every 2 minutes** or sooner if fatigued; use the rhythm check as the switch point
- Hard, flat surface; heel of the hand on the lower half of the sternum
- **Use waveform capnography where available: an ETCO₂ below 10 mmHg indicates inadequate compressions**

#### Defibrillation and special situations

- **Attach the AED or defibrillator as soon as it arrives** and analyse the rhythm
- **Shockable (VF / pulseless VT) → shock immediately, then resume compressions at once** without a pulse check; recheck the rhythm after 2 minutes
- **Non-shockable (asystole / PEA) → resume compressions immediately**; adrenaline as early as possible
- Biphasic energy: manufacturer’s recommendation, usually 120–200 J; if unknown, use the maximum

**Opioid-associated life-threatening emergency**

- Suspected opioid overdose with **respiratory depression but a pulse**: support ventilation and give **Inj. Naloxone 0.4–2 mg IV/IM/intranasal**, repeated every 4 minutes as needed
- **If there is no pulse, start CPR — naloxone does not replace CPR**, and standard ACLS takes priority

**Choking**

- Effective cough → encourage coughing. Ineffective and conscious → **5 back blows then 5 abdominal thrusts**, repeated. Unconscious → start CPR and look in the mouth at each compression cycle

### 9.5  Basic Life Support — Paediatric

#### Sequence

- Ensure safety; check responsiveness
- **In a child, hypoxia is the usual cause of arrest** — ventilation matters more than in the adult
- **Check the brachial pulse (infant) or carotid/femoral pulse (child) for no more than 10 seconds**
- **Pulse present but \<60/min with signs of poor perfusion → start CPR**
- Lone rescuer: 2 minutes of CPR before leaving to call for help (unless a sudden witnessed collapse)
- Two rescuers: one starts CPR, the other activates the emergency response and fetches the defibrillator

#### Technique

- **Rate 100–120/min**
- **Depth: at least one-third of the anteroposterior chest diameter** — about 4 cm in an infant, 5 cm in a child
- **Infant:** two fingers (single rescuer) or **two thumbs encircling the chest** (two rescuers)
- **Child:** heel of one or both hands on the lower half of the sternum
- **Ratio: 30:2 single rescuer; 15:2 with two rescuers**
- **With an advanced airway: 1 breath every 2–3 seconds (20–30/min)** — this is faster than the adult rate
- Allow full recoil; minimise interruptions

#### Drugs and energy — paediatric

- **Adrenaline 0.01 mg/kg (0.1 mL/kg of 1:10,000) IV/IO**, every 3–5 minutes; maximum 1 mg
- **Defibrillation: 2 J/kg for the first shock, 4 J/kg for the second, then 4–10 J/kg** (not exceeding the adult dose)
- **Amiodarone 5 mg/kg IV/IO** bolus for refractory VF/pVT, may repeat twice
- **Fluid bolus 20 mL/kg** of isotonic crystalloid (10 mL/kg in suspected cardiac dysfunction or DKA)
- **Dextrose 0.5–1 g/kg** — as 10% dextrose 5–10 mL/kg in infants

*Use a length-based resuscitation tape or the formula weight (kg) = 2 × (age in years + 4) for children aged 1–10.*

## 9.6 Advanced Cardiac Life Support — Adult

### 9.6.1 / 9.6.2  Adult cardiac arrest algorithm

#### Shockable rhythm — VF / pulseless VT

- **Start CPR, give oxygen, attach the monitor or defibrillator**
- **Shock** (biphasic 120–200 J, or maximum if unknown)
- **Resume CPR immediately for 2 minutes**; obtain IV or intraosseous access
- **Rhythm check → Shock**
- **CPR 2 minutes + Inj. Adrenaline 1 mg IV/IO, then every 3–5 minutes**
- Consider an advanced airway and waveform capnography
- **Rhythm check → Shock**
- **CPR 2 minutes + Inj. Amiodarone 300 mg IV/IO bolus** (second dose 150 mg), *or* **Inj. Lignocaine 1–1.5 mg/kg** (second dose 0.5–0.75 mg/kg)
- **Treat the reversible causes throughout**
- Continue the cycle: rhythm check → shock → 2 minutes of CPR → drug

*If the rhythm becomes organised, check the pulse. If a pulse is present, begin post-arrest care.*

#### Non-shockable rhythm — asystole / PEA

- **Start CPR, give oxygen, attach the monitor**
- **Inj. Adrenaline 1 mg IV/IO as soon as feasible, then every 3–5 minutes**
- **CPR for 2 minutes**; obtain IV or IO access; consider an advanced airway and capnography
- **Rhythm check every 2 minutes**
  - If shockable at any point → move to the shockable pathway
  - If asystole or PEA persists → continue CPR and adrenaline
- **The priority in PEA is finding and reversing the cause** — use bedside ultrasound during the rhythm check to look for tamponade, pneumothorax, hypovolaemia and right ventricular strain

*Atropine is no longer recommended in the cardiac arrest algorithm.*

*Routine sodium bicarbonate is not recommended; reserve it for hyperkalaemia, tricyclic overdose and pre-existing severe acidosis.*

#### Reversible causes — the H’s and T’s

**H**

- **Hypovolaemia** — fluid, blood, control haemorrhage
- **Hypoxia** — airway and oxygen
- **Hydrogen ion (acidosis)** — ventilation, bicarbonate if indicated
- **Hypo- or hyperkalaemia** — calcium, insulin–dextrose, bicarbonate, potassium
- **Hypothermia** — active rewarming; **continue resuscitation for longer**
- **Hypoglycaemia** — dextrose

**T**

- **Tension pneumothorax** — needle decompression
- **Tamponade (cardiac)** — pericardiocentesis
- **Toxins** — specific antidote (naloxone, bicarbonate, lipid emulsion, calcium)
- **Thrombosis — pulmonary** (consider thrombolysis during CPR)
- **Thrombosis — coronary** (post-arrest angiography)

**Quality targets during resuscitation**

- **ETCO₂ \<10 mmHg → improve compression quality**; a sudden rise in ETCO₂ suggests return of spontaneous circulation
- Diastolic arterial pressure \<20 mmHg if invasively monitored → improve compressions

### 9.6.3  Immediate post-cardiac-arrest care

#### Oxygenation, ventilation and haemodynamics

- **Target SpO₂ 92–98% — avoid hyperoxia**, which worsens reperfusion injury
- **Target PaCO₂ 35–45 mmHg — avoid hyperventilation**, which reduces cerebral blood flow
- Secure the airway; confirm tube position with waveform capnography; chest X-ray
- **Target systolic BP \>90 mmHg and mean arterial pressure \>65 mmHg**
- IV crystalloid bolus 1–2 L if hypotensive
- *Vasopressor infusions: Noradrenaline 0.1–0.5 mcg/kg/min, Adrenaline 0.1–0.5 mcg/kg/min, or Dopamine 5–20 mcg/kg/min*

#### Diagnosis and reperfusion

- **12-lead ECG immediately**
- **STEMI or high suspicion of acute myocardial infarction → coronary angiography** (or fibrinolysis if PCI is unavailable)
- Treat the identified precipitant — the H’s and T’s again
- Blood gas, electrolytes, glucose, lactate, troponin, RFT, LFT, coagulation
- Echocardiography for ventricular function, tamponade and RV strain
- CT head and CT pulmonary angiogram where the cause is unclear

#### Neuroprotection and disposition

- **Targeted temperature management: maintain a constant temperature between 32°C and 37.5°C for at least 24 hours** in patients who do not follow commands
- **Actively prevent fever for at least 72 hours**
- Maintain **normoglycaemia (140–180 mg/dL)**; avoid hypoglycaemia
- Treat seizures; continuous EEG monitoring where available
- Head end at 30°; sedation and analgesia as required
- **Do not prognosticate within the first 72 hours** — multimodal assessment after rewarming
- **Transfer to ICU.** Counsel the family; document the discussion

### 9.6.4  Adult bradycardia with a pulse   \|   9.6.5  Adult tachycardia with a pulse

#### Bradycardia (HR \<50/min) with a pulse

- **Identify and treat the underlying cause**: oxygen if hypoxic, monitor, IV access, 12-lead ECG

**Is it causing harm? Look for:**

- Hypotension, acutely altered mental status, signs of shock, ischaemic chest discomfort, acute heart failure
- **Stable → monitor and observe; look for the cause (drugs, ischaemia, electrolytes, hypothyroidism, raised ICP)**

**Unstable → treat**

- **Inj. Atropine 1 mg IV bolus, repeated every 3–5 minutes to a maximum of 3 mg**

*Atropine is often ineffective in high-degree AV block and in the transplanted heart — do not delay pacing for repeated atropine*

- **If atropine is ineffective:**
  - **Transcutaneous pacing**, with sedation and analgesia, *or*
  - **Inj. Dopamine infusion 5–20 mcg/kg/min**, *or*
  - **Inj. Adrenaline infusion 2–10 mcg/min**
- *Consider expert consultation and transvenous pacing*

**Specific causes**

- **Beta-blocker or calcium channel blocker overdose** → **Inj. Glucagon**, calcium, high-dose insulin–euglycaemia therapy
- **Hyperkalaemia** → anti-hyperkalaemia measures (Section 11)
- **Inferior wall MI** → often vagally mediated and atropine-responsive

#### Tachycardia (HR \>150/min) with a pulse

- Identify and treat the cause; oxygen if hypoxic; monitor; IV access; **12-lead ECG**

**Is the patient unstable?**

- Hypotension, acutely altered mental status, signs of shock, ischaemic chest discomfort, acute heart failure
- **UNSTABLE → SYNCHRONISED CARDIOVERSION**, with sedation if the patient is conscious
  - Narrow regular: 50–100 J  •  Narrow irregular: 120–200 J biphasic
  - Wide regular: 100 J  •  **Wide irregular: defibrillate (unsynchronised) — do not synchronise**
  - *Consider adenosine first only if narrow and regular*

**STABLE → assess the QRS width**

- **Narrow QRS (\<0.12 s), regular:**
  - **Vagal manoeuvres** (modified Valsalva is the most effective)
  - **Inj. Adenosine 6 mg rapid IV push with a saline flush; if no response, 12 mg; may repeat 12 mg** — warn the patient about the transient unpleasant sensation
  - If unsuccessful: beta-blocker or calcium channel blocker; expert consultation
- **Narrow QRS, irregular — usually atrial fibrillation:** rate control with a beta-blocker or diltiazem; anticoagulation assessment; **cardiovert only if unstable or clearly of onset \<48 hours**
- **Wide QRS (≥0.12 s):**
  - **Assume ventricular tachycardia until proved otherwise**
  - **Inj. Amiodarone 150 mg IV over 10 minutes**, repeated as needed, then 1 mg/min for 6 hours, *or* **Inj. Procainamide**, *or* **Inj. Lignocaine**
  - **Correct potassium and magnesium; Inj. Magnesium sulphate 2 g IV for torsades de pointes**
  - *Adenosine may be considered only if the wide complex tachycardia is regular and monomorphic*
  - Expert consultation

### 9.6.6  Acute coronary syndrome algorithm

#### First 10 minutes

- **Triage as Red. ECG within 10 minutes of arrival**
- Monitored bed, continuous ECG, SpO₂ and NIBP; IV access; targeted history and examination
- **Oxygen only if SpO₂ \<94%**
- **Tab. Aspirin 300 mg chewed** (unless contraindicated)
- **Nitroglycerine or isosorbide dinitrate sublingual** — check the contraindications in protocol 2.1
- Analgesia — **Inj. Morphine 2–4 mg IV** if the pain persists despite nitrates (*use sparingly; it may reduce antiplatelet absorption*)
- **Show the ECG to the Consultant Cardiologist immediately** and send troponin
- Portable chest X-ray; do not send the patient out of the department

#### STEMI — reperfusion

- **ST elevation ≥1 mm in two contiguous leads (≥2 mm in V2–V3 in men, ≥1.5 mm in women), or new LBBB with a compatible history**
- **Primary PCI is preferred if it can be achieved within 120 minutes of first medical contact** — **target door-to-balloon ≤90 minutes**
- **Otherwise, fibrinolysis — target door-to-needle ≤30 minutes.** Complete the checklist in Section 10 first
  - **Inj. Tenecteplase as a single weight-adjusted IV bolus over 5–10 seconds**
  - With **Inj. Enoxaparin 30 mg IV bolus followed by 1 mg/kg SC BD** (omit the bolus and use 0.75 mg/kg SC BD if aged over 75)
- **Dual antiplatelet therapy: Aspirin 300 mg + Clopidogrel 300 mg** (600 mg if for primary PCI; 75 mg if aged over 75 and for fibrinolysis)
- **Tab. Atorvastatin 80 mg**
- *Transfer for rescue PCI if there is no resolution of ST elevation or pain by 60–90 minutes after fibrinolysis*

#### NSTEMI, unstable angina, and complications

- **Aspirin, clopidogrel or ticagrelor, statin, and anticoagulation with enoxaparin or fondaparinux**
- Risk stratification with the **GRACE or TIMI** score determines the timing of angiography
- *Very high risk (haemodynamic instability, refractory angina, life-threatening arrhythmia, mechanical complication) → immediate invasive strategy*

**Complications to anticipate**

- **Inferior wall MI with RV infarction** — hypotension, raised JVP, clear lungs. **Do not give nitrates or diuretics; give fluid**. Confirm with right-sided leads V3R–V4R
- **Cardiogenic shock** — inotropes, early revascularisation
- **Ventricular arrhythmia** — defibrillate; correct potassium and magnesium
- **Complete heart block** — atropine, pacing
- **Acute mitral regurgitation or ventricular septal rupture** — new murmur with pulmonary oedema; urgent echocardiography and surgical referral

## 9.7 Paediatric Advanced Life Support

### 9.7.1  Paediatric cardiac arrest   \|   9.7.2  Tachycardia with poor perfusion   \|   9.7.3  Bradycardia with poor perfusion

#### 5.7.1  Cardiac arrest

- **Start CPR; give oxygen; attach the monitor or defibrillator**

**Shockable (VF / pulseless VT)**

- **Shock 2 J/kg** → CPR 2 minutes, obtain IV/IO access
- **Shock 4 J/kg** → CPR 2 minutes + **Inj. Adrenaline 0.01 mg/kg IV/IO every 3–5 minutes**
- **Shock 4–10 J/kg** → CPR 2 minutes + **Inj. Amiodarone 5 mg/kg IV/IO** (may repeat up to twice) *or* **Inj. Lignocaine 1 mg/kg**

**Non-shockable (asystole / PEA)**

- **CPR + Inj. Adrenaline 0.01 mg/kg IV/IO as soon as possible, then every 3–5 minutes**
- Rhythm check every 2 minutes

*In children, arrest is usually the end point of respiratory failure or shock. Ventilation, oxygenation and volume are the priorities, and reversible causes are found more often than in adults.*

- **Reversible causes:** hypoxia (commonest), hypovolaemia, hypoglycaemia, hypo- or hyperkalaemia, hypothermia, hydrogen ion, tension pneumothorax, tamponade, toxins, thrombosis

#### 5.7.2  Tachycardia with a pulse and poor perfusion

- Identify and treat the cause; oxygen; monitor; IV/IO access; 12-lead ECG

**Assess the QRS**

- **Narrow QRS (≤0.09 s)**
  - **Sinus tachycardia:** P waves present and normal, rate variable, infant \<220/min, child \<180/min → **treat the cause** (fever, pain, hypovolaemia, sepsis)
  - **Supraventricular tachycardia:** P waves absent or abnormal, rate not variable, infant ≥220/min, child ≥180/min
    - **Vagal manoeuvres** (ice to the face in an infant) — do not delay
    - **Inj. Adenosine 0.1 mg/kg rapid IV push (maximum 6 mg), then 0.2 mg/kg (maximum 12 mg)**
    - **If unstable: synchronised cardioversion 0.5–1 J/kg, then 2 J/kg**, with sedation if possible
- **Wide QRS (\>0.09 s) — possible ventricular tachycardia**
  - **Synchronised cardioversion 0.5–1 J/kg** if there is cardiopulmonary compromise
  - **Inj. Amiodarone 5 mg/kg IV over 20–60 minutes** *or* **Inj. Procainamide 15 mg/kg IV over 30–60 minutes** — **do not give both**
  - Expert consultation

#### 5.7.3  Bradycardia with a pulse and poor perfusion

- **Bradycardia in a child is usually hypoxic in origin. The first treatment is oxygen and ventilation, not a drug**
- Maintain the airway; assist ventilation with bag and mask and 100% oxygen; attach the monitor; obtain IV/IO access

**Reassess**

- **If the heart rate remains \<60/min with signs of poor perfusion despite oxygenation and ventilation → START CPR**
- **Inj. Adrenaline 0.01 mg/kg (0.1 mL/kg of 1:10,000) IV/IO, repeated every 3–5 minutes**
- **Inj. Atropine 0.02 mg/kg IV/IO (minimum dose 0.1 mg, maximum single dose 0.5 mg)** — for increased vagal tone or primary AV block
- *Consider transthoracic or transvenous pacing for complete heart block or sinus node dysfunction not responding to the above*
- **Identify and treat the cause:** hypoxia, acidosis, hypothermia, hypoglycaemia, hyperkalaemia, raised intracranial pressure, drugs, heart block, toxins

*If the pulse is lost at any point, move to the cardiac arrest algorithm.*

## 10.0 Fibrinolytic Checklist for STEMI

Complete this checklist **before** giving a fibrinolytic. **A single "yes" in the absolute contraindication column means fibrinolysis must not be given** — arrange transfer for primary PCI instead. Attach the completed checklist to the case sheet and have the Consultant countersign it.

### Fibrinolytic checklist

#### Step 1 — Is fibrinolysis indicated?

- **Ischaemic chest discomfort or an anginal equivalent lasting 15 minutes or more, and continuing**
- **Symptom onset within the last 12 hours** (benefit is greatest within 3 hours; consider up to 24 hours if symptoms and ST elevation persist)
- **ECG shows:**
  - ST elevation ≥1 mm in two contiguous limb leads, **or**
  - ST elevation ≥2 mm in men (≥1.5 mm in women) in V2–V3, **or**
  - New or presumed new left bundle branch block with a compatible history, **or**
  - True posterior infarction (ST depression V1–V3 with tall R waves; confirm with V7–V9)
- **Primary PCI is not available within 120 minutes of first medical contact**

*If all of the above are satisfied, proceed to Step 2. Target door-to-needle time ≤30 minutes.*

#### Step 2 — Absolute contraindications (any "yes" = DO NOT give)

- **Any prior intracranial haemorrhage, at any time**
- **Known structural cerebral vascular lesion** — arteriovenous malformation, aneurysm
- **Known malignant intracranial neoplasm**, primary or metastatic
- **Ischaemic stroke within the last 3 months** (except acute ischaemic stroke within 4.5 hours, where thrombolysis is the treatment)
- **Suspected aortic dissection**
- **Active bleeding, or a known bleeding diathesis** (excluding menses)
- **Significant closed head or facial trauma within the last 3 months**
- **Intracranial or intraspinal surgery within the last 2 months**
- **Severe uncontrolled hypertension not responding to emergency therapy**
- **For streptokinase: prior treatment with streptokinase within the previous 6 months**

#### Step 3 — Relative contraindications (weigh with the Consultant)

- History of **chronic, severe, poorly controlled hypertension**
- **Systolic BP \>180 mmHg or diastolic \>110 mmHg** on presentation
- History of prior ischaemic stroke more than 3 months ago; dementia; other intracranial pathology not listed as absolute
- **Traumatic or prolonged (\>10 minutes) CPR**; **major surgery within the last 3 weeks**
- **Recent internal bleeding within 2–4 weeks**; non-compressible vascular puncture
- **Pregnancy**
- **Active peptic ulcer disease**
- **Current use of an anticoagulant** — the higher the INR, the higher the risk

**Before giving**

- **Two IV cannulae sited; blood samples drawn; blood group and cross-match sent**
- **Patient and family counselled about the risk of bleeding, including intracranial haemorrhage, and consent documented**
- **Continuous cardiac monitoring and a defibrillator at the bedside** — reperfusion arrhythmias are expected

**After giving — assess reperfusion at 60–90 minutes**

- Resolution of pain; **\>50% resolution of ST elevation**; reperfusion arrhythmia (accelerated idioventricular rhythm)
- **If reperfusion has not occurred, arrange rescue PCI**

## 11.0 Guide to Common Medications used in the Emergency Department

All doses are for a **normal-sized adult with normal renal and hepatic function** unless stated otherwise. **Verify every weight-based dose and every renal adjustment before administration.** Antibiotics are prescribed after discussion with the Consultant, except where this manual mandates immediate empirical therapy.

### 11.1  Hyperkalaemia (K⁺ \>5.5 mEq/L)   \|   11.2  Initial loading for suspected acute coronary syndrome

#### Hyperkalaemia — in order of urgency

- **Get IV access, send a VBG and RBS, and obtain an ECG. Connect to a continuous cardiac monitor**
- **ECG changes: tall tented T waves → flattened P → widened QRS → sine wave → arrest**

**1. Stabilise the myocardium (immediately, if there are ECG changes)**

- **Inj. Calcium gluconate 10% — 10 mL IV over 10 minutes**, repeat if the ECG does not improve
- *Calcium chloride 10% 5–10 mL through a central line, if available and the patient is peri-arrest*

*Calcium does not lower potassium — it protects the heart for 30–60 minutes while the other measures work*

**2. Shift potassium into cells**

- **Inj. Regular Insulin 10 units IV with Inj. Dextrose 25% 100 mL IV over 30 minutes** — give dextrose only if RBS \<250 mg/dL. **Recheck RBS at 30, 60 and 120 minutes; hypoglycaemia is the commonest complication**
- **Nebulised Salbutamol 10–20 mg (4–8 respules) over 10 minutes**
- *Inj. Sodium bicarbonate 50–100 mEq IV over 30 minutes — only if there is significant metabolic acidosis*

**3. Remove potassium from the body**

- **Inj. Furosemide 40–80 mg IV** — *avoid if anuric or hypotensive*
- **Oral or rectal potassium binder** (sodium polystyrene sulphonate, or a newer binder where available)
- **Urgent haemodialysis** — the only reliable method in anuria; **inform Nephrology early**

*In cardiac arrest, all the above are given as rapid IV boluses.*

#### ACS loading doses

- **ECG within 10 minutes of arrival. Show it to the Consultant Cardiologist**
- **Tab. Nitroglycerine 0.4 mg or Isosorbide dinitrate 5 mg sublingual stat**, repeated every 5 minutes up to 3 doses
- **Tab. Aspirin 300 mg (four 75 mg tablets), non-enteric coated, chewed, PO stat**
- **Tab. Clopidogrel 300 mg (four 75 mg tablets) PO stat**
  - **600 mg** if going for primary PCI
  - **75 mg only** if aged over 75 and receiving fibrinolysis
- **Tab. Atorvastatin 80 mg (two 40 mg tablets) PO stat**

*If the patient cannot swallow, all of these may be crushed and given through a nasogastric tube.*

**Precautions**

- **Avoid nitrates:** inferior wall or right ventricular infarction, SBP \<90 mmHg or a fall \>30 mmHg from baseline, marked bradycardia or tachycardia, severe aortic stenosis, HOCM, sildenafil within 24 hours or tadalafil within 48 hours
- **Avoid aspirin and clopidogrel:** massive upper GI bleed, suspected haemorrhagic stroke, **suspected aortic dissection**, known aspirin allergy

### 11.3  Upper GI bleed   \|   11.4  Analgesia in the ED

#### Initial management of upper GI bleed

- **Two wide-bore IV cannulae (16G grey or 18G green)**
- **Send: CBC, blood group and cross-match, VBG, PT/INR, LFT, RFT**
- **IVF Ringer’s lactate 1 L bolus** if shocked
- **Inj. Pantoprazole 80 mg IV stat, then 8 mg/h infusion** (or 40 mg IV BD) — for suspected peptic ulcer or NSAID-related bleed
- **Inj. Terlipressin 2 mg IV stat then 1–2 mg q4–6h**, *or* **Inj. Somatostatin 250 mcg IV bolus then 250–500 mcg/h**, *or* **Inj. Octreotide 50 mcg bolus then 50 mcg/h** — for known or suspected chronic liver disease
- **Inj. Ceftriaxone 1 g IV OD for 7 days — mandatory prophylaxis in cirrhotic GI bleed**
- **Inj. Ondansetron 8 mg IV** or **Inj. Metoclopramide 10 mg IV**
- **Transfuse PRBC to a target haemoglobin of 7–9 g/dL** (\>8 g/dL if known coronary disease). **Over-transfusion worsens variceal bleeding**
- **Inform Consultant Gastroenterology for endoscopy** — within 12 hours for variceal, 24 hours for non-variceal bleeding

*NG lavage only with a stable airway, and never in suspected variceal bleeding.*

*Tranexamic acid is not recommended for routine GI bleeding.*

#### Analgesics and adjuncts

**Non-opioid**

- **Inj. / Tab. Paracetamol** — IV 1 g q6h (maximum 3–4 g/day); oral 325–650 mg q6h
- **Inj. Diclofenac 75 mg IM / slow IV / PO BD** — *dilute before IV; avoid IM in coagulopathy; avoid in renal impairment, GI bleed, dengue and hypovolaemia*
- **Tab. Ibuprofen 400–800 mg q6h**; **Tab. Indomethacin 25–50 mg q8h**

**Opioid — always with monitoring**

- **Inj. Tramadol 50–100 mg IM / slow IV / PO** — *dilute in 10 mL NS and give slowly with an antiemetic; causes marked nausea; lowers the seizure threshold*
- **Inj. Fentanyl 1 mcg/kg IV** — *the opioid of choice in trauma and in the haemodynamically unstable; connect to ECG and SpO₂ monitoring*
- **Inj. Morphine 2–3 mg IV** (maximum 0.1 mg/kg per dose), or 10 mg IM/SC
- **Inj. Pentazocine 30 mg IV / IM / SC q6h**

*Keep Inj. Naloxone available whenever opioids are used.*

**Antispasmodic and neuropathic**

- **Inj. Drotaverine 40–80 mg slow IV / IM q6h**; **Inj. Hyoscine butylbromide 20 mg IV / IM**
- **Tab. Pregabalin 50–150 mg**, **Tab. Nortriptyline 10 mg**, **Tab. Gabapentin 300 mg** — for neuropathic pain

**Adjuncts**

- **Inj. Promethazine 25–50 mg IV / IM**; **Inj. Pantoprazole 40 mg + Inj. Metoclopramide 10 mg**

### 11.5  Common antibiotics in the ED — a syndromic quick guide (prescribe after consulting the Consultant on call)

#### Respiratory and CNS

**Pneumonia / lower respiratory tract infection**

- **CRB-65 ≤1, outpatient:** Tab. Amoxicillin–clavulanate 625 mg PO TDS **+** Tab. Azithromycin 500 mg PO OD × 5 days
- **CRB-65 ≥2, inpatient:** Inj. Amoxicillin–clavulanate 1.2 g IV TDS **or** Inj. Ceftriaxone 1 g IV BD **+** Inj./Tab. Azithromycin 500 mg OD
- **Hospital-acquired or ventilator-associated:** Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6–8h **or** Inj. Meropenem 1 g IV q8h ± Inj. Vancomycin

**Meningitis**

- **Inj. Ceftriaxone 2 g IV BD**
- **Inj. Vancomycin 15 mg/kg IV BD** in 100 mL NS over 1 hour
- **Inj. Ampicillin 2 g IV q4h** — add if elderly, pregnant, immunosuppressed or malignancy
- **Inj. Dexamethasone 10 mg IV q6h — the first dose just before or with the first antibiotic dose**
- **Inj. Acyclovir 10 mg/kg IV TDS** — add if viral encephalitis is suspected

*Give the first dose within an hour. Do not wait for the lumbar puncture.*

#### Abdominal, urinary and skin

**Suspected peritonitis**

- **Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis** (ascitic neutrophils \>250/µL): Inj. Cefotaxime 2 g IV TDS **or** Inj. Ceftriaxone 2 g IV OD, **plus Inj. Albumin 20% 1.5 g/kg on day 1**
- **Secondary bacterial peritonitis** (perforation, post-operative): Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV TDS (2.25 g TDS if AKI or CKD)

**Liver abscess**

- **Inpatient:** Inj. Ceftriaxone 1 g IV BD **+** Inj. Metronidazole 500 mg IV TDS
- **Outpatient:** Tab. Cefixime 200 mg PO BD **+** Tab. Metronidazole 800 mg PO TDS × 7–10 days

**Urinary tract infection**

- **Uncomplicated:** Tab. Nitrofurantoin 100 mg PO BD **or** Tab. Cefixime 200 mg PO BD **or** Tab. Ciprofloxacin 500 mg PO BD × 5 days
- **Pyelonephritis, catheter-related, or a sick patient:** Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV TDS **or** Inj. Meropenem 1 g IV TDS

**Cellulitis and soft tissue**

- **Inpatient:** Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV TDS ± Inj. Vancomycin 15 mg/kg IV BD **or** Inj. Clindamycin 900 mg IV TDS
- **Outpatient:** Tab. Amoxicillin–clavulanate 625 mg PO TDS × 5 days

**Diarrhoea**

***Non-bloody diarrhoea needs no antibiotic. Do not prescribe ciprofloxacin and metronidazole for every case.***

- **Bloody diarrhoea or fever:** Tab. Ciprofloxacin 500 mg PO BD **or** Tab. Cefixime 200 mg PO BD × 3–5 days; if not tolerating orally, Inj. Ciprofloxacin 200 mg IV BD ± Inj. Metronidazole 500 mg IV TDS

#### Sepsis, tropical fevers and the neutropenic patient

**Suspected sepsis, source unknown**

- **Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV TDS** *or* **Inj. Meropenem 1 g IV TDS** *or* **Inj. Cefoperazone–Sulbactam 3 g IV BD**
- **Plus** Inj. Vancomycin 15 mg/kg IV BD *or* Inj. Teicoplanin 400 mg IV (loading at 0, 12 and 24 hours, then OD)
- **Plus** Inj. Doxycycline 100 mg IV/PO BD where scrub typhus, leptospirosis or rickettsial illness is possible

**Malaria**

- **Severe:** Inj. Artesunate 2.4 mg/kg IV at 0, 12 and 24 hours, then every 24 hours
- **P. vivax:** Tab. Chloroquine 300 mg base — 2 tablets on days 1 and 2, 1 tablet on day 3; **plus** Tab. Primaquine 15 mg OD × 14 days (**exclude G6PD deficiency first**)
- **P. falciparum:** Artemether–Lumefantrine fixed-dose combination BD × 3 days **plus** Tab. Primaquine 45 mg on day 2

**Enteric fever**

- **Inpatient:** Inj. Ceftriaxone 2 g IV OD
- **Outpatient:** Tab. Cefixime 400 mg PO BD × 10–14 days **+** Tab. Azithromycin 1 g × 5 days

**Febrile neutropenia (see protocol 7.4)**

- **MASCC \<21, inpatient:** Inj. Piperacillin–Tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6–8h *or* Inj. Cefoperazone–Sulbactam 3 g IV q12h *or* Inj. Meropenem 1 g IV q8h ± Inj. Amikacin 15 mg/kg IV OD ± Inj. Vancomycin
- **MASCC ≥21, selected outpatients:** Tab. Amoxicillin–clavulanate 625 mg PO TDS **+** Tab. Levofloxacin 750 mg PO OD, with daily review

**Renal dose modification required — ask the Consultant**

- **Vancomycin, Piperacillin–Tazobactam, Amikacin, Meropenem, Levofloxacin, Fluconazole, Acyclovir**

### 11.6  Antidotes and toxicology drugs   \|   11.7  Sedation, RSI and vasoactive infusions

#### Antidotes — keep these stocked and checked

- **Atropine** — organophosphate, carbamate, bradycardia
- **Pralidoxime (PAM)** — organophosphate only
- **N-acetylcysteine** — paracetamol. *IV: 150 mg/kg in 200 mL 5% D over 1 h, then 50 mg/kg in 500 mL over 4 h, then 100 mg/kg in 1 L over 16 h. Oral: 140 mg/kg loading then 70 mg/kg q4h × 17 doses*
- **Naloxone** — opioids. *0.4–2 mg IV; use 0.04–0.1 mg increments in the chronic user or the palliative patient*
- **Sodium bicarbonate** — tricyclic and other sodium-channel-blocker toxicity, salicylate, severe acidosis
- **Glucagon** — beta-blocker and calcium channel blocker overdose
- **Calcium gluconate / chloride** — hyperkalaemia, calcium channel blocker overdose, hydrofluoric acid, magnesium toxicity
- **IV lipid emulsion 20%** — local anaesthetic systemic toxicity, and lipophilic drug overdose
- **Methylene blue** — methaemoglobinaemia (nitrites, benzocaine, sulphonamides, dapsone); also ifosfamide encephalopathy
- **Hydroxocobalamin / sodium thiosulphate** — cyanide
- **Vitamin K, PCC, FFP** — warfarin and superwarfarin
- **Pyridoxine** — isoniazid overdose, gram for gram
- **Deferoxamine** — iron; **Penicillamine / DMSA** — heavy metals
- **Anti-snake venom (polyvalent)** — snake envenomation, 10 vials over 1 hour
- **Flumazenil** — benzodiazepines; ***generally avoided*** because of seizure risk

***National Poisons Information Centre, AIIMS New Delhi: 1800-116-117 (24 hours)***

#### Rapid sequence intubation, sedation and infusions

**Pre-treatment and induction**

- **Inj. Fentanyl 1–3 mcg/kg IV** — blunts the pressor response
- **Inj. Ketamine 1–2 mg/kg IV** — preferred in shock, asthma and bronchospasm; maintains blood pressure
- **Inj. Etomidate 0.3 mg/kg IV** — the most haemodynamically neutral induction agent
- **Inj. Propofol 1–2 mg/kg IV** — *causes hypotension; avoid in shock*
- **Inj. Midazolam 0.1–0.3 mg/kg IV**

**Paralysis**

- **Inj. Succinylcholine 1–1.5 mg/kg IV** — *contraindicated in hyperkalaemia, burns or crush injury beyond 24–48 hours, chronic neuromuscular disease, and known malignant hyperthermia*
- **Inj. Rocuronium 1.2 mg/kg IV** — the safer default when succinylcholine is contraindicated

**Post-intubation sedation**

- **Inj. Midazolam infusion 0.02–0.1 mg/kg/h**; **Inj. Fentanyl infusion 1–2 mcg/kg/h**; **Inj. Propofol 1–4 mg/kg/h**

**Vasoactive infusions**

- **Noradrenaline 0.05–0.5 mcg/kg/min** — first-line vasopressor in septic and most other shock
- **Adrenaline 0.05–0.5 mcg/kg/min** — anaphylaxis, cardiac arrest, refractory shock
- **Dopamine 5–20 mcg/kg/min** — bradycardia, second-line
- **Dobutamine 2–20 mcg/kg/min** — cardiogenic shock with adequate blood pressure
- **Nitroglycerine 5–200 mcg/min** — SCAPE, hypertensive emergency, ACS

*Vasopressors are given through a central line where possible; a large proximal peripheral vein is acceptable in an emergency, with vigilant monitoring for extravasation.*

## 12.0 Summary of Substantive Changes from Issue 01

This table records where Revision 02 departs from the previous issue on clinical grounds, so that the change is transparent to staff and to auditors. Changes that are purely expansions of detail are not listed.

### Clinical changes and their basis

#### Trauma and neurology

- **Polytrauma priority order.** Issue 01 stated the priority as head → chest → orthopaedic. Revised to the ATLS sequence: catastrophic haemorrhage and airway, then tension pneumothorax and massive haemothorax, then circulation, then head. *(ATLS 10th edition)*
- **Head injury blood pressure target.** Raised from SBP \>100 mmHg for all ages to **SBP ≥110 mmHg** (≥100 for ages 50–69). *(Brain Trauma Foundation, 4th edition)*
- **Tranexamic acid in trauma** added, with the 3-hour window specified. *(CRASH-2, CRASH-3)*
- **Steroids explicitly contraindicated in traumatic brain injury.** *(CRASH trial)*
- **Urethral injury.** The Issue 01 escalation sequence (16 Fr → infant feeding tube → abandon) is withdrawn. **No urethral instrumentation where the classic triad is present**; retrograde urethrogram then suprapubic catheterisation by Urology.
- **Stroke imaging.** Issue 01 directed MRI if onset \<6 hours and CT if \>6 hours. Reversed: **NCCT head first in every suspected stroke, in every time window**, to exclude haemorrhage. Thrombolysis window 4.5 hours; thrombectomy up to 24 hours in selected patients. *(AHA/ASA 2019, with 2023 update)*
- **Status epilepticus.** Levetiracetam loading corrected from 20 mg/kg to **60 mg/kg (maximum 4500 mg)**; full weight-based benzodiazepine dosing emphasised; a timed 5 / 20 / 40-minute structure introduced. *(ESETT; NCS guideline)*

#### Medicine, oncology and toxicology

- **Undifferentiated breathlessness.** The fixed sequence of deriphylline, hydrocortisone, nebulisation and frusemide for every dyspnoeic patient is withdrawn. Management is now differential-driven, with a POCUS-based approach. **Aminophylline is no longer routine.**
- **Oxygen.** Routine oxygen for the normoxaemic patient with chest pain is withdrawn; oxygen is given to a target saturation. *(AVOID, DETO2X-AMI)*
- **Upper GI bleed.** Management added in full (Issue 01 had history and signs only): restrictive transfusion to Hb 7–9 g/dL, vasoactive drugs for variceal bleeding, and **Ceftriaxone prophylaxis in cirrhotic bleeding**, which reduces mortality.
- **Tranexamic acid in GI bleeding** removed as a routine measure. *(HALT-IT)*
- **Vertigo.** Reflex prochlorperazine for all vertigo replaced by the **HINTS plus examination** and the Dix–Hallpike / Epley pathway; central features now trigger the stroke pathway.
- **Hypoglycaemia.** Mandatory 24-hour observation added for sulfonylurea and long-acting insulin.
- **Activated charcoal and gastric lavage** repositioned: charcoal within 1 hour with a protected airway for tablet ingestion, and lavage restricted to a narrow indication. The Issue 01 blanket instruction “do not use activated charcoal” is retained **for organophosphate poisoning specifically**, where it is correct.
- **Pralidoxime.** The internal contradiction in Issue 01 (stating that oximes are not useful while giving dosing) is resolved: the uncertainty in the evidence is stated, and the primacy of atropine and ventilation is emphasised. Bolus corrected to **30–45 mg/kg over 30 minutes** with maintenance **8–12 mg/kg/h**.
- **Febrile neutropenia.** Door-to-antibiotic target harmonised to **60 minutes**, given on suspicion rather than after the neutrophil count; MASCC scoring and line-removal indications added.

#### Structural additions and next steps

**Added in this revision**

- Four-column protocol format with an explicit **RED FLAG** row for every presentation
- **Documentation standard** for the ED case sheet
- **Special alerts** — STEMI, stroke, sepsis, trauma — activated at triage
- Oncology-specific protocols expanded to reflect this hospital’s case-mix: SVC obstruction, malignant cord compression, hypercalcaemia, tumour lysis, immune-related toxicity, neutropenic enterocolitis
- **Aluminium phosphide and snake bite** protocols, given the local epidemiology
- **Sepsis one-hour bundle**; **ACLS/PALS algorithms in text**; **fibrinolytic checklist** expanded to a three-step decision aid
- Emergency drug guide with antidotes, RSI agents and vasoactive infusions

**Section 8 addendum — thirteen presentations absent from Issue 01, added in this revision**

- **8.1 Anaphylaxis**  •  **8.2 Sepsis and septic shock**  •  **8.3 Hyperkalaemia**  •  **8.4 Burns** with Parkland calculation and transfer criteria  •  **8.5 Acute limb ischaemia**  •  **8.6 Hypertensive emergency and urgency**  •  **8.7 The agitated or violent patient**, with a restraint standard  •  **8.8 Acute transfusion reaction**  •  **8.9 Needle-stick and body-fluid exposure**  •  **8.10 Common paediatric emergencies**  •  **8.11 Brought dead, death in the ED and medico-legal documentation**  •  **8.12 Sexual assault — examination and care**  •  **8.13 Mass casualty incident**

*Diabetic ketoacidosis and the hyperosmolar state are covered in full at 4.3 and are not duplicated.*

- **Also added in this revision:** **2.4** Syncope and transient loss of consciousness  •  **4.7** Headache  •  **5.2** Animal bite, scratch and rabies prophylaxis  •  **7.5** Anxiety, panic attack and acute psychological distress — each placed within its existing body-system section
- **13.0 Alphabetical Index** added, keyed to protocol numbers rather than page numbers so it survives reprinting and editing

**Still recommended for a future revision**

- **Acute stroke pathway audit and thrombolysis consent form**  •  **Procedural sedation protocol**  •  **Difficult airway algorithm with a departmental equipment list**  •  **Inter-hospital transfer checklist**  •  **Drowning and hypothermia**  •  **Heat stroke**  •  **Ophthalmic and ENT emergencies**  •  **Do-not-resuscitate and end-of-life documentation**

**Algorithms**

- **Every protocol in Sections 1 to 10 now ends with a one-page flowchart** — navy for the entry point, blue for an action, amber for a decision, red for a critical or time-bound step, green for a safe endpoint, and a grey box for the pitfall or the correction from Issue 01. The charts are the bedside summary; the tables remain the authority.

**Document control.** This revision supersedes Issue 01 dated 01/03/2024. It is to be reviewed annually, or earlier if a relevant guideline changes. Every member of the emergency medical and nursing staff signs the acknowledgement register on issue.

**Feedback.** Errors, omissions and suggested additions should be reported to the Department of Emergency Medicine in writing so that they can be incorporated at the next revision.
